# KB CORE — Jason Koop, Jim Rutberg, Corrine Malcolm — Training Essentials for Ultrarunning (2021)

Companion file: `koop-training-essentials-ultrarunning_biblioteca.md` (full plans, workouts, exercises, recipes, templates). IDs like `TEU-L2-004` point there.

## C01 Overview & Scope

### [TEU-C01-001] Overview & Scope

**What the book is.** *Training Essentials for Ultrarunning* (2nd ed., 2021) by Jason Koop (head coach, CTS-Ultrarunning) with Jim Rutberg and Corrine Malcolm. It is a training, nutrition and race-strategy reference for ultramarathon runners (50K to 200+ miles) and their coaches. It is self-published, with no sponsors or endorsements (declared in Ch 1). Corrine Malcolm wrote the female-athlete section (Ch 2); Nick Tiller PhD and Stephanie Howe PhD reviewed the science. About 400 references.

**Problem it solves.** Ultrarunning has little dedicated research, scattered anecdote and a tendency to treat ultras as long marathons. The book consolidates observation, borrowed endurance-sport science and coaching practice into one system. It teaches the reasoning so athletes build and adjust their own plans.

**Central thesis.**
- An ultramarathon is not a longer marathon. Performance is limited by many stressors: gut, hydration/sodium, thermoregulation, muscle damage, feet, sleep, motivation. Marathon performance is mostly VO2 max × fraction × running cost (Ch 4).
- Every limiting factor is trainable. Failure points that runners blame (nausea, blisters, muscle pain) are largely symptoms of inadequate training.
- Success comes from a systematic, individualized, event-specific structure: five principles, five key workouts, RPE-based intensity, block periodization, a long-range plan (LRP) and a short-range plan (SRP).
- Rest and sleep, adequate energy intake, and practised race strategies matter more than marginal gains.

**Audience.** Novice to elite ultrarunners, time-crunched amateurs, female athletes (including cycle, contraception, RED-S and menopause), aging athletes, and coaches.

**Organization (18 chapters).**
- Ch 1–4: philosophy, energy systems and training principles, performance anatomy, failure points.
- Ch 5–6: four disciplines, tracking.
- Ch 7–8: environment, recovery.
- Ch 9–12: key workouts, LRP, strength, SRP and taper.
- Ch 13–14: fueling and hydration.
- Ch 15–17: mental skills, race strategy, racing frequency.
- Ch 18: coaching guide to 14 major races.

**What it does NOT cover / contain.**
- No stock training plans (a deliberate choice). Only illustrative phases and an example LRP.
- No detailed coverage of deep medical treatment, or of micronutrient prescription (judged unnecessary for healthy people with a varied diet).
- It is not a strength or yoga program; both are treated as optional adjuncts.
- Recommends against full-time low-carbohydrate/ketogenic diets, sleep-deprivation training, salt tablets, NSAIDs, and heart-rate- or running-power-based intensity.

### [TEU-C01-002] Purpose and scope of Training Essentials for Ultrarunning, 2nd edition

- The book is a comprehensive guide for ultramarathon athletes and their coaches. It synthesizes best practices, the art of coaching and emerging science.
- It covers any ultramarathon, from a 50K in the desert to 200 miles in the mountains, and any distance and terrain in between. It serves novices through elites.
- It contains NO stock training plan. There is no page to tear out and follow blindly for twenty weeks. It teaches the reader to solve their personal puzzle of ultramarathon performance: how ultramarathons work, how to prepare, and how to thrive in the process.
- Goal for the reader: enough information and wisdom to create and adjust their own training, nutrition and ultramarathon game plan with confidence. Preparation is described as the athlete's greatest ally and most powerful resource.
- What is new in the 2nd edition: more than 75 percent of the content revised, expanded or newly written. Additions include a section on low-carbohydrate diets, a chapter on the benefits (or lack thereof) of strength training, a chapter on mental skills, and an in-depth section for female athletes written by coach Corrine Malcolm to avoid male-coach bias.
- Scientific scrutiny came from Nick Tiller, PhD, and Stephanie Howe, PhD. Earlier guidance came from biomechanist Rodger Kram, PhD, and Martin Hoffman, MD (former Western States medical director). The book has nearly 400 scientific references.

### [TEU-C01-003] Author background and credentials stated in Ch 1

- Jason Koop: professional coach well into his third decade of coaching; worked with thousands of athletes (marathoners, cyclists, triathletes, ultrarunners, MMA fighters, motorsport and winter sport athletes). Lifelong runner with a background in 5K, 10K and marathon. Began coaching ultrarunners in 2004.
- Runs an office and physiology lab. Leads an ultrarunning coaching department of twelve coaches at the time of publication. The first edition sold tens of thousands of copies and was translated into multiple languages.
- Used Daniels' Running Formula and The Lore of Running as references early in his career.
- Ultrarunners were late to adopt coaching: in the early 2000s elite ultrarunners rejected coaching; from 2004 some sought it, and their success created a coaching industry.

### [TEU-C01-004] Ultrarunning is a different sport; state of its science

Ultrarunning is not a long marathon; it is a different sport. Research is sparse compared with road/track, cross-country skiing, cycling and triathlon, because of difficulty recruiting treadmill subjects for long durations, remote races, and testing exhausted finishers. Research has grown (Figure 4.1, PubMed searches for "ultramarathon", "ultra marathon", "ultra endurance": near-flat until late 20th century, then steep rise to 2020).
Much existing research is pre/post-race questionnaires. These give the runner's interpretation of what happened, not why it happened. Example: nausea is known to be an issue, but its key triggers in ultras are unknown (too much food, too little fluid, wrong carbohydrate type, gut damage with endotoxin leakage, altered vision causing disequilibrium?). No single "aha" discovery prevents nausea in every case.
Author's approach: combine ultra-specific research, research from other endurance disciplines applicable to ultrarunning, and practical coaching experience; explain the science and show how to use it. Be wary of popular-media and social-media interpretations of single studies; sports science evolves.

### [TEU-C01-005] Ch 10 overview: ultramarathon is not a longer marathon; organize non-training picture first

- An ultramarathon cannot be treated as "longer than a marathon". Marathon programs cannot simply be extended, because the stressors and success factors are not just greater versions of marathon ones.
- Ultra preparation includes correct mileage, volume and intensity, PLUS: vertical specificity, terrain specificity, training of the gut, adaptation to environmental stressors (heat, altitude), familiarization with equipment (packs, poles), and other items not routine in marathon training.
- Organizing the season does not start with event demands, a type of interval or a physiological phenomenon. It starts with the non-training picture: choose meaningful events and involve your people, then plan the physical work.
- Chapter purpose: how to build the long-range plan (LRP); the author notes this is the most-discussed chapter (questions on "when to do what workout").

### [TEU-C01-006] Key points: strength training for ultrarunners

- Strength training can acutely enhance performance via running economy and/or injury reduction.
- Not all ultrarunners are candidates.
- It costs time and energy, to be balanced against goals.
- Corrective exercise is a low-cost, low-effort adjunct to mitigate injury risk.
- Other cross-training is fine if enjoyed, but do not expect meaningful ultrarunning improvement.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-training
src: Ch 11 | Key points

### [TEU-C01-007] Ch 12 Short-range plan: scope and why not rigid 3:1

The short-range plan (SRP) is the day-by-day schedule (or a single "block" of training) for the coming days and weeks. It contains precise volume, intensity, intervals, recovery and terrain specificity per day.
The author calls the common 4-week structure (3 weeks on, 1 week off; generic 12-week magazine/online plans) "lazy and uneducated": different stressors affect the body differently, recovery from high volume takes a different time than from high intensity, and adaptation to different intensities follows a different time course. The 3:1 work-rest paradigm is only a reference point; it must be adjusted to the adaptation sought, and individualized plans will most likely break it.
Three concepts guide SRP design: (1) biggest load when most rested; (2) higher intensity = shorter adaptation, longer recovery, lower workout density; (3) lower intensity = longer adaptation, shorter recovery, higher workout density.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:short-range-plan subtopic:3-1-paradigm sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Activating Your Training" | intro

### [TEU-C01-008] Ch 13 scope: fueling and hydrating for ultras

Gastrointestinal distress is the leading reason athletes cite as the main cause of DNFs. Physical ability to complete the distance is not the limiting factor; nutrition and hydration failure undoes good training and planning. The athlete needs to understand the fueling challenges specific to ultra-endurance and nutrition and hydration strategies that work for that individual. Fluids are even more important than calories in ultra-endurance events. The chapter gives training and racing strategies together: train the stomach as well as legs, heart and lungs.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:ultra-fueling-overview
src: Ch 13 intro | start of chapter
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C01-009] Ch 14 scope: adapting sports nutrition to ultrarunning events

Chapter 14 adapts the training-nutrition guidelines to race conditions in ultramarathons. Core claim: most ultrarunners can fuel and hydrate well in training, but nutrition and hydration strategies "frequently go off the rails" during events. GI symptoms (nausea, vomiting) are the top reasons athletes cite for dropping out (Hoffman 2011), and dehydration and upset stomachs knock out even experienced athletes. Chapter themes: ultra-specific challenges, the bull's-eye food strategy, fat-metabolism strategies (and why the author advises against aggressive ones), and the hydration/sodium state matrix (nine combinations).
Colleague's summary of ultra nutrition: "develop the ability to simultaneously run and eat as much as you can without barfing." This is especially true at 100 miles, where caloric expenditure far exceeds stored carbohydrate.
tags: type:philosophy level:all sport:running subtopic:ultra-nutrition-overview condition:general focus:energy-balance
evidence: author-experience; Hoffman 2011
src: Ch 14 intro | no page markers

### [TEU-C01-010] Ch 15 Mental Skills for Ultrarunning: scope and thesis

Chapter thesis: ultrarunning is physically brutal (impact forces several times body weight, muscle damage comparable to serious trauma, GI damage resembling an acute infectious episode, musculoskeletal injuries), yet athletes focus almost only on physical training and neglect mental training (their biggest blind spot). Physiological capacity sets the upper limit of performance; mental skills determine how much of that capacity is used. Both can be trained.
Mental skills are hard to measure and link to performance; research is mechanistic and equivocal between some effect and no effect on performance. The author therefore gives a primer on how psychology interacts with physiology, then a framework (no static program, consistent with the book having no static physical plans) of skills to string together like physical training.

evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 intro | p. n/a

### [TEU-C01-011] Ch 18 purpose: course knowledge and race-specific coaching guide

Course knowledge is treated as a performance asset in ultramarathons: the more an athlete knows about terrain, weather, aid stations, gradients, climb durations and landmarks, the better the plan of attack. Ultrarunning is "an intellectual pursuit as much as it is a physical challenge".
Ways to gain course knowledge: (1) course reconnaissance (often unrealistic for travelers); (2) racing an event once as a recon mission and returning to pursue a performance goal (effective, but at least a two-year process); (3) talking with athletes who have done the event (suffers from N-of-1 bias).
The chapter is a coaching guide to major North American and international ultras built from the library of course-specific knowledge from coaching many athletes over a long period. It supplements race websites and race bibles; it gives insider advice rather than republishing every detail.

## C02 Philosophy & Core Principles

### [TEU-C02-001] Core principles (summary)

**What the author optimizes for**
- Putting the fittest, best-rested, best-fuelled, best-prepared athlete on the start line.
- Consistency of training over long periods (annual volume over any single workout).
- Event-specificity as the race nears.
- Practised, simple race strategies the athlete owns.

**Principles (with reasoning)**
1. **Five training principles**: overload and recovery, progression, individuality, specificity, systematic approach. Neglecting any one derails training (data junkies ignore individuality and specificity; social runners ignore progression and individuality).
2. **First things first.** Volume and rest form the base. Chase the big ~20% gains before the 1–2% marginal gains (altitude, sauna, strength, taper).
3. **Everything limiting is trainable** (gut, feet, heat, descending, mental skills). Training addresses root cause, not symptoms.
4. **Specificity ordering.** Develop least-specific physiology first and most-specific closest to the race. Work weaknesses early, strengths late. Visit SSR, TR and RI at some point in the season. Event demands rank slightly above the other two rules.
5. **Block periodization** over mixed-intensity for experienced athletes, because ultra race intensities fall in a narrow range.
6. **Time at intensity** drives adaptation, so intervals have fixed structures. Tampering risks losing the adaptation. Do hard work hard and easy work easy.
7. **Hardest workout when freshest**; volume of intensity falls through a phase. Higher intensity means shorter phases and longer recovery; lower intensity allows longer phases and denser workouts.
8. **RPE over heart rate, pace or power** for training and racing intensity, because those measures are confounded and RPE is what is available on race day.
9. **Rest and sleep are training**; sleep is the gold standard of recovery, energy balance second. Passive modalities feel good but add little by next day.
10. **Hydration, sodium and thermoregulation outrank fueling** (slower and more dangerous to fix).
11. **Food first, quality and adequacy over restriction.** No demonizing foods or macronutrients; periodize carbohydrate lightly; avoid low energy availability (RED-S).
12. **Simple, tested race plans**: nothing new on race day; process goals under the runner's control; stay in the moment; crew aligned to goals.
13. **Evidence hierarchy**: meta-analyses and RCTs over anecdote; borrow from other sports but adapt what fits.
14. **Emotional engagement and purpose**: choose events with emotional pull; know your WHY.

**What the author rejects**
- Stock plans and "go run more".
- Aerobic Deficiency Syndrome as a real condition.
- One-pace mentality.
- Foundation/preparation/specialization labels.
- Weekly ever-increasing overload (hardest last).
- Pursuing weight loss as performance strategy.
- High-rep low-weight leg circuits, sleep-deprivation training, weighted sleds or tires.
- Salt tablets, NSAIDs, drinking "as much as tolerable" and blind "drink to thirst".
- Long runs without fuel; hard workouts fasted or at the end of long runs.
- Radical race-strategy changes mid-race, and forecasting.

**Non-negotiables**
- Individuality (the athlete's own program).
- Recovery phases scheduled by the checklist.
- Safety cautions in C13 (NSAIDs, hyponatremia, RED-S, altitude illness, heat).

### [TEU-C02-002] Method of learning a new sport: observe first, borrow from related sports

- When coaching an unfamiliar sport (MMA, NASCAR, motocross, Formula One, winter sports, ultrarunning), the author first learns the sport from the ground up, before the x's and o's of coaching.
- He peels apart the physiological and psychological demands through old-school observation (attending practices and races, pacing athletes, speaking with successful athletes, running ultras himself) plus best practices borrowed from similar sports (cycling, triathlon, cross-country skiing, where demands overlap with ultrarunning).
- NASCAR example (transferable lesson): drivers were fitted with heart rate monitors and breathing was observed over race radio. Cardiorespiratory stress was found to be as high as a marathoner's. Car and suit temperatures were similar to a summer day in Death Valley. Interventions: marathoner/triathlete-style training (intervals, threshold runs, long runs) and sauna heat stress protocols, like those for Western States 100 or Badwater 135.
- Result: better cardiovascular fitness and heat tolerance reduced physical strain, so drivers controlled cars and communicated better because fewer physiological resources went to fighting the event demands. This shows that endurance training can improve performance in sports outside traditional endurance sports.
- Limits: observation and educated guessing work to an extent, particularly when it is all you have. In 2004 there were only six research papers on ultrarunning and no ultra training books. Research is now more than 100 papers per year.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:cross-sport-borrowing subtopic:observation sport:general
src: Ch 1 | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-003] Marginal gains: prioritize what matters most, weigh interventions against each other

- Critics (coaches David Roche, Ian Torrence, Ian Sharman, in a 2016 Trail Runner Magazine feature) said the author chases marginal gains (compounding many small improvements) and relies on strategies derived from other sports such as cycling. Others claimed he does not believe in strength training or that ultrarunners are not cross-country skiers. The author calls these criticisms ill-informed and lacking context.
- His consistent position: focus on what matters most for improvement BEFORE chasing marginal gains. In limited, certain circumstances many marginal gains can decide winning vs losing, or making a cutoff or not.
- Interventions such as sauna, strength training or any other marginal intervention can be weighed against one another, choosing which gains to chase and which to forgo.
- Ultrarunning is unique but not so dissimilar from cycling, cross-country skiing, triathlon or marathon that research and best practices cannot be borrowed and adapted. The key is finding the nuance: what applies across disciplines, what must be adapted, and what does not apply at all. Far more information exists on traditional endurance sports than on ultrarunning, and likely always will.
- Ultramarathon training is highly nuanced and rewards coaches and athletes open to new information who accept that there may be multiple paths to a successful outcome.

### [TEU-C02-005] Five fundamental principles of training

Distilled from the world's most successful programs across sports: (1) Overload and recovery, (2) Progression, (3) Individuality, (4) Specificity, (5) Systematic approach. Everyone makes energy the same way but adapts differently; effective training rests on this common set. They must be combined; applying one individually rarely satisfies.
tags: type:principle type:philosophy level:all subtopic:training-principles
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Fundamental Principles of Training"

### [TEU-C02-006] Principle 1: Overload and recovery

- Training leverages the body's response to stress: properly overload a system, allow recovery, and it adapts to be ready for the same or greater stress. Applies to individual sessions and entire training periods. Example: a lactate-threshold interval must be intense and long enough that total stimulus is great enough to prompt adaptation.
- Experienced runners (more than four years; most ultrarunners) get diminishing improvement, so workout architecture matters more than for novices, who improve just by "not screwing it up". Gains require finding ways to raise overload within limited time while scheduling adequate recovery.
- Novices make gains even with haphazard training, but progress stalls when each workout's stimulus is not high or consistent enough. Remedy: focus on one area for a number of weeks (e.g., a lactate threshold block) to concentrate workload. Ultra athletes are already adapted to a high workload, so to progress in one aspect you must focus on it AND reduce focus on other areas in the same period. Example: when working on VO2 max, reduce overall volume to recover between sessions and keep workout quality.
- Recovery (Chapter 8) is not merely the absence of workouts but a crucial component. Days off are not missed opportunities. Adaptations such as plasma volume expansion, mitochondrial biogenesis and red blood cell volume increases occur between workouts. During a run you only apply stress and accumulate fatigue; fitness improves when you rest, sleep, hydrate and eat adequately. You cannot train hard and gain fitness without rest.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:overload subtopic:recovery subtopic:block-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Overload and Recovery"

### [TEU-C02-007] Principle 3: Individuality

- The program that works must be based on your physiological and personal needs, down to individual workouts and interval intensities; total mileage, number/type of intervals, terrain must all be personalized. You can train with others but stay true to your own program.
- Reason there are no full-length plans in the book: ultrarunning is not a longer marathon; generalized plans fail. A plan for Western States 100, Hardrock, Wasatch Front 100 etc. would need to be thirty weeks long minimum, so a prewritten plan will under- or overestimate response and adherence (too hard, running you into the ground, or too easy). A static plan also cannot account for nutrition, equipment, psychology, environment, pacing.
- Ideal: work with a coach; otherwise apply individuality when fitting the book's workouts into your schedule.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:individuality subtopic:no-stock-plans sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Individuality"

### [TEU-C02-008] Principle 4: Specificity

- Training must resemble the activity: broadly, spend the vast majority of training time running; narrowly, determine the exact demands of the event and tailor training to them, especially as key races approach.
- Evidence offered: front-of-pack ultrarunning speeds have risen dramatically in a decade (a decade-old course record might not make top ten now). Author attributes it not to talent, mileage, shoes or energy bars but to training becoming more specific to the events' unique demands; all ability levels can benefit.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:specificity sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Specificity"

### [TEU-C02-009] Principle 5: Systematic approach

- Integrate overload and recovery, progression, individuality and specificity; focusing on one while neglecting others derails training.
- Wasted time: workouts neither hard enough to adapt nor easy enough to recover just add fatigue; scattered plans that jump intensity before anything has developed give "all bang, very little buck".
- Failure types: (a) "data junkies" master overload/recovery and progression but neglect individuality and specificity, following data trends away from needed fitness; (b) "social runners" neglect individuality and progression, substituting socializing, so progress stalls. Balance focused training with some social running; the author prefers runners relax individuality/progression for a while and stay active in the community than drop out or burn out.
- Few people stay fit regardless of what they do; most, even self-styled rebels, thrive with structure. With limited time every hour and interval counts and all workouts are connected through the principles.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:systematic-approach subtopic:data-junkies subtopic:social-runners
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Systematic Approach"

### [TEU-C02-010] Anecdote versus hierarchy of evidence

Anecdotes ("it worked for me") are a poor basis for an ultramarathon plan. Reasons: (1) anecdotes can be found both for and against any intervention (the runner saved by a grilled cheese vs. the runner who vomited it); (2) many anecdotes do not equal data (a Facebook poll showing everyone wears HOKAs does not make them right for you). This applies to low-carbohydrate diets, gel types, altitude tents, etc.

Hierarchy of evidence the author uses (coaches should use it when evaluating claims):
1. Systematic reviews and meta-analyses (top): studies of studies; interventions with reasonable confidence of a positive effect. Examples named: strength training, sauna, block training, altitude training (all discussed in the book).
2. Randomized controlled trials and case-controlled studies: single studies of a specific intervention in a homogeneous group; combine findings with fundamental physiology to create reasonable ways to train athletes.
3. Anecdotes and field reports from athletes and coaches: often the basis for designing RCTs; must not take precedence over RCTs, case-controlled studies or meta-analyses. Anecdote can add context; overriding a meta-analysis with a friend's tale is like throwing a dart blindfolded.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all subtopic:hierarchy-of-evidence subtopic:anecdote
src: Ch 3 "The Problem with Anecdote"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-011] Three characteristics of elite ultrarunners

Elite ultrarunners share three characteristics, present to varying degrees in every runner and all developable:
1. TALENT: high genetic potential for aerobic power and other variables of ultra performance; trainable, so optimizing the talent you have makes you better regardless of starting point.
2. MENTAL SKILLS: they thrive on best days and at times on worst days; anyone can build them with focused training.
3. EMOTIONAL ENGAGEMENT: attachment to races they enter, care about the race and community, not just own performance. Athletes should pick races with a genuine, visceral attraction (excited, giddy, a bit scared).
Their harmonious intertwining enables peak performance. Most runners will be slower than elites; the key is to optimize your own talent, mental skills and emotional engagement.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all subtopic:talent subtopic:mental-skills subtopic:emotional-engagement
src: Ch 3 "Characteristics of Elite Ultrarunners"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-012] Why talent matters: closing the gap to your ceiling

Every athlete has some talent; identify where genetics suit ultrarunning (e.g., sustaining lactate threshold pace for long periods, useful on long climbs; adapting well to altitude; recovering and adapting quickly to sustain heavy workloads) and leverage them. Elites at a start line are similar in talent; those who operate closest to their physiological ceiling (smallest gap between actual performance and maximum potential) have the advantage. The goal of training for everyone is to close the gap between current performance and maximum potential. Nobody, even Olympic-caliber athletes, operates at the maximum; this persistent gap is why all can improve.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:talent subtopic:performance-ceiling
src: Ch 3 "Why Talent Matters"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-013] Mental skills: why they matter

No preparation covers everything a race throws at you; even elites arrive with chinks (missed mileage, insufficient vertical). Mental skills cover vulnerabilities left by training, and elites reduce them better than the pack. They keep elites in contention, or just moving when victory is out of reach. For non-elites mental skills may matter more: longer on course means more exposure to adversity, weather, and stomach problems (fatigue at hour 23 or 27 while elites finished hours earlier). Mental skills are developed through day-to-day training and specific challenges (hard workouts, bad patches in long runs). Covered fully in Chapter 15.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:mental-skills subtopic:adversity
src: Ch 3 "Why Mental Skills Matter"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-014] Emotional engagement: choose events with emotional pull first

Elites show emotional engagement: knowledge of race history and past winners, the course, aid station captains by name, volunteering at races they have won. Example: Rob Krar (2015 Western States winner) ran the final mile with the last finisher in flip-flops.
Method rule: when planning a season, Koop begins with events that have the most emotional pull for the athlete, then builds physical tools around that event, not the reverse (rather than only choosing races matching strengths such as climbing, heat).
Why: at some point in any ultra (exhausted, wet, cold, nauseated) part of the brain says it is not worth it and you will quit; you had better care. Talented, well-trained athletes "checked out" underperform.
Lesson from case: an athlete trying for years to finish Leadville Trail 100 within the 30-hour cutoff (best-day capability 29:45:00, only a razor-thin margin; requires perfect training, weather, execution and deep mental skills) is encouraged to continue rather than switch to an easier race, because her best chance is in the race she is most passionate about.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:emotional-engagement subtopic:race-selection
src: Ch 3 "Emotional Engagement"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-015] Training ties talent, mental skills and emotional engagement together

Training is the catalyst that maximizes all three. Talent: training pushes raw capabilities toward genetic limits; better, more precise design gets closer to the ceiling. Mental skills: training should be difficult because adaptation is Darwinian and requires enough stress; deliberately pushing yourself builds the skills used on race day (athletes should still work hard and be mindful of work accomplished). Emotional engagement: training is elective, so the daily ritual reinforces commitment; hills, intensity and aspects of training should remind you of your chosen event (e.g., posting the race elevation profile on the wall or fridge). The most successful athletes train smarter, not just more or harder.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all subtopic:training-integration subtopic:specificity
src: Ch 3 "Training Ties..."
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-016] Failure points are symptoms; root cause is inadequate training

Author's argument: if you are nauseated, cannot make cutoffs, or have muscle pain that forces a drop, above all you are inadequately trained. Runners identify acute causes of discomfort but rarely link them to inadequate training, so they focus on symptoms, not the root cause. A successful ultra training process addresses all of these; training mitigates or alleviates them (any ultra is still hard). Every stressor (nausea, blisters, dead legs, muscle pain, etc.) can be trained, provided you know what the science says. Ultra stressors differ from marathon stressors; performance is more than VO2max, lactate threshold and cost of running; limiting factors are interrelated (hydration, thermoregulation, muscle damage, gut function).
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:inadequately-trained
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "The Science of Ultrarunning"; Key Points

### [TEU-C02-017] Success by lack of failure; outcome vs process goals

Coach asks athletes "What does success look like to you?" Answers include outcome goals (finish Leadville under thirty hours) and process goals (be able to run at the end of a 100-miler). Process goals teach the coach more; outcome range is simple math, how to achieve it is the harder question.
Most ultrarunners, even elites, succeed through lack of failure: preventing time at 0 mph, preventing becoming a nauseated, sore, blistered mess, continuing to eat, drink and locomote. Exception: elites in 50Ks, 50-milers and flat 100Ks, whose finish comes before failure points take effect.
Goal: arrive at the start line 100% ready, with training prepared for all potential stressors.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:success-by-lack-of-failure
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "Limiting Factors"

### [TEU-C02-018] Four disciplines: train each as a separate sport

Ultramarathons mix level running, uphill running, downhill running and walking (power-hiking). The author treats these as four distinct disciplines, like a swimmer's different strokes, each with its own specificities. They are different enough to be addressed individually in training, "akin to separate sports", although the cardiovascular system links them all.
- Common errors seen: running too much when preparing for a mountainous ultra that needs lots of hiking; hammering track intervals at the wrong phase of training or for the wrong adaptations.
- Consequences: the author prefers uphill interval work, avoids downhill intervals, and recommends matching the average grade and locomotor specificity of training to the course.
- The disciplines differ on three facets: ground reaction forces, how muscles propel or slow the body, and joint segment angles.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:four-disciplines subtopic:specificity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "The Four Disciplines of Ultrarunning", "Biomechanical Differences"

### [TEU-C02-019] Why about 80 percent of prescribed intervals are uphill

Roughly 80 percent of the intervals the author prescribes are uphill, because:
- Uphill running usually elicits a higher cardiovascular response than level running, which is why standardized running VO2max tests raise the grade.
- It is a hedge against injury: it slows you down and so lowers peak GRFs at each foot strike.
- More time is spent climbing than descending in ultrarunning, so a 1 percent improvement uphill is worth more race-day time than 1 percent downhill.
- A little downhill work goes a long way and its effects last long, so focus on uphills (see "How to Combat Muscular Breakdown", Chapter 4).
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all system:vo2max subtopic:uphill-intervals
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "What's So Great About Uphill Intervals?"

### [TEU-C02-020] Tracking philosophy: no magic bullet, synthesize everything

- Trail ultrarunning lacks the tangible workload/work-rate quantification of road running and cycling; surfaces and terrain change between and within runs, and improvement markers are hard to find.
- The author's approach is an amalgamation of tools; no single one gives a definitive answer. Even when better tools emerge, do not expect stoplight (green/red) answers; the picture is a blur of colors subject to interpretation. Information does not do the training for you.
- Very little information is actionable by itself. Action is determined by the aggregate of the information plus athlete feedback, interpreted with knowledge and experience.
- Heart rate is not a good tool for setting intensity; RPE is the main intensity tool. Subjective feedback is the most important daily tool.
- Reason RPE is primary: everything else is irrelevant during competition (e.g., on a 25% grade in a thunderstorm at 10,000 ft, 65 miles into a 100, no heart rate or pace defines lactate threshold). The brain is the only tool that can determine correct interval and racing intensities for an ultrarunner.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:tracking-philosophy
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "What the Coach Uses", "No Magic Bullet", "Why Ultrarunners Should Embrace Perceived Exertion"

### [TEU-C02-021] Fitness is the first weapon against heat and altitude

- Fitness is the primary weapon for performance at altitude and in heat. The fitter the runner, the more effective the natural cooling mechanisms in heat and the more red blood cells available to carry oxygen at altitude.
- No heat or altitude intervention should be done at the expense of building fitness. Total workload volume and workout quality matter far more than specific heat or altitude protocols.
- Examples: if an altitude tent compromises sleep (and so the ability to complete or recover from hard workouts), skip sleeping in it. Hard intervals in the heat of the day may boost heat acclimation but limit the workout's effectiveness, so do not do them.
- The interventions are not mutually exclusive with training: if the right amount of work fits in alongside them, good; if workload or quality must be compromised, it is likely not a good trade-off.
- Many popular interventions (dryer-vent treadmill, extra layers, space heater, tents, masks, camps) are said to rarely work: some have a net-zero effect and several are counterproductive.
- Coach's heat principle: elicit the adaptation with the lowest dose or minimum intervention possible, because anything added to training is stress and requires a trade-off.
tags: type:principle level:all subtopic:fitness-first subtopic:minimum-effective-dose sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Fitness Is Your First Weapon" | Ch 7 intro, "Acclimation Methods"

### [TEU-C02-022] Recovery philosophy: use the time you are not running

- An endurance athlete exercises at most ~35 h in a big week and rarely more than 25 h on an ongoing basis (15 to 20 percent of the 168 h in a week). Most ultrarunners exercise 10 to 15 h per week (6 to 9 percent), some fewer, so they are basically inactive 91 to 94 percent of the time unless their job is active.
- The author rejects the instinct to raise the percentage of time spent exercising (standing desks, extra lunges, calf raises). Time spent running is rarely the limiting factor, and more running is rarely the most effective or time-efficient way to improve.
- The real challenge is how to use the time you are not running to maximize the quality and effectiveness of the time you are.
- Stress and recovery must work in harmony, though not always in perfect balance. Lifestyle, career, relationships and training can tip the balance toward stress, or the athlete can purposely tip it toward recovery.
- "More activities aren't necessarily better for recovery than simply doing less between training sessions. Don't mistake being busy with being effective."
- Non-exercise stress is inescapable. Being better prepared to cope with it creates more capacity to recover from exercise stress, which allows a higher training workload and greater adaptation.
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:recovery-philosophy sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 intro; Key Points | p. n/a

### [TEU-C02-023] Train smarter: structure replaces go run more

- Early ultrarunning had no specific training; as winning times fell, "go run more" stopped being a viable strategy.
- A sport milestone is when training becomes organized and sport-specific: specific activities separated by rest and organized into a schedule.
- Periodization (Bompa and Eastern Bloc coaches, 1950s): systematically changing the focus and workload of training to maximize the impact of overload and recovery on adaptations.
- Interval training (Gerschler, 1930s) refined Swedish fartlek by adding structure: precise times, distances and paces, so work and recovery are quantified.
- Interval training works because it lets you accumulate time at specific intensities; time at intensity is the most critical aspect for positive adaptation.
- Workouts apply the principles of training (overload and recovery, progression, individuality, specificity, systematic approach; Ch 3) to single sessions.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:time-at-intensity subtopic:interval-history
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 intro | p. [no page marker]

### [TEU-C02-024] Three principles of the LRP (in order of priority)

Priority order of principles for building each athlete's LRP:
1. Develop what is MOST specific to the event closest to the event; develop the LEAST specific furthest away.
2. At some point in the season, incorporate each of three critical workouts: SteadyStateRun, TempoRun, RunningIntervals.
3. Work strengths closer to the race and weaknesses further away.
The author only uses a small handful of workouts (the five in Ch 9) among unlimited options (fartleks, ladders, progressive tempos, strength, cross-training, plyometrics, downhill/uphill intervals). Judgment: sometimes time frame or mismatch forces picking among principles, skimping on one, even omitting one; few right/wrong approaches. In general he favors training for event demands (Principle 1) as ever-so-slightly higher priority.
tags: type:principle type:methodology level:all subtopic:long-range-planning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Starting at the End"; "Application of Principle 3"

### [TEU-C02-025] Seiler's hierarchy of training priorities and 'first things first' (Fig 10.5)

Pyramid base to apex (Seiler and Sjursen 2004; book prints "Sjusren"):
1. Total frequency/volume of training (VOL) – well-established
2. High intensity training (HIT) – well-established
3. Training intensity distribution (TID) – well-established (VOL, HIT and TID likely have interactive effects)
4. General periodization details (annual) – unclear, but likely overrated
5. Sports-specific and micro-periodization schemes – not established, but likely modest
6. Training stimuli enhancement (altitude, heat, energy availability) – potentially important effects, but individual and condition specific
7. Race/pace training – potentially decisive if everything else is done right
8. Taper – potentially decisive if you have one isolated competition... and everything else is done right
Premise: accumulate lots of training time, much at intensity below and around lactate threshold, before specialized techniques. Author: "first things first": go after the big 20-percent gains from hours of fundamental endurance training before squeezing 1–2 percent from marginal gains; don't add altitude until the aerobic engine is developed.
tags: type:principle type:evidence level:all subtopic:training-hierarchy
evidence: guideline/theory (Seiler and Sjursen 2004)
src: Ch 10 Fig 10.5

### [TEU-C02-026] Koop's hierarchy of ultramarathon training needs (Fig 10.6)

Base to peak (11 tiers): Total volume of workload → Adequate rest → Adequate and periodized nutrition → Developed mental skills → Building a team → High-intensity training → Long- and short-range planning → Training the gut → Terrain-specific training → Training stimuli enhancement (heat, altitude, energy, strength/cross training) → Taper.
Rationale: athletes are not machines; physiology is measurable, performance is not (no equation for mental toughness or for adapting when Plans A, B, C fail); coaching is art and science. Focus on things that matter more (basics matter more than 8 vs 10 sauna days). Include "forgotten" items (team building, gut training) in LRP notes. "You have prepared more for the things that matter more." Breakthroughs rarely come from physical training alone; arrive with a full toolbox of mental, physical, emotional skills.
tags: type:principle type:philosophy level:all subtopic:training-hierarchy
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Fig 10.6, "Last Word"

### [TEU-C02-027] Strength training: purposeful, complementary to running, individualized

- The author admits the first edition under-treated strength and cross-training; "strength training" is too broad a term (Olympic lifts to glute activation), so the goal, exercises and integration must be decided per athlete.
- Strength training should complement run training, never detract from it. Priority is appropriate run volume balanced with recovery.
- Time and energy are limited; adding strength usually means removing another quality session (e.g., the second interval session). Ask: "What do I prioritize, and what do I sacrifice?" using the LRP and each block's goals.
- Gains of a few percent can be had by adding miles, adding recovery, or lifting, but not necessarily all three; they are at times mutually exclusive. Two hours of strength per week is two hours that could be rest; if burning the candle at both ends, resting may be better.
- No binary yes/no answer; individuality is key.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-training subtopic:prioritization
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 "Strength Training for Ultrarunning" | intro, "Strength Training and Injury Reduction", "Are you a good candidate"

### [TEU-C02-028] Ch 14 final word and key points

"Sports nutrition is dynamic. There is no singular formula that will produce optimal results for all athletes in all conditions." Athletes must manipulate carbohydrate, fluid and sodium based on intensity, duration and environment. Sports nutrition habits are trainable: first find food/drink combinations that work, then train over time to increase the amounts you can consume without upsetting the stomach.
Key points: adapt strategies to event duration, conditions, aid-station access; develop a variety of reliable foodstuffs; enhanced fat metabolism is a natural byproduct of endurance training but costs more oxygen per energy liberated; can be done by chronic diet or acute training interventions, each with pros/cons; if chosen, use low-risk approach (some EnduranceRuns with low carbohydrate availability); avoid extreme strategies (restricting carbohydrate on hard workouts and longer runs); hydration is hard to get right and to diagnose; practice nutrition in training multiple times under different conditions and intensities.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all sport:running focus:behavior subtopic:practice-nutrition condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Final Word", "Key Points" | no page markers

### [TEU-C02-029] Mental skills limit or enhance performance; measurement problem

- Mental skills are difficult to measure directly, and the causal link to physical performance is less concrete than for physiology (VO2, force, lactate). This encourages the thinking "if you can't measure it, it's not worth doing"; coaches and athletes stick to what they can measure.
- Much research is mechanistic (e.g., fMRI of meditation brain activation); the link to performance is an "extension of the mechanism".
- Research is equivocal between some effect and no effect on performance.
- Author's position: mental skills either enhance or limit performance; train them.
- Clichés ("90 percent mental") are not accompanied by actual training.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:mental-skills
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 intro | p. n/a

### [TEU-C02-030] Race strategy is the last piece of training; start by reviewing training (Step 0)

Personal race strategies are the final piece of the training process and bring training to life on race day. Goals, nutrition strategy, and choice of crew/pacers are completed in the weeks before the event (often during taper, as an outlet for pent-up energy).
Step 0: review the last several months of training (paper log, spreadsheet, Strava, TrainingPeaks, Movescount, Garmin Connect). Find strengths and chinks in the armor; look at paces, grades, food and fluids used.
- Effectiveness of training sets a realistic framework for goals.
- Nutrition used in training is the platform for race-day nutrition.
- Paces and workouts accomplished guide how to meter race-day effort.
- Training shows the boundaries of what you are capable of and need to do.
tags: type:principle level:all sport:running subtopic:race-strategy subtopic:training-review
src: Ch 16 "Creating Your Personal Race Strategies", "Step 0" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-031] Race-day goals belong to the athlete; outcome vs process goals

Question asked to athletes: "What does success look like to you?" Goals ultimately belong to the athlete, not partner, children, boss or training buddy; the support network matters but goals are the runner's own.
Race-day goals are concrete, actionable, task-focused (not necessarily times/paces/placings; "Run with a smile" is as valid as "eat 200 calories an hour"). They must describe two things:
- OUTCOME GOAL: what you ultimately want to achieve.
- PROCESS GOALS: what you do during the race to support the outcome goal.
Example 1: outcome = finish the American River 50 in under eight hours. Process: eat a smart 600-calorie breakfast; begin the race running 8:00 to 8:30 min/mi; eat early and often, around 250 calories per hour; stay positive; tell the crew "thanks."
Example 2: outcome = finish the Vermont 100 having enjoyed the day. Process: keep pace easy and manageable; tell crew to keep you smiling; thank aid station volunteers; encourage other runners; focus on gratitude that you get to run.
tags: type:philosophy type:principle level:all sport:running subtopic:outcome-goals subtopic:process-goals
src: Ch 16 "What Is Your Race-Day Goal?" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-032] Process goals: three criteria and personalization

Process goals are the actions, thoughts, checkpoints and supporting activities during the race; they should dominate thinking during the race (e.g. at mile 30 of a 50-miler, "eat another gel to hit 200 calories an hour" helps more than "finish in nine hours"). Athletes with similar outcome goals often have very different process goals. Illustration: MMA fighters answer "How do you see the fight ending?" with how (knockout/armbar), not just "I win".
Criteria:
1. Must directly support the outcome goal. Pace, effort, attitude, nutrition planning, gear selection are valid if they improve the likelihood of the outcome. Process goals that do not affect the outcome are frivolous and drain energy. Litmus test: run them by the crew; a good one is easily reinforced at an aid station and has impact down the road.
2. Must be under the runner's control (calories, pace, attitude). Not valid: running with another runner, avoiding weather, staying in top twenty, beating a fellow runner (not in complete control).
3. Must suit the individual's needs, psychology and personality. Include at least one technical (pace, calories/hour, quantifiable) and one psychological (e.g. stay positive) goal. Stat-driven runners get more technical goals; psychologically driven runners get more cue-based goals. Coaching feedback differs accordingly ("You ran that section right on your target time!" vs "Stay positive! This next section is your strength...").
Crew reinforcement: list process goals on a cue card for the crew; they ask e.g. "How many calories have you taken in? It's three hours in, so you should have consumed 600 calories by now" (for 200 cal/hr).
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all sport:running subtopic:process-goals subtopic:crew
src: Ch 16 "Process Goals" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C02-033] No race is required: race by values, not by need

- Athletes often ask how many races they need before a goal event. The author finds the question puzzling: no one "needs" a race, and there is no magical formula ("complete x and y races and you will be prepared").
- Doing a race because you feel you "need" it, or simply because "it's there" (Mallory), is a poor way to choose events. Athletes who do this chase races endlessly, race too much and race without true purpose.
- Ultramarathon events keep growing in number, so races are easy to find nearly every weekend. This makes the "because it's there" trap easy to fall into.
- Racing and what it represents are rooted in personal values. Those values should determine what and when you choose (not need) to race. The value system is entirely individual: the same series of races can work for one athlete and be counterproductive for another, even with the same goal event.
- The author's basis: a conference call with his athletes. Some race nearly every weekend, others once a year. Optimum quantity and type of races varied wildly, as did their views on why and when to race. What emerged was the different set of values each athlete placed on racing, which set how many and what types of races each did.
- Statistic cited (Fig 17.1, adapted from Andersen 2020): a greater percentage of ultrarunners race more than one race per year than marathon, half marathon, 10K and 5K runners. This is notable because the effort and recovery required for an ultra are far greater.
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:racing-values subtopic:race-selection
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 "Racing Wisely" intro | p. [not marked]

### [TEU-C02-034] Four racing values (what racing represents)

Determine your racing values first; distance, timing and frequency of races follow from them. These four are examples, not an exhaustive list, and values can ebb and flow over time.
1. **Racing as the End of Means**: racing is the end point of training. The author views training as continuous, but some athletes bookend the process with chosen races. Racing validates the work and sacrifice (miles, intervals, time lost with family/friends or other pursuits). These athletes likely do not need to race often and place greater value on particular important races than on racing for its own sake.
2. **Racing as a Competitive Outlet**: competitive psychology drives the wish to race, against others or oneself, to see how fitness and toughness stack up. When satisfying competitive drive, it may make sense to include more high-quality racing in the annual plan.
3. **Racing as Part of the Training Process**: training cannot replicate aid stations, drop bags, foreign terrain and event-day logistics, and races can bridge those gaps. A race also gives a larger training stimulus, since its duration and intensity usually exceed what is possible in day-to-day training; a huge training day with aid station support instead of self-managed support; and an arena to work on what the goal event will demand. Some athletes race three out of four weeks a month for many months. Crucial: decide each race's individual emphasis, because there is only so much physical and emotional bandwidth for racing, and assigning emphasis manages that bandwidth.
4. **Racing to Be Part of a Community**: racing lets people touch base with a community they identify with ("the same group of idiots at every race"), and many become aid station volunteers or spectators. If community draws you but you struggle to balance purposeful training with preparation for and recovery from frequent races, volunteering at events keeps you engaged while staying true to the long-range plan.
Key question when setting up a season: what do the races represent to you (competition, stepping-stone, end point of training, something else)?
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:racing-values subtopic:race-selection subtopic:community
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 "Racing as the End of Means" … "Find Your Racing Values"

## C03 Integral Model

### [TEU-C03-001] Integral Model

**1. Chain: beliefs → mechanisms → methodology → decisions**

- *Belief:* ultrarunning success is the sum of coping with many stressors, i.e. "success by lack of failure" (see "Failure points are symptoms; root cause is inadequate training"; "Marathon performance model versus ultramarathon determinants").
- *Mechanisms:*
  - All three energy systems run at once ("Three energy systems: overview and no on/off switch"; "The Endurance String Theory").
  - Adaptation happens during recovery (Principle 1: "Overload and recovery").
  - Intensities adapt on different time courses: high intensity is fast and plateaus early; low intensity is slow ("Time course of adaptation and diminishing returns").
  - The gut, skin, muscle and thermoregulatory systems are trainable organs.
  - Perceived effort, not just physiology, limits pacing ("Psychobiological model of fatigue (PBM)"; "Perceived exertion end point interaction").
- *Methodology:*
  - Five key workouts with fixed RPE and structure ("Table 9.1 The five critical workouts").
  - Block periodization by an LRP of roughly 8-week, two-4-week phases (see "Training phase design: lengths and construction steps").
  - An SRP that front-loads hard work.
  - Specific overlays: terrain matching ("Training for the four disciplines"), heat and altitude, nutrition, strength, mental skills.
- *Decisions:* what to do this phase, this week, when to rest, what to eat or drink, how to pace and how to rescue a bad patch.

**2. Variables and priority order**

1. Total volume of workload over long periods (by time, not miles), with adequate rest ("Koop's hierarchy of ultramarathon training needs").
2. Sleep and energy balance.
3. Nutrition adequacy.
4. Mental skills, support team.
5. Intensity distribution: high intensity ≈ 20% of sessions, ≈ 10% of hours.
6. LRP/SRP structure.
7. Gut training.
8. Terrain specificity.
9. Enhancements (heat, altitude, strength).
10. Taper.

Manipulable workout components: intensity, volume (time-at-intensity), frequency/repetition, environment (grade, surface), stride rate.

**3. How the tools fit**

- *Measurement:* RPE with the Talk Test and breathing shift sets intensity. GPS/NGP, rTSS, ATL/CTL and Strava segments retrospectively compare workouts. Subjective log, HRV and wearables only nudge decisions. Lab VO2 max/LT is a benchmark only, but shows ultrarunners' LT sits at 95–97% of VO2 max, which supports adding RunningIntervals.
- *Planning:*
  - LRP from the goal event backward; last phase = the most race-specific intensity or a strength, two 4-week blocks.
  - First phase = least important or weakness.
  - Fill the middle with the leftover intensity.
  - Shorten high-intensity or low-importance blocks, lengthen low-intensity or important ones.
  - Adjust when the athlete fails to improve after a recovery phase.
- *SRP:* hardest workouts first; back-to-back hard days to concentrate load (test at SSR first); recovery on the three-point checklist.
- *Overlays:* elevation change/mile matched to the race in the final weeks; heat acclimation (7–10 days passive sauna, with maintenance and re-acclimation); altitude (live high/train low, ferritin check); strength only if candidate (flowchart) and phased early/mid/late; fueling practised at race rates.
- *Race:* effort-based strategy built from training time-at-intensity; goals outcome plus process; nutrition via bull's-eye foods and hourly targets; hydration-sodium state awareness; ADAPT for problems.

**4. Decision flow for a new athlete**

1. Ask "What does success look like to you?" and pick the event with emotional pull ("Emotional engagement: choose events…"; "Choosing your event…").
2. Check time: does the athlete meet the minimum maximum ("The time-crunched ultramarathoner and the minimum maximum")?
3. Screen health/energy: RED-S signs, iron/ferritin if altitude, injury history; for women record cycle and contraception.
4. Build the support team and set outcome/process goals ("Outcome goals…"; "Process goals…").
5. Analyze event demands: elevation change/mile, heat, altitude, surface, aid spacing, sleep.
6. Identify strengths and weaknesses; draft the LRP backward with the principles.
7. Set phase lengths; place recovery weeks; add heat/altitude and strength only if the hierarchy allows.
8. Write the SRP for each phase: hardest first; use the checklist for recovery.
9. Train nutrition (bull's-eye foods, nutrition report card, sweat test), gut and mental skills in sequence.
10. Monitor via RPE, NGP and log; adjust.
11. Taper over 2–3 weeks (volume down fast, intensity type and frequency kept).
12. Race with the effort-based plan, simple nutrition strategy, crew briefed on goals; use ADAPT for problems.

**5. Tensions and trade-offs the author acknowledges**

- Strength, heat, altitude and extra sessions cost recovery. Always ask "what do I prioritize and sacrifice?"
- Block versus mixed periodization: research favors blocks in other sports, but extrapolation to ultras is debated.
- Hard training requires getting worse before better, but too much goes into overreaching.
- Back-to-back hard days add load but are tricky; require careful monitoring.
- Heat acclimation versus taper stress. Protocol 1 needs a bigger taper; protocol 2 may collide with heavy training.
- Fat adaptation offers fuel but costs ~8% more oxygen per unit energy and intensity quality; the author accepts only low-risk tools.
- Pace/heart-rate tools have narrow valid uses; RPE is preferred but is subjective.
- Slight dehydration or hypernatremia are preferred errors over overhydration/hyponatremia.
- Women's data are limited; cycle tracking is individual; oral contraceptives trade a small performance cost against clinical benefits.
- Goals: challenge must balance with risk affinity; a DNF is acceptable when it follows from clearly chosen aggressive goals.
- Author admits N-of-1 bias, evolving science and that no tool gives stoplight answers.

tags: type:methodology type:philosophy level:all subtopic:integral-model sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 1–18 (synthesis)

## C04 Mechanisms

### [TEU-C04-001] Three energy systems: overview and no on/off switch

The body has three primary energy systems that produce ATP (the energy currency): (1) immediate system of stored ATP and creatine phosphate (ATP-PCr), (2) glycolytic (anaerobic, lactic acid) system, (3) aerobic system. Energy is released when one of ATP's three phosphate bonds is broken; the resulting ADP is resynthesized to ATP. All three always work together synergistically in varying amounts; there is no on/off switch, and the contribution of each at any moment is based on demand. Understanding them is helpful for training them.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:energy-systems system:aerobic system:anaerobic sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Physiology of a Better Engine" | no page markers

### [TEU-C04-002] Immediate energy system: ATP-PCr

- Supports high-power efforts lasting less than eight to ten seconds (escaping a bus; sprinting, MMA, powerlifting). In endurance running used for explosive movements such as jumping a creek or bounding up boulders.
- Yields immediate energy because ATP is stored directly in the muscle and needs none of the multiple steps of the anaerobic/aerobic systems. Supply is very limited.
- Demand in those seconds exceeds what glycolytic or aerobic systems can deliver.
- Endurance athletes do not rely heavily on it; it is typically adequately developed through normal training.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:atp-pcr system:neuromuscular sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Immediate Energy System: ATP-PCr"

### [TEU-C04-003] Lactic acid (glycolytic) system and lactate

- The "high-speed express lane" for delivering energy quickly under heightened demand; commonly called "anaerobic" (without oxygen), which is confusing because the body has not stopped using oxygen. When demand outstrips the aerobic system's ATP production, the mitochondria's aerobic functions are supported by energy from anaerobic processes.
- Aerobic metabolism delivers all the energy in a glucose molecule; glycolysis converts glucose to lactate, delivering some of the energy, but more quickly. It can only use carbohydrate, produces less ATP per molecule, and produces metabolites that lower body pH and interfere with muscle contractions.
- Lactate is a normal by-product of glucose breakdown to ATP. 75 percent of it enters the bloodstream and is shuttled to mitochondria as an energy substrate (Brooks 1986); the remaining 25 percent goes to the liver to be turned into glucose by gluconeogenesis.
- Lactate has been wrongly blamed for muscle burning and delayed-onset muscle soreness; massaging, flushing and buffering it have largely failed. Increases in lactate are not the cause of fatigue, they merely coincide with it. Best use: reintegrate it into aerobic metabolism so it is broken down into energy, water and carbon dioxide.
- Under normal conditions (aerobic supply meets demand) clearance is faster than accumulation. When production outstrips the ability to buffer, lactate accumulates: the "lactate threshold", a shift from predominantly aerobic energy to a greater anaerobic contribution. It is the intensity an athlete can sustain for an extended period; raising the pace/intensity at which it occurs improves performance and is a primary outcome of endurance training. It also speeds recovery from hard efforts.
- Credit analogy: glycolysis is buying energy on credit; you must pay it back and cut spending (slow down) while repaying. A stronger aerobic system puts more cash in your pocket so you use less credit.
- Key adaptation: better integration of lactate into aerobic production so it is fully oxidized. The faster you process lactate, the more work before fatigue-inducing anaerobic metabolites (not lactate) accumulate in muscle and blood.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:lactate subtopic:lactate-threshold system:lactate system:threshold sport:running
evidence: study (Brooks 1986)
src: Ch 2 "The Lactic Acid System"

### [TEU-C04-004] The aerobic system

- Produces the majority of energy for daily life and exercise; burns carbohydrate, fat and protein simultaneously and regulates the mix by fuel availability and demand. Protein is not a significant source unless energy reserves are very low.
- A "flex-fuel engine", clean and efficient: waste products from carbohydrate are only water and carbon dioxide.
- For endurance athletes, increasing the oxygen the body can transport and utilize is a primary training goal, because oxygen is the limiting factor in how fast you can produce energy aerobically.
- Transport: hemoglobin largely dictates how much oxygen goes from lungs to working muscles. Utilization: mitochondria (the muscle cell's power plants) use oxygen and fuel (carbohydrate and fat) to make usable energy. Training improves both transport and utilization.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:aerobic-system system:aerobic system:fat-oxidation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Aerobic System"

### [TEU-C04-005] VO2 max and why it matters to ultrarunners

- VO2 max (maximum aerobic capacity) is reached when the body delivers and utilizes as much oxygen as possible and intensity is at its absolute peak. Measured in liters of oxygen per minute; mL/kg/min is used to compare athletes because it accounts for body weight.
- It is one indicator (not the only one) of endurance potential. A very high VO2 max does not guarantee a champion: it means a big engine. You need a big engine to be elite, but whatever engine you start with you can optimize with effective training.
- VO2 max workouts generate enormous lactate and burn energy very fast, but raising pace at VO2 max (pace run while using maximal oxygen) and absolute VO2 max (L/min) give the physiological base to run faster at any distance.
- Ultrarunners often have a "one-pace mentality", thinking endurance plus some lactate threshold work suffices because the goal is 15 to 30 hours of movement. Volume is important, but intensities at and near VO2 max are also critical for building the engine.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle level:all subtopic:vo2max system:vo2max sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "VO2 Max"

### [TEU-C04-006] The Endurance String Theory

- Knowing each system helps design specific training, but has wrongly led people to think the systems operate independently. Training in a given "zone" targets one system but it is not the only one working or benefiting.
- Energy is always produced through all pathways; demand sets relative contribution. At low to moderate intensity (e.g., 40 to 50 percent of VO2 max, an easy endurance run) most energy is aerobic (mitochondria burning primarily fat and some carbohydrate). As intensity rises, more energy is anaerobic; glycolysis ramps up near and above lactate threshold, which can be anywhere from 65 to 85 percent of VO2 max for a given athlete. Because glycolysis uses only glucose, the carbohydrate share rises dramatically from lactate threshold to VO2 max; fat is still used but at a lower rate since fat breakdown is slower.
- Model: the pathways are segments of one continuous string arranged by relative energy contribution as the duration of an all-out task increases: ATP-PCr for ~8 to 10 seconds; lactic acid system provides a lot of energy at full gas for efforts lasting less than about two minutes; then a large aerobic segment that could theoretically power moderate intensity forever given oxygen and fuel.
- Improving fitness in one system lifts the string: all other areas rise too, to varying extents. Targeting VO2 max lifts lactate-threshold fitness and aerobic endurance more than aerobic-intensity training lifts lactate threshold or VO2 max. All systems are interconnected; even narrow training has upstream and downstream effects.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle level:all subtopic:endurance-string-theory system:aerobic system:threshold system:vo2max
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Endurance String Theory"

### [TEU-C04-007] RED-S (Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport) and energy availability

- Formerly the Female Athlete Triad (menstrual dysfunction, low energy availability, decreased bone density); the IOC and others moved to RED-S, covering the relationship among energy availability, reproductive function and bone health, and acknowledging males can also suffer low energy availability.
- Body's priority is survival: when energy is inadequate for exercise cost it diverts energy from metabolism and reproduction. Over time low energy availability lowers performance and affects bone and musculoskeletal health, cardiovascular disease, and neuroendocrine abnormalities such as thyroid dysfunction (Clark 2018; Mountjoy 2018).
- Energy expenditure is dynamic: you cannot balance the calorie checkbook per calendar day. Rest days after a long weekend run may need overcompensation; "limiting calories" because you are not training that day ignores that you may still be behind from a day whose expenditure was too large to make up in 24 hours.
- Figure 2.6 (Keay 2018): energy intake must cover energy for life processes plus training demand. Insufficient intake or excessive training load reduces energy left for life processes (below the "energy requirement for life processes" line); matched intake and demand keeps life processes sufficient.
tags: type:mechanism type:caution level:all subtopic:red-s subtopic:energy-availability focus:energy-balance condition:general
evidence: guideline (IOC; Mountjoy 2018; Keay 2018)
src: Ch 2 "RED-S", Figure 2.6

### [TEU-C04-008] Why training strategies differ widely among ultrarunners

- Ultrarunning performance is hard to define; post-race reports vary (legs shot, heat, stomach, climbs vs descents), leading to a barrage of training strategies. Volume, intensity distribution, strength training and coaching philosophies vary wildly, even among elites.
- Elite example (2018 Ultrarunners of the Year, profiled by Koop 2019, reconstructed from interviews, Strava and social media, as Koop did not coach them): Courtney Dauwalter 719 hours annual training vs Jim Walmsley 745 hours (similar); both used specificity, tailoring vertical gain and terrain to the approaching race. Vertical gain differed: Walmsley 926,906 ft vs Dauwalter 453,000 ft (nearly double). Some athletes run >30 h/week; others do 3-4 hard workouts per week; some swear by strength training, others do none.
- Volume convention: the book expresses training volume in hours per week, not miles, because time is more consistent than distance on varied terrain (uphill, downhill, technical).
- Causes of discrepancy: (1) ultrarunning history: methods rooted in anecdote, passed on like a game of telephone (e.g., the Ultra-list message board of the 1990s-2000s) and distorted over time; (2) individual adaptation (principle of individuality, Ch 2): people in the same program (e.g., collegiate cross-country) adapt differently to speedwork, high volume, etc. Mechanisms studied: muscle fiber type distribution (Bellinger et al. 2020; Leivens et al. 2020), genetics (Bouchard 2012; Sarzynski, Ghosh, Bouchard 2017), psychological response to training (Wahl et al. 2013).
- Author's caution: tailoring by one narrow trait (fast-twitch vs slow-twitch, tough vs not-so-tough) misses the mark; individual variability is the constellation of many variances, not any single one.
tags: type:principle type:mechanism level:all subtopic:individuality subtopic:training-variability sport:running metric:volume
src: Ch 3 intro
evidence: author-experience; Bellinger 2020; Leivens 2020; Bouchard 2012; Sarzynski 2017; Wahl 2013

### [TEU-C04-009] Marathon performance model versus ultramarathon determinants

Marathon performance is limited by three factors: (1) VO2 max (maximum amount of oxygen an athlete can utilize in one minute of maximal exercise); (2) the fraction of that VO2 max an athlete can sustain over the course of the marathon; (3) the oxygen cost of running (how much oxygen it takes to go a given distance). Manipulating any or all of these three variables can influence marathon performance.
Joyner (1991) predicted a marathon performance upper limit of 1:57:58 if all three variables were optimized. In 2017 (26 years later), the Nike Performance Laboratory (led by Brad Wilkins, PhD) tested the hypothesis: they selected athletes with high VO2 max, high fractional utilization, and extremely low cost of running, and optimized environmental and tactical factors (optimal temperature, delivered nutrition by bicycle, flat sea-level course with few turns, rotating pacers to reduce aerodynamic drag, Tesla lead car with video board, Vaporfly shoes) to attempt to break the two-hour threshold.

Author's position: Ultrarunning is not marathon running; the marathon is likely the longest race where pure physiology is the main determinant of performance. In ultrarunning, physiology is still important, but the body must overcome markedly more obstacles that have greater potential for cataclysmic failure. There is no simple three-variable formula that describes the upper limits of ultramarathon performance.

Figure 3.3 (proposed determinants of ultramarathon performance, adapted from Millet 2012):
- Three direct contributors to Ultramarathon Performance: Maximal Sustainable Power, Running & Walking Energy Cost, and Psychological & Motivational Factors.
- Sub-pathways:
  - GI Disorders → Low-Intensity Endurance (solid arrow down)
  - Muscle & Osteoarticular Damage → Low-Intensity Endurance (solid arrow up)
  - Low-Intensity Endurance → Maximal Sustainable Power (solid arrow down)
  - VO2 max → Maximal Sustainable Power (solid arrow right)
  - Equipment → Running Patterns (solid arrow left)
  - Neuromuscular Activation → Running Patterns (solid arrow up)
  - Equipment → Psychological & Motivational Factors (solid line with arrow)
- Indirect / Secondary pathways (dashed arrows):
  - Equipment → GI Disorders
  - Equipment → Running & Walking Energy Cost
  - Running Patterns → Muscle & Osteoarticular Damage; Running Patterns → Running & Walking Energy Cost
  - Neuromuscular Activation → Muscle & Osteoarticular Damage; Neuromuscular Activation → Running & Walking Energy Cost
  - Flexibility → Muscle & Osteoarticular Damage; Flexibility → Running & Walking Energy Cost
  - Muscle Mass → Muscle & Osteoarticular Damage; Muscle Mass → Running & Walking Energy Cost
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:performance-determinants subtopic:marathon-model sport:running system:aerobic
src: Ch 3 "Cracking the Code of Ultramarathon Performance" | Fig 3.3
evidence: Joyner 1991; Millet and Millet 2012, 2020; Garbisu-Hualde and Santos-Concejero 2020; Knechtle and Nikolaidis 2018; Millet et al. 2011; Thompson 2017; Knechtle and Senn 2011

### [TEU-C04-010] Talent: genetics, VO2 max and the physiological ceiling

- Author's meaning of talent: genetic predisposition for athletic performance (author's background in biochemistry and genetics). Genetics sets parameters and limits of potential, even if training, nutrition and recovery are optimal.
- For endurance athletes, physiological talent is largely (not exclusively) measured by VO2 max: maximum volume of oxygen an athlete can consume, transport and use, in ml/kg/min. Oxygen releases energy from carbohydrate and fat (protein in dire circumstances); more oxygen use = faster energy delivery = greater work capacity.
- "In the club": an athlete needs to be near a threshold VO2 max for the elite level. Elite male long-distance runner average about 77.4 ml/kg/min (Nevill et al. 2003); not all elites reach it and one can be elite with less, but must be close; a 40 ml/kg/min tester has no realistic chance.
- Figure 3.4 (Nevill 2003): VO2 max of elite athletes by sport (badminton, heavyweight rowers, lightweight rowers, long-distance runners, middle-distance runners, squash, triathletes), y-axis 45-80 ml/kg/min; males higher than females in all; long-distance runners highest, badminton lowest; exact values not labeled in source.
- Ultra elites need not match marathoners but must exceed a rate. Koop's testing of elite ultrarunners: successful males about 60-70 ml/kg/min; successful females about 55-65 ml/kg/min (needed to win races like Western States 100, Leadville Trail 100, Lake Sonoma 50).
- VO2 max is not the only variable: ultra contenders need a more diverse set of talents: skill over varied terrain, balance, reflexes, foot placement, coping with environmental stress, eating and drinking large quantities during intense competition without getting sick. Elites are talented across many physiological and psychological variables.
- Trainability: novice VO2 max can improve well over 20 percent; elite with very high VO2 max can still improve about 5 percent (but other areas matter more for them). Also trainable: eating more, technical terrain, heat and cold handling. All athletes maximize innate talent; elites have a higher ceiling.
tags: type:mechanism type:threshold level:elite level:all subtopic:vo2max subtopic:talent system:vo2max sport:running metric:pace
src: Ch 3 "Talent" | Fig 3.4
evidence: Nevill et al. 2003; author-experience (testing)

### [TEU-C04-011] Race stressors: why cardiovascular fitness is not the limiter (Olson example)

Figure 4.2 Ultramarathon race stressors: sleep deprivation; cardiovascular stress; GI stress; mechanical stress on the joints; muscular stress; blisters; thermoregulation and blood volume; environmental stress (temperature, altitude, cold); energy depletion; toughness; neuromuscular fatigue. Not all equally limiting, all play a part.
