Biomechanical fact: preferred human walking speed is around a nineteen-minute mile (Browning and Kram 2005; Levine and Norenzayan 1999; Mohler et al. 2007); eighteen-minute miles are easily achievable and give a thirty-hour 100-mile finish. All reasonably healthy individuals can beat any ultra's cutoffs by locomotion speed.
Example lesson: Timothy Olson ran Western States in 14:46:44 (about 8:50/mile); lactate threshold pace under 6:00/mile in lab. On Cal Street (mile 62-78, net downhill) he ran about 9:00/mile, an easy recovery-run pace for cardiovascular fitness, yet could not go faster. Success depends on coping with the sum of all stressors, not cardiovascular capacity alone.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:race-stressors
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "Limiting Factors"

### [TEU-C04-012] Gut: blood-flow competition and why nausea occurs

Digestion: mechanical (chewing, saliva enzymes, stomach churning) then chemical (gastric juices, acids, enzymes), then intestinal absorption into the bloodstream. Stomach and intestines are muscular and need blood flow. Blood volume is a fixed resource shared by working muscle (including gut), and skin for heat dissipation. The body prioritizes cooling over oxygen delivery to muscles; gut blood flow falls, movement of food slows or stops, GI distress follows. Training increases heat tolerance and reduces blood flow needed by skeletal muscle at the same pace, freeing blood for the gut, so fitter athletes process food better.
The gut is trainable like heart and skeletal muscle: faster carbohydrate absorption, quicker digestion, resistance to bacterial damage (Carrio 1989; Costa 2017; Cox 2010; Harris, Lindeman, Martin 1991; Jeukendrup and McLaughlin 2011). Use different types of calories in training to learn what works. Nausea is accentuated in ultras because duration lets poor nutritional choices accumulate (okay for a 4-hour marathon or 50K, not 20 more hours).
tags: type:mechanism focus:gut-health sport:running level:all subtopic:nausea
evidence: study (multiple cited)
src: Ch 4 | "Fueling and GI Distress"

### [TEU-C04-013] Gut damage and endotoxin leakage in ultramarathons

Endurance running damages the gut through digestion, reduced blood flow and constant jostling (Heer 1987; Lucas and Schroy 1998; Øktedalen 1992; Papaioannides 1984). Monash University (Gill et al. 2015) found most ultramarathon participants had blood markers of endotoxin leakage equivalent to hospital patients with an acute infectious episode, i.e. an immune response as severe as a life-threatening infection. Little evidence on how to avoid it; the one correlating factor: individuals who trained more showed less damage (Monash University 2015). Takeaway: better training = less gut damage = less GI distress = better performance.
tags: type:mechanism focus:gut-health sport:running level:all subtopic:endotoxin
evidence: study (Gill et al. 2015)
src: Ch 4 | "Damage to the gut"

### [TEU-C04-014] Blister mechanism: friction, shear, heat, moisture, hydration

A runner's blister is a "friction blister". Heat + moisture + friction = blister (Figure 4.3). Friction/shear forces (parallel to ground, anterior-posterior and mediolateral) act on each foot strike between ground-shoe, insole-sock, sock-skin and reverse at push-off. Soft tissue moves more than rigid bone; shoe and sock move against the epidermis more than epidermis against dermis; layers separate and fluid fills the void by hydrostatic pressure.
- Heat loosens the dermis-epidermis bond; a 4°C rise in skin temperature accelerates blister formation by 50 percent (Hashmi 2013; Kiistala 1972b). Sources: environment, metabolism, friction heat; warmer feet sweat more.
- Moisture (sweat, dumping water, river crossings, rain) increases friction and weakens the outer skin (Naylor 1955; Worthing 2017).
- Hydration: even slight hyponatremia pulls water from plasma to extracellular space, pooling in extremities (puffy feet), making shoes tight and loosening skin layers, accelerating blisters.
Variables in Figure 4.3: environment, shoe/sock material, sweating, shoe/sock fit, hydration status, biomechanics, trail dust/debris.
Blisters matter because foot damage alters biomechanics up the kinetic chain, causing a cascade (nutrition disrupted, longer exposure) and often DNF.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:blisters
evidence: study (Hashmi 2013; Kiistala 1972b; Naylor 1955; Worthing 2017)
src: Ch 4 | "Blisters"

### [TEU-C04-015] Muscular breakdown: eccentric contractions and CK

Significant muscle breakdown occurs in ultras (CK studies in Spartathlon, UTMB, Western States). Post-race CK ranges from relatively normal to more than 100 times normal (Fallon 1999; Kim, Lee, Kim 2007; Millet 2011; Overgaard 2002); huge individual variability (Figure 4.5: UTMB finishers, n=22, post-race CK 0-50,000 IU/L; most below 20,000; subject 3 about 50,000, subject 7 over 45,000, subject 17 above 20,000; approximate from chart). Correlations of fatigue with volume, vertical gain/loss, nutrition, pacing or hydration are scant, so rely on best practices from other areas.
Biomechanics: muscles only pull. Positive force propels; an equal negative force is needed at foot strike as the body lowers, when muscles actively lengthen = eccentric contraction (unavoidable in walking/running; DeVita 2007; Enoka 2008). Breakdown varies with individual, velocity, force and total repetitions (Chapman 2006; Nosaka and Clarkson 1996; Tiidus and Ianuzzo 1982). Stronger, more frequent, more numerous eccentric contractions reduce muscle function; ultras involve hundreds of thousands of steps.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:eccentric-muscle-damage
evidence: study (multiple cited)
src: Ch 4 | "Muscular Breakdown and Fatigue"

### [TEU-C04-016] Biomechanical differences: ground reaction forces, EMG, joint angles

**Ground reaction forces (GRFs).** Each foot strike is met by an equal force from the ground.
- Level running: vertical force of about 2.5 to 3 times body weight per step. Running faster increases these forces.
- Uphill and downhill at the same speed: peak vertical forces are only slightly different, slightly greater downhill. In the real world you go downhill much faster, so at the same effort level downhill running produces much larger forces than level or uphill running.
- Walking: forces are far more benign and the pattern is different. Peak GRF is 1.2 times body weight on level terrain.
- Fig 5.1 (73 kg runner, 3 m/s, ~9 min/mi): downhill shows large braking (negative parallel) peaks and minimal propulsion. As slope goes toward uphill, braking falls and propulsion rises. At +9° it is almost all propulsive.
- Fig 5.2: running vertical GRF rises with speed (5:21 min/mi = 5 m/s; 6:42 min/mi = 4 m/s; 9:00 min/mi = 3 m/s). Running shows an impact peak at ~15–20% of stance and an active peak at ~50%. Walking is bimodal and much lower.

**Muscles (EMG) and joint segment angles.**
- EMG measures the electrical activity of surface muscles; more forceful contraction gives a higher reading.
- Joint segment angles come from high-speed 3D video and show how limbs move.
- Fig 5.3 (Cappellini et al. 2006): going walk to run to walk on a level treadmill, nearly all lower leg muscles showed more excitation in running. The pattern of muscle activation also changed significantly, so muscles work together differently.
- Fig 5.4: hip, knee and foot positions differ in walking, level running, uphill running and downhill running.
- Conclusion: the four disciplines present different biomechanical stressors, and muscles are used differently in each.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:ground-reaction-force subtopic:emg subtopic:joint-angles
evidence: study (Cavanagh and Lafortune 1980; Gottschall and Kram 2005; Browning and Kram 2007; Cappellini et al. 2006)
src: Ch 5 "Biomechanical Differences" | Figs 5.1–5.4

### [TEU-C04-017] When to hike and when to run: grade and speed, not economy alone

- University of Colorado Boulder Locomotion Laboratory built a treadmill reaching 45 degrees. For reference, the back side of Hope Pass (Leadville 100) is about 10 degrees (18 percent grade). Research is "in its infancy".
- The run-to-hike transition is determined by many factors, not one. Ultramarathon performance is less tied to aerobic power and running economy than other endurance events such as the marathon. Localized neuromuscular fatigue, supraspinal input, muscle damage and other non-cardiovascular factors also matter, and together can affect performance more than economy changes alone.
- The decision involves both grade and speed. There is no universal grade or speed for switching. Dismiss absolute advice like "always hike on grades over 10 percent".
- Author's working theory (a guess): the range of speeds where hiking beats running is relatively small on most normal grades (~4 to 16 percent).
- Level ground: the U-shaped cost-of-transport vs walking speed curve has its minimum at preferred walking speed (PWS), ~1.5–1.6 m/s, about 16:45–18:00 min/mi. The preferred transition speed (PTS) to running is ~2 m/s (~13:30 min/mi). At PTS walking is still more economical than running, so preference depends on more than oxygen cost. The gap between the PTS and the truly most economical transition explains why there is no single magic transition speed. (Fig 5.5, Bramble and Lieberman 2004)
- Steep treadmill: at a 30° slope (58 percent grade), all runners were more economical below 0.9 m/s (just under 30 min/mi). On extremely steep terrain only the best runners at the highest speeds do better running than walking. (Fig 5.6, Ortiz et al. 2017)
- At constant vertical speed, walking is more economical across a range of (very fast) speeds and grades, except a 10° slope (17 percent grade) at 2.14 m/s, which is a 12:30 min/mi pace up the back of Hope Pass (slightly under 18 percent). It applies only to the fastest runners. (Fig 5.7, Giovanelli et al. 2015)
- Some combination of grade and speed makes walking vs running more economical for all runners, but the combinations are not yet known.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:run-hike-transition subtopic:running-economy
evidence: study (Ortiz et al. 2017; Giovanelli et al. 2015; Bramble and Lieberman 2004)
src: Ch 5 "When to Hike and When to Run" | Figs 5.5–5.7

### [TEU-C04-018] Workload vs work rate in running (physics basis)

- Volume = amount of training (time or miles); intensity = effort. Total workload (kilojoules) and work rate (power) better indicate training stress.
- Work = force × distance. In level running, workload is a function of body weight (ground reaction forces) and distance; a heavier runner has higher workload for the same miles.
- Work rate = work / time. On level ground a mile costs about the same work regardless of speed (caloric expenditure: total calories = 1 calorie per kilogram per kilometer, a function of distance and weight, not speed). Work rate depends on speed: faster = higher work rate; heavier runner has higher work rate at same speed.
- On flat terrain workload ≈ miles, work rate ≈ pace; comparable between runners of the same mass.
- Cycling power meters measure workload/work rate directly; nothing analogous exists for trail running (surface and elevation gain/loss make it problematic).
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:workload-work-rate metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Comparing Workload and Work Rate"

### [TEU-C04-019] Why heart rate is a poor intensity tool: confounding factors

Heart rate is an indirect response to muscular workload (correlates strongly with workload; HR zones created in the 1980s) but is not reliable or predictable enough for prescribing intensity in ultrarunning. Factors:
- Core temperature: higher core temp raises HR at given intensity (blood redirected to skin for cooling).
- Caffeine and other stimulants raise HR.
- Excitation/nervousness (epinephrine, norepinephrine); frustration, anger, anxiety also.
- Hydration status: falling blood volume raises HR, with or without core temp rise.
- Altitude: HR and respiration rise starting at about 5,000 feet above sea level (lower oxygen partial pressure).
- Fatigue: suppresses HR; response to rising demand is slower and blunted; tired athlete cannot reach usual HR for an intensity.
- Cardiac drift (below).
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:heart-rate-confounders metric:hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Factors Affecting Heart Rate"

### [TEU-C04-020] Cardiac drift

Up to 75 percent of muscle energy is lost as heat; skin acts like a radiator. HR rises to deliver oxygen and to send blood to skin for sweat/convection cooling. Sweat fluid comes from plasma, so blood plasma volume falls and HR must rise to deliver the same oxygen. Result: HR rises slightly over time at constant effort. Good hydration lowers the impact, but some drift is unavoidable in intense endurance exercise.
Consequence (Fig 6.5, 3 × 10-min TempoRun, interval HR avg 145, 150, 152 bpm; max 152, 157, 157 at similar pace): training by a fixed HR range makes the first interval faster than later ones; workload actually falls and workout loses effectiveness.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:cardiac-drift metric:hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Cardiac Drift" | Fig 6.5

### [TEU-C04-021] Mechanism: respiration, CO2 and RER link to RPE

Resting breathing is driven more by CO2 to expel than by O2 need. As energy demand rises CO2 rises; heart and breathing rate rise to transport it. CO2 production is nonlinear: higher intensity draws a higher share of energy from carbohydrate (glycolysis breaks down only carbohydrate, produces lactate), producing more CO2. RER (CO2 expired / O2 inspired) is 0.7 to 0.85 at rest/low activity (mixed fuels) and approaches and can exceed 1.0 with rising glycolytic carbohydrate use. This dramatic CO2 rise is why breathing rate signals lactate threshold.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:rer system:lactate
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "RPE and Breathing Rate"

### [TEU-C04-022] Why altitude feels hard: partial pressure of oxygen

- Air composition does not change from sea level to 8,000 ft: 20.93% oxygen, 0.03% carbon dioxide, 79.04% nitrogen.
- What drops with elevation is the partial pressure of oxygen. Lungs rely on a pressure gradient for inhalation, exhalation and gas exchange between alveoli and capillary beds (Sinex and Chapman 2015). There is not "less air", but less oxygen per lungful.
- Mexico City 1968 Olympics (~7,350 ft) heightened research interest in altitude performance.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:partial-pressure sport:running
evidence: research (Sinex and Chapman 2015)
src: Ch 7 "Altitude"

### [TEU-C04-023] Physiological adaptations to altitude (acute and long term)

Adaptations are two-pronged: some occur in the first 24 hours, others take several weeks; they involve cardiovascular, respiratory, and metabolic systems, not just the lungs.
- Cardiovascular/hematologic (almost immediate): hemoconcentration. Plasma volume can fall 10 to 25%, raising hematocrit (percentage of red blood cells per unit of blood), temporarily improving oxygen transport (Beidleman et al. 2017).
- Heart rate is elevated for the first few days: lower blood volume lowers stroke volume, so HR rises to keep cardiac output stable (Q = HR x stroke volume). Rest and low aerobic intensities normalize sooner. People generally feel worst 24 to 72 hours after arrival.
- Athletic programs recommend keeping intensity low (below 70 percent of VO2 max) on first arrival to avoid fatigue or maladaptation (Sinex and Chapman 2015).
- Respiratory: less oxygen entering blood increases ventilation rate and depth. Faster breathing exhales more CO2, so less CO2 in blood, which can lead to respiratory alkalosis (pH imbalance). The kidneys correct this mainly by increasing urine volume.
- Kidneys in hypoxia also release more erythropoietin (EPO; Goldfarb-Rumyantzev and Alper 2014), stimulating erythropoiesis (red blood cell production). Erythropoiesis takes at least 1 week and up to 2 to 3 weeks for cells to mature (Garvican-Lewis 2017).
- This long-term response lets plasma volume return to normal and then expand, with increased red blood cells, hematocrit and hemoglobin: the desired adaptation of carrying more oxygen.
- Figure 7.1 loop: hypoxia (decreased RBC count, decreased O2 availability, or increased tissue O2 demand) -> imbalance in blood O2 -> reduced O2 in blood -> kidney (and liver to a lesser extent) release EPO -> EPO stimulates red bone marrow -> increased RBC count -> increased O2-carrying ability -> normal blood O2.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:epo subtopic:hemoconcentration metric:hr sport:running
evidence: research (Beidleman 2017; Sinex and Chapman 2015; Garvican-Lewis 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (altitude) | Fig 7.1

### [TEU-C04-024] Thermoregulation and heat production

- Humans are inefficient: only a fraction of work propels you; most generates heat. Core temperature ideal 37°C/98.6°F.
- Cold: blood vessels constrict, shivering produces heat. Heat: vessels dilate, more blood to skin, sweat glands activate for evaporative cooling (hypothalamus-driven; Fig 7.3).
- Running produces as much as 5 to 15 times more heat than at rest (Sawka et al. 1993), plus environmental heat strain.
- Four heat transfer categories (Sawka et al. 2011): conduction (direct transfer of energy), convection (air or water moving over skin), radiation (direct or indirect electromagnetic heat transfer), evaporation (liquid to gas). Effect depends on temperature gradient between body and environment (heat moves high to low). Environment examples: pavement reflecting sun (Badwater 135), direct vs indirect sun, wind direction. Fig 7.4 shows solar, reflected, infrared exchange, evaporation, convection, metabolic heat.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:thermoregulation subtopic:heat-transfer sport:running
evidence: research (Sawka 1993, 2011)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (heat) | Fig 7.3, 7.4

### [TEU-C04-025] Heat acclimation adaptations and time course (Figure 7.5)

Acclimation: the body becoming more efficient and adjusting to the environment over time (applies to altitude and heat). Key adaptations from chronic daily heat exposure (Périard, Racinais, and Sawka 2015; Rivas et al. 2017):
- earlier onset of sweat response; higher sweat rate with lower sweat sodium concentration; better sodium and chloride reabsorption in sweat duct
- lower skin and core temperature via plasma volume expansion
- lower heart rate during exercise (a return to normal)
- increased fluid and cardiovascular stability; lower metabolic cost of exercise
- Most important result: ability to tolerate and feel comfortable in heat.
Mechanism: repeatedly raising core temperature (exercise, environment, passive sauna/hot-water immersion, or all) induces adaptations increasing thermal tolerance (Minett et al. 2016).
Fig 7.5 (adaptation as % of Day 1, days 1–14, read from chart, approximate): plasma volume peaks ~days 5–6 then eases; heart rate falls sharply to ~80% by day 6 and plateaus; exercise capacity rises to ~116% by ~day 13; thermal comfort plateaus ~108% from days 5–6; skin temperature decreases to day 6 then stable; core temperature falls through days 5–6, slight further decrease to day 11; sweating rate near baseline through day 4, rises days 4–11, plateau ~117% days 11–14.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:heat-acclimation metric:hr sport:running
evidence: review (Périard, Racinais, Sawka 2015; Rivas 2017; Minett 2016)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (heat) | Fig 7.5

### [TEU-C04-026] Consequences of too little vs too much stress; supercompensation, overreaching, OTS/UUPS

**Too much rest/recovery:** performance first improves as stress recedes. When training frequency drops or the time between stimuli becomes too long, workouts stop building on one another and fitness stagnates or slides backward.

**Detraining resilience:**
- Complete cessation (zero training) for several weeks can cause performance declines of >20 percent.
- Keeping as few as three sessions per week (~50 percent training reduction) holds losses to 5 to 10 percent, even over several weeks of detraining.
- Cutting training in half costs only a single-digit penalty, and the return to previous fitness does not take long.
- Refs: Bosquet, Laurent and Mujika 2012; Lacour and Denis 1982; Mujika and Padilla 2000; Neufer 1989; Pallarés et al. 2009.

**Too much work/too little recovery:**
- To induce adaptation, a physiological system must be stressed until performance starts to decline. One session causes a small decrease, usually too small for significant long-term adaptation.
- After a session with adequate recovery there is a short-lived "supercompensation" effect with increased performance. Proper training architecture schedules repeated bouts to exploit this cycle.
- Over weeks, cumulative stress produces a decrease in performance called functional overreaching. It is reversed by adequate recovery and is a normal part of training.
- Trouble comes from pushing functional overreaching too far: piling on fatigue instead of letting up. Eventually more than two weeks of complete rest cannot reverse extreme fatigue, disrupted sleep, irritability and lack of desire to train.
- This state is called "overtraining syndrome" (OTS), but the author says training is not the root problem, recovery is. "Under-recovery" is more accurate. The newer term "unexplained underperformance syndrome" (UUPS) pushes athletes and coaches to look beyond the training plan at all sources of stress and rule out RED-S, anemia, hypothyroidism, and acute or chronic illness (Lewis 2015).
- The tipping point between functional overreaching and OTS/UUPS is highly individual and hard to quantify, because individual symptoms can be part of a normal response to hard, effective training.
- "To the body, stress is stress": workout, hard day at the office, argument, or infection. A multimodal recovery approach gives many tools to chip away at the stress.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle level:all subtopic:overreaching subtopic:supercompensation subtopic:detraining subtopic:overtraining-syndrome sport:running metric:load
evidence: meta-analysis (Bosquet, Laurent and Mujika 2012; others cited above); Lewis 2015
src: Ch 8 "The Consequences of Doing Too Much" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C04-027] Architecture of sleep: temperature, cortisol, melatonin (Figure 8.1)

- Cortisol prepares you to deal with stress. Melatonin helps regulate the sleep-wake cycle.
- At sleep onset, core body temperature and cortisol go down and melatonin goes up. The key to powerful sleep is setting up the room and habits to take advantage of this constellation of changes.
- Figure 8.1 (described): the sleep period is 8 to 10 hours, running from Bedtime to Wake up.
  - Core body temperature peaks near bedtime, falls to its minimum in the middle of sleep, and rises again approaching wake-up.
  - Cortisol is low around bedtime and early in sleep, rises sharply in the second half of sleep, and peaks around wake-up.
  - Melatonin is low in the daytime, rises sharply at the start of sleep to a high peak, and falls to low levels around wake-up.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:sleep subtopic:circadian metric:hrv
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Architecture of Sleep"; Figure 8.1 | p. n/a

### [TEU-C04-028] The Body-Weight Conundrum (sidebar)

Lighter is not always better for performance, recovery, health or emotional well-being.
- **Energy cost of running:** on flat ground, energy expenditure per distance is roughly independent of speed. Losing weight reduces the energy cost of running by 1 percent for each 1 percent reduction in body weight. Over 160K (100 miles), a 5 kg loss saves about 800 calories.
- **Ground reaction forces:** vertical GRFs are typically 2.5 to 3 times body weight, so weight loss lowers them.
- **Muscle damage:** much greater in an ultramarathon than a marathon. More muscle may spread damage over more tissue, which may partly explain why top ultrarunners are more muscular than extremely lean marathoners.
- **VO2max:** with weight loss, absolute VO2max stays pretty constant and relative VO2max rises. Example: a 70 kg elite ultrarunner with VO2max 65 ml/kg/min. A higher relative VO2max raises top intensity, aerobic pace and lactate threshold pace, lengthens how long a pace can be sustained, and speeds recovery from efforts above lactate threshold.
- **Bigger picture:** proactively pursuing weight loss often does ultrarunners more harm than good. More leg muscle correlates with faster ultramarathon finishing times (Knechtle, Rosemann and Lepers 2010). Training volume and marathon finishing time predicted ultra performance better than any body measurement (Knechtle et al. 2009; 2010; Knechtle and Senn 2011).
- Author's preference: focus on cardiovascular development and the limiting factors of Ch 4. Weight management then tends to follow from increased training volume and intensity. Coaches who focus on weight loss to improve performance risk creating abusive environments and forgo the improvements better training would give.
tags: type:principle type:mechanism level:all subtopic:body-weight subtopic:vo2max sport:running focus:weight-management
evidence: clinical-trial (Knechtle et al.); author-experience
src: Ch 8 sidebar "The Body-Weight Conundrum" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C04-029] Time course of adaptation and diminishing returns (Seiler 2006, Fig 10.4)

- Theoretical curve (Seiler 2006), x-axis years of sport-specific training (0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4), y-axis improvement (no values): VO2 max improves rapidly then plateaus after ~1 year; lactate threshold improves steadily and plateaus after 3 to 4 years; efficiency has no plateau after 4 years.
- Higher intensities produce changes in endurance athletes more quickly but to a lesser extent and reach diminishing returns sooner than lower intensities. As fitness rises, it takes longer to improve less (generally good). Diminishing returns may arrive after weeks, months or years depending on the stress. Even when VO2 max/threshold plateau, efficiency (and thus performance) can still improve.
- Analogy: eating cookies, each one less satisfying.
tags: type:mechanism type:evidence system:vo2max system:threshold level:all subtopic:diminishing-returns subtopic:efficiency
evidence: author-experience citing Seiler 2006
src: Ch 10 Fig 10.4

### [TEU-C04-030] Strength training: running economy and injury-reduction mechanisms

- Strength training is long used by distance runners for performance and injury prevention (injury prevention indirectly improves performance via training availability). Studies nearly universally show improved running economy, increased VO2 max, improved real-world performance and reduced injury rates (Balsalobre-Fernández et al. 2016).
- Author's caution: improvements are maybe a few percent, probably not clearly applicable to real life, usually cost time and effort; the key question is how much they matter for ultramarathon performance.
- Running economy: legs act as springs. Muscles/tendons stretch during gait like a rubber band, and the elastic energy is harnessed and released ("free" energy). Heavy lifting and jumping increase spring stiffness (a "thicker rubber band") and neuromuscular recruitment, improving push against the ground. Improvement is 2 to 4 percent (Balsalobre-Fernández et al. 2016); uncertain whether it matters over 100 miles.
- Injury: one meta-analysis (Lauersen 2018) suggests ~66 percent reduction in injury risk in athletes who strength train. Fewer missed days means more consistent training, a predictor of ultramarathon performance and finishing rates. Other injury-lowering factors: rest days (Ristolainen et al. 2014), sleeping 8+ hours (O'Donnell 2018), avoiding RED-S.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all system:aerobic subtopic:running-economy subtopic:injury-prevention
evidence: meta-analysis (Lauersen 2018; Balsalobre-Fernández et al. 2016; Ristolainen et al. 2014; O'Donnell 2018)
src: Ch 11 | "Strength Training as an Ergogenic Aid"

### [TEU-C04-031] Why endurance athletes do not bulk up (concurrent training)

- Most athletes will not look like bodybuilders from a couple of strength days per week; there is nearly no hypertrophy with concurrent strength and endurance training. Long-term studies (24 to 40 weeks) showed no muscular growth despite strength improvements (Blagrow, Howatson, and Hays 2017).
- Mechanism as stated: strength training upregulates protein synthesis and releases mTOR; with concurrent endurance training, adenosine monophosphate-activated kinase (as written by the author, "enzyme") blunts mTOR effects.
- Result: strength, power and muscle-tendon stiffness can increase without mass gain.
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:hypertrophy subtopic:concurrent-training
evidence: clinical-trial (Blagrow, Howatson, and Hays 2017)
src: Ch 11 | "But Won't I Gain Weight That Will Slow Me Down?"

### [TEU-C04-032] Macronutrients and energy production during exercise

- Energy comes from carbohydrate, protein and fat at all times and at all activity levels; the share depends on how fast energy is needed and how much supply of each exists.
- At rest ~80-90% of energy comes from fat (brain uses carbohydrate). At very low intensity (20-25% VO2max) fat still gives ~70% because the body conserves carbohydrate.
- Carbohydrate storage is only ~1,600-2,000 calories (muscle and liver); even a lean athlete (70 kg/154 lb, 10% body fat) has >53,000 calories of stored fat.
- Aerobic energy production occurs in mitochondria (carbohydrate and fat to CO2, water, resynthesized ATP). More and bigger mitochondria = more fuel processed per minute.
- With rising demand the percentage from carbohydrate rises exponentially, from endurance pace to lactate threshold and above. Absolute fat and protein use also rise.
- There is NO "switch" from fat to carbohydrate. Absolute fat oxidation rises from RecoveryRun to EnduranceRun intensity; near the upper EnduranceRun / lower TempoRun intensity (60-70% VO2max, depending on trainability; Maunder et al. 2018) fat oxidation begins to decline.
- Crossover: at 60-70% VO2max fuel is about equal fat and carbohydrate. Better-trained athletes reach and keep this 50:50 balance at a higher % of VO2max. Above 60% VO2max the carbohydrate share rises dramatically (still aerobic metabolism).
- Beyond lactate threshold intensity (70-90% VO2max depending on fitness), >80% of energy is from carbohydrate.
- Fat is slower to break down than carbohydrate. Fatal flaw of extremely low-carbohydrate strategies: limited by the rate muscles can process fat, which needs ~8% more oxygen than carbohydrate; you keep moving but slower at the same relative intensity.
- Glycolysis releases part of carbohydrate energy quickly, yielding less energy per molecule than complete oxidation. Lactate is not the cause of fatigue once thought; the best way to clear it is to reintegrate it as fuel in aerobic metabolism. Endurance training at a variety of intensities does this well and speeds recovery from hard efforts: glycolysis is "buying energy on credit" you repay by slowing down; faster lactate processing means less slowing.
- Protein: needed for building/maintaining muscle, immune function, enzymes, but a poor fuel (mostly converted to carbohydrate in liver via gluconeogenesis). Contributes only 10-15% of total caloric expenditure regardless of intensity.
tags: type:mechanism level:all system:fat-oxidation system:aerobic system:lactate subtopic:crossover-concept subtopic:substrate-use metric:pace
src: Ch 13 "Macronutrients and Energy Production" | Figure 13.1
evidence: author-experience; Maunder et al. 2018 (60-70% VO2max)

### [TEU-C04-033] Micronutrients in ultrarunning

Vitamins and minerals are essential (energy production, oxygen binding, bone density, muscle contraction), but little is lost through sweat or used during training/racing, while the athlete eats plenty of food. A normally healthy person without a deficiency condition will not improve performance by focusing on micronutrient intake or supplementation, provided the diet is diverse, has adequate total calories, and does not eliminate specific foods or macronutrients.
tags: type:principle level:all condition:general focus:micronutrients subtopic:supplementation
src: Ch 13 "Micronutrients"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C04-034] Essential roles of fluids

- Core temperature regulation: much exercise energy is lost as heat; the body works only within ~97-104°F, with 97-100°F optimal for performance (Gleeson 1998). Sweat evaporation is the primary cooling; sweat is replaced from blood plasma, making blood volume the reservoir. Fluid need depends on sweat rate and body size, which vary with intensity, temperature, wind, humidity.
- Gut motility and digestion: carbohydrate needs enough water in the intestine to be transported. When sweating to cool, the body prioritizes thermoregulation over digestion; digestion slows dramatically and is slow to recover; food sitting in gut ferments and is jostled, causing pressure, bloating and a cascade of GI issues. Nutrition strategy depends on hydration status.
- Blood volume: 4.5-5.5 L of blood; delivers oxygen and nutrients, removes waste, carries heat to skin, supplies sweat. Training and heat/altitude acclimation increase plasma volume. Low plasma volume: better-hydrated athletes stay warmer in cold/wet conditions (dehydration hastens hypothermia); in heat, resting and exercise heart rate rise; lower still, the body prioritizes sweating over digestion; in dire cases oxygen delivery over sweating, causing heatstroke.
- Waste removal: kidneys filter waste into urine; important in 50-/100-mile events given muscle damage. Mild dehydration darkens urine and reduces output; severe dehydration can damage kidneys and alter blood pH.
tags: type:mechanism level:all focus:hydration subtopic:thermoregulation subtopic:blood-volume subtopic:gut-motility
src: Ch 13 "The Essential Roles of Fluids"
evidence: Gleeson 1998; author-experience

### [TEU-C04-035] Mouth to bloodstream: digestion and absorption pathway

Everything ingested combines in the stomach; how it combines determines speed into the small intestine and bloodstream. Properties of one drink matter less than understanding this pathway.
- Mouth: anticipation (opening a packet, smelling food) triggers saliva and gastric juices; chewing/saliva begin breakdown.
- Stomach to intestine: stomach holds the slurry until chemical balance is right ("gastric emptying"); athletes want it rapid. Slowed by highly concentrated foods/solutions (gels, concentrated drinks), which wait for dilution, hence ~8 oz water with a gel. Too much food/fluid also slows emptying (sloshing belly). To a point more stomach volume speeds emptying and it is trainable, but there is a limit, so smaller fluid volumes several times per hour beat guzzling the full amount once an hour.
- Intestinal absorption: wall is a selective semipermeable membrane. Water moves passively from low osmolality (intestine) to high osmolality (interstitial space) between cells. Osmolality = total amount dissolved in a fluid.
- Carbohydrate is actively transported through distinct channels per sugar type (glucose, fructose, etc.); multiple sugar types let you absorb energy faster (more "doors"). Sugar also drags water with it.
- Sodium: passive between cells, plus two active routes: (1) cotransport with glucose (one glucose with two sodium ions) into the intestinal cell, so sodium and carbohydrate absorb faster together (why low-calorie electrolyte drinks contain some sugar); (2) the sodium-potassium pump (3 Na out, 2 K in) moves sodium to the interstitial space, raising osmolality and drawing more water.
- Into blood: pressure in the interstitial space rises; the capillary membrane is more permeable than the intestinal side, so water, sodium and carbohydrate move into the bloodstream.
tags: type:mechanism level:all focus:hydration focus:macronutrients subtopic:gastric-emptying subtopic:absorption subtopic:osmolality
src: Ch 13 "How Sports Nutrition Moves from Mouth to Bloodstream" | Figure 13.3
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C04-036] Fat adaptation: how it occurs and its costs (author's view)

Context: fat adaptation (using more fat at higher intensities) has been hot for a decade (Burke, Jeukendrup and others). Attractive for ultras: GI distress is a main cause of stopping; ultra intensities are low and use more fat than 10K/marathon; fat stores are virtually unlimited. Substrate metabolism is trainable; with diet and training fat oxidation can be as much as doubled at a given submaximal intensity (Purdom et al. 2018).
Misconception: LCHF/keto is not the only route. Fat adaptation comes through (1) normal endurance training (more aerobic energy production, more oxygen utilized at a given intensity, so more opportunity to use fat); (2) dietary interventions (LCHF/ketogenic upregulate fat oxidation pathways); (3) changing fuel availability in specific sessions. The body is a "flex-fuel machine".
Perceived win-win: tap a near-limitless fuel and rely less on exogenous carbohydrate (self-sufficient, fewer gels, less GI distress).
Downsides:
- Oxygen/economy cost: about 8 percent more oxygen to liberate energy from fat than from carbohydrate. Argument that the penalty matters little at 50–60 percent VO2max is answered: it is not just less economical, it limits capacity to use other substrates.
- Diet manipulation makes carbohydrate scarcer and the body less efficient at using it (Burke et al. 2016; Jeukendrup 2017). Even in the most fat-adapted state, ultras need some exogenous carbohydrate; without carbohydrate during/around training you may not transport the carbohydrate you ingest, ironically raising GI distress risk.
- Training suffers: reduced economy lowers performance, esp. at high intensity; less intensity or less time at intensity, so less training stress from TempoRuns/RunningIntervals; some adaptations are attenuated without carbohydrate.
- Bone health: Heikura et al. 2020 found after 3.5 weeks of LCHF increased CTX (bone resorption marker) and decreased P1NP (bone formation marker); possible implications for injury risk and long-term bone health.
Author's position: fat adaptation is neither all good nor all bad; no compelling evidence for a full-time LCHF strategy for ultrarunners for performance; if an athlete chooses LCHF it is fine if they accept the performance limits; alternatively manipulate diet for specific goals.
tags: type:mechanism type:evidence level:all sport:running system:fat-oxidation focus:macronutrients subtopic:fat-adaptation subtopic:bone-stress condition:general
evidence: clinical-trial (Heikura 2020); Purdom 2018; Burke 2016; Jeukendrup 2017
src: Ch 14 "What about Strategies to Enhance Fat Metabolism?" | no page markers

### [TEU-C04-037] Psychobiological model of fatigue (PBM)

- Limiting factors (musculoskeletal stress, thermal load, glycogen depletion) matter, but what slows us is increasingly understood to be how we psychologically perceive them. Physiological capacity = upper limit; psychological skills decide how much of it is used (Ikai and Steinhaus 1961). Figure 15.1: improved mental skills raise the utilized portion of an unchanged total physiological capacity.
- PBM: motivation and perception of effort are the primary drivers of performance; brain and body act in concert, constantly modulating effort, pain, fatigue and perception of these.
- Contrast with Tim Noakes's central governor theory (original form): brain as ultimate regulator whose only goal is to guard against catastrophe. That led to strategies built on maximizing pain/discomfort tolerance ("no pain, no gain", early-2000s high-intensity-training craze, athletes pushing often too hard). Toughness and grit are desirable but not the only mental skills.
- PBM puts the athlete and her regulatory skills in the driver's seat: we interpret sensations (pain, effort, drowsiness, fatigue, discoordination) and can voluntarily change regulatory strategies such as pacing. Allows deliberately enhancing motivation, shifting attention inward/outward, and using ergogenic aids like caffeine to alter perception of the sensations telling us to slow down.
- Author's interpretation is an amalgamation of several authors, most notably Samuele Marcora (Marcora 2008, 2009; Marcora and Staiano 2010).
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:psychobiological-model subtopic:central-governor metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience (cites Ikai and Steinhaus 1961; Noakes 1997, 2012; Noakes et al. 2005; Marcora 2008, 2009, Marcora and Staiano 2010)
src: Ch 15 "Psychobiological Model of Fatigue" | Fig 15.1

### [TEU-C04-038] Perceived exertion end point interaction

- During a difficult task we continually monitor effort to mete it out. Example: 3 x 12-minute TempoRun intervals; you pace the first so you can complete the next two. You mentally draw a line from current effort to what you think you can tolerate at the end (the perceived exertion end point), and redraw it as new pain/fatigue sensations arrive, modulating effort to the end (Figs 15.2, 15.3: if forecast exceeds maximum tolerable exertion, adjust pace to reduce rate of rise so you reach maximum tolerable exertion exactly at FINISH).
- Works for workouts, short races, 5K, even marathon. In an ultramarathon it backfires (Fig 15.4): predictive capacity is limited and duration confounds forecasting; an early forecast that exertion will exceed the tolerable maximum leads athletes to drop out halfway ("I don't think I can finish") with plenty of physiological reserve, then regret it moments later.
- Remedy: "stay in the moment" with constant evaluations (Fig 15.5), bypassing the tendency to evaluate and predict effort. Discussed further in the next chapter (Ch 16).
tags: type:mechanism sport:running level:all subtopic:perceived-exertion-end-point subtopic:dropping-out metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Perceived Exertion End Point Interactions" | Figs 15.2-15.5

### [TEU-C04-039] Balancing challenge and affinity for risk (Figures 16.1-16.5)

Successful outcome goals balance achievable and challenging on an "achievability teeter-totter" set by individual risk tolerance. Closer to the limits of capability = higher risk that must be accepted. Goals beyond capabilities are not well constructed. Risk-averse athletes (not a character flaw) need goals more within their comfort zone, even on an underperforming day.
Gauge states: "Exceedingly achievable, not challenging" | "Challenging and achievable" (ideal) | "Challenge exceeds affinity for risk".
- Fig 16.1: challenge balanced with risk affinity = ideal.
- Fig 16.2: bigger challenge balanced with larger affinity for risk; athletes with greater risk affinity can pursue greater challenges.
- Fig 16.3: easier challenge balanced with smaller affinity; good for beginners, since risk affinity typically increases with experience, making harder events achievable over time.
- Fig 16.4: highly challenging goal but insufficient risk tolerance: unlikely to be achieved. You may be physically capable yet fail repeatedly unless risk affinity lets you fully apply the capacity.
- Fig 16.5: high risk affinity with highly achievable goal: out of balance; virtually guaranteed but not challenging enough.
Why not easy goals: disrespects your abilities and leads to complacency, lack of focus, big mistakes; challenging goals force focus because training, nutrition and strategy must go right; easy goals mean you don't prepare and can be in a dangerous position on race day.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle level:all sport:running subtopic:risk-affinity subtopic:goal-setting
src: Ch 16 "Risky Business" Figs 16.1-16.5 | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C04-040] Perceived exertion end point interaction and staying in the moment

Mental tricks ("focus on the mile you are in", "one aid station at a time") are compartmentalizing the effort, which has a scientific rationale.
Study: Wingfield et al. 2018 (Australia), 30K cycling time trial with vs without distance-remaining cues: athletes who knew distance achieved higher power (particularly last two minutes) with no change in heart rate or RPE (Fig 16.8).
Perceived exertion end point interaction: athletes pace by (1) how they feel at the moment and (2) how far they have to go; they constantly draw and redraw an internal line from present feeling to projected feeling at the end point, calibrating so they are spent at the very end. If the forecast exceeds maximum tolerable exertion, the athlete drops out; if within, finishes (Fig 16.9).
Explains: novices pace poorly (don't know the end point); "tougher" athletes accept a steeper slope/higher end point (Fig 16.10); youth start too hard (poor internal exertion measures). Related models: Noakes central governor (Noakes, Peltonen, Rusko 2001); Tucker Anticipatory Regulation Model (2009).
Forecasting DNFs: at Leadville mile 75 (Outward Bound), appearance does not correlate with dropping; each runner extrapolates current feeling to the remaining 25 miles (including Powerline climb); if the imagined line exceeds tolerance they drop. Finishers have higher exertion tolerance, more accurate forecast, or lower instantaneous suffering; forecasting error is the same for all given long duration and unknowns.
In the moment: in ultras, predicting feelings hours ahead is clouded by duration and impaired comprehension. When all seems hopeless, forget forecasting, stay in the moment, focus on present effort, use Chapter 15 mental skills.
tags: type:mechanism type:principle level:all sport:running metric:rpe subtopic:perceived-exertion-end-point subtopic:dnf subtopic:mental-skills
src: Ch 16 "Why Staying in the Moment Works" Figs 16.8-16.10 | no page markers
evidence: study (Wingfield et al. 2018; Noakes, Peltonen, Rusko 2001; Tucker 2009)

## C05 Glossary

### [TEU-C05-001] Acclimation

The process of your body getting more efficient and adjusting to your environment over time. Applies to both altitude and heat exposure, representing the body's capability to maintain, survive, and thrive across a wide range of environmental conditions.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:acclimation sport:running
evidence: unsupported
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (heat)

### [TEU-C05-002] Active Recovery

Reducing pace to a light jog or walk for ten minutes at the end of a workout to maintain elevated circulation, assisting the body in transitioning back toward baseline conditions.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:active-recovery subtopic:cooldown sport:running
evidence: review (Van Hooren and Peake 2018); author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Postworkout Cooldown" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-003] Acute Training Load (ATL)

A seven-day rolling weighted average of daily training stress (calculated using TSS, rTSS, or hrTSS) representing short-term aggregate training load. Used as a snapshot of fatigue, where the highest ATL indicates the point of highest accumulated fatigue. The calculation time frame can be customized in TrainingPeaks.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:atl metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Aggregating Total Training Stress"

### [TEU-C05-004] Aerobic Deficiency Syndrome (ADS)

Phil Maffetone's term (Maffetone 2015) for a purportedly "deficient" aerobic system. The author states it is not found in any scientific literature and is a fictitious combination of words; contrasted with overtraining syndrome (underperformance syndrome), which is scientifically established.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:aerobic-deficiency-syndrome
evidence: unsupported
src: Ch 2 sidebar

### [TEU-C05-005] Amenorrhea

The absence of the menstrual cycle (the bleeding portion) for ninety days or more. Reported in up to 70 percent of women participating in sports compared to 2 to 5 percent in the general population. In endurance athletes, it should be treated as a warning sign ("canary in the coal mine") of low energy availability and RED-S.
tags: type:definition level:all condition:salud_hormonal subtopic:amenorrhea subtopic:female-athletes subtopic:red-s
evidence: study (Jacobsson et al. 2014; Nazem et al. 2012)
src: Ch 2 "RED-S"

### [TEU-C05-006] Anaerobic (as used by the author)

"Without oxygen" literally, but the author notes the term misleads: the body does not stop using oxygen; anaerobic processes supplement mitochondrial aerobic function when energy demand outstrips aerobic ATP production.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:anaerobic system:anaerobic
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Lactic Acid System"

### [TEU-C05-007] Associative focus / dissociative focus

Associative: athlete directs focus inward toward sensations and feelings presently experienced; uses physiological sensory feedback to regulate performance. Dissociative: athlete temporarily removes himself from the present situation and redirects thoughts to something unrelated to the task (from counting to planning an elaborate meal), occupying the mind with anything other than physiology. Origin: Morgan and Pollock 1977 found elite marathoners focused on internal cues (breathing rate, leg feel) more than non-elites, who distracted themselves with outside thoughts.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:associative-dissociative
evidence: study (Morgan and Pollock 1977)
src: Ch 15 "Associative and Dissociative Focus Strategies"

### [TEU-C05-008] Cardiac Drift

The gradual upward drift in heart rate during continuous endurance exercise even when pace, workload, or effort remains constant. Driven by blood plasma volume loss through sweating and redistribution of circulatory volume to the skin for convective and evaporative cooling, which forces the heart to beat faster to maintain oxygen delivery to working muscles.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:cardiac-drift metric:hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Cardiac Drift"

### [TEU-C05-009] Chronic Training Load (CTL)

A forty-two-day rolling weighted average of daily training stress (calculated using TSS, rTSS, or hrTSS) representing long-term aggregate training load. Used as a proxy for historical fitness, where peak CTL indicates peak fitness. The calculation time frame can be customized in TrainingPeaks.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:ctl metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Aggregating Total Training Stress"

### [TEU-C05-010] Crossover concept

The point at which you derive 50% of calories from fat and 50% from carbohydrate; ~65% of VO2max is quite common for trained endurance athletes (60-70% cited earlier). Not a switch.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:crossover-concept
src: Ch 13

### [TEU-C05-011] Erythropoiesis

The physiological process of producing red blood cells, stimulated by the hormone erythropoietin (EPO) released primarily by the kidneys (and liver to a smaller extent) in response to hypoxia. Takes at least one week and as many as two to three weeks for new red blood cells to mature and become fully functional.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:erythropoiesis subtopic:epo subtopic:altitude sport:running
evidence: research (Goldfarb-Rumyantzev and Alper 2014; Garvican-Lewis 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (altitude)

### [TEU-C05-012] Foundation / preparation / specialization (rejected labels)

Traditional periodization labels: foundation (build aerobic base, like a house foundation), preparation ("develops cardiovascular fitness, readying you for higher-level work"), specialization (higher intensities specific to the event). The author dislikes them: building an athlete is not like building a house with a fixed order, and an athlete is always "preparing". He instead names phases by the physiology emphasized: endurance (EnduranceRuns and SteadyStateRuns), lactate threshold (TempoRuns), or VO2 max (RunningIntervals). All aspects are always trained; the phase name reflects the primary adaptation sought.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:phase-naming
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Creating a Long-Range Plan"

### [TEU-C05-013] Functional Overreaching

A decrease in performance resulting from cumulative training stress over a period of weeks, which is reversed by taking adequate time for recovery. It is a normal and necessary part of the training process to induce positive adaptation.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:overreaching sport:running metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Consequences of Doing Too Much" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-014] Functional Threshold Pace

TrainingPeaks' terminology for the running pace corresponding to lactate threshold intensity. Defined as approximately the maximum pace that an average runner can sustain in an all-out effort for one hour (60 minutes).
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:ftp pace:metric system:lactate
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Total Training Stress"

### [TEU-C05-015] Gastric emptying

The rate at which the stomach releases its food/fluid slurry to the small intestine once the mixture has the right chemical balance; athletes want it rapid.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:gastric-emptying
src: Ch 13

### [TEU-C05-016] Glycogen window

First thirty to forty-five minutes after exercise, when muscle cells have more "gates" open to take in sugar and glycogen replenishment is fastest.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:glycogen-window
src: Ch 13

### [TEU-C05-017] Ground reaction forces (GRFs)

The force with which the ground pushes back, equal to the force of each foot strike. Level running vertical ~2.5–3 times body weight; walking peak ~1.2 times body weight.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:ground-reaction-force
src: Ch 5

### [TEU-C05-018] Heart Rate Variability (HRV)

The measure of inter-heartbeat variation over a given duration, quantified via the R-R interval between consecutive cardiac cycles on an ECG. Serves as a noninvasive proxy for autonomic nervous system homeostasis. Higher variability reflects healthy autonomic regulation; suppressed variability (more rigid timing) signals fatigue, overreaching, or stress.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:hrv metric:hrv
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Heart Rate Variability (HRV)"

### [TEU-C05-019] Hematocrit

The percentage of red blood cells—which contain hemoglobin, the oxygen-carrying component—per unit of blood.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:hematocrit subtopic:altitude sport:running
evidence: research (Beidleman et al. 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (altitude)

### [TEU-C05-020] Hemoconcentration

An acute physiological process occurring upon initial exposure to high altitude wherein plasma volume decreases by 10 to 25 percent, thereby artificially boosting the density of red blood cells and increasing hematocrit to temporarily improve oxygen transport before new red blood cells can be produced.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:hemoconcentration subtopic:altitude sport:running
evidence: research (Beidleman et al. 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (altitude)

### [TEU-C05-021] Hydration and natremic state terminology

- EUHYDRATED: normal hydration, typically less than 3 percent weight change.
- HYPOHYDRATED/DEHYDRATED: greater than 3 percent decrease in body weight.
- HYPERHYDRATED/OVERHYDRATED: increase in body weight.
- NORMONATREMIC: normal blood sodium (135 to <145 mmol/L).
- HYPONATREMIC: blood sodium <135 mmol/L.
- HYPERNATREMIC: blood sodium >145 mmol/L.
Hydration state is measured relative to body weight; natremic state by blood sodium concentration (mmol/L).
tags: type:definition type:threshold level:all sport:running focus:hydration metric:mmol-l subtopic:hyponatremia condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Balancing Hydration and Sodium" | no page markers

### [TEU-C05-022] Lactate threshold

The point where lactate production outstrips the body's ability to buffer/clear it and it begins to accumulate in the blood, marking a shift from predominantly aerobic energy to a greater anaerobic contribution. In short, the intensity an athlete can sustain for an extended period. Can fall at 65 to 85 percent of VO2 max depending on athlete.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:lactate-threshold system:threshold
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "The Lactic Acid System"

### [TEU-C05-023] Long-range plan (LRP)

A season-long snapshot of what the athlete should focus on at any point. Detail limited to weeks (not days), overarching intensities (RunningIntervals, TempoRun, SteadyStateRun) and misc components (amount of vertical, surface, heat/altitude adaptation). It does not specify days per week or specific interval combos (that is the short-range plan, next chapter). Purpose: ensure proper strategies through the year: when to peak volume and how much, when to include intensity, how race-and-recover cycles fit. Individual to each athlete's goals. Template: color-coded spreadsheet (workout types, higher/lower-volume periods, critical events, vertical gain/loss, trail vs road); blank template in the Appendix. Gives the athlete a heads-up on high volume/intensity periods and when to work on other items such as nutrition planning. Must be adapted as the season goes; think broad brushstrokes, not minutia; keep priorities straight ("know what to push and what to shove").
tags: type:definition type:methodology level:all subtopic:long-range-planning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Starting at the End", "A Closer Look at the LRP"

### [TEU-C05-024] MAC approach (mindfulness-acceptance-commitment) and mindfulness

Mindfulness (as presented): originates in ancient Buddhism; core belief that all perceptions from thoughts, feelings and the inner/outer world are impermanent; they come and go and we can alter how we react physically/psychologically. In sport used to focus attention, perceive pain, become more accepting, reduce anxiety and improve running economy (Bishop 2006; Brown and Ryan 2003; Brown, Ryan, Creswell 2007; De Petrillo 2009; Hill 2020). Neuroimaging shows it can alter brain reaction to pain (Grant et al. 2011) and body awareness (Hase 2015; Mehling 2009). MAC approach: multiphase mindfulness approach for athletes by Gardner and Moore (2004, 2015; Moore 2009): fundamentals first, then gradually increase difficulty by applying to real athletic scenarios.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:mindfulness
evidence: study
src: Ch 15 "Mindfulness"

### [TEU-C05-025] Menopause

The point at which ovaries permanently stop releasing eggs and a female experiences complete cessation of the menstrual cycle.
tags: type:definition level:masters condition:menopausia stage:postmenopause subtopic:menopause
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Menopause"

### [TEU-C05-026] Minimum maximum

The minimum amount of training time an athlete needs to be able to devote during their period of highest training volume. It sets a reference point for what can be expected on race day and helps determine whether the chosen race distance is reasonable:
- 50K and 50 miles: minimum maximum of 6 hours per week for 3 weeks, starting 6 weeks before the goal event.
- 100K and 100 miles: minimum maximum of 9 hours per week for 6 weeks, starting 9 weeks before the goal event.
tags: type:definition sport:running metric:volume level:all subtopic:minimum-maximum
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "The Time-Crunched Ultramarathoner"

### [TEU-C05-027] Neuromuscular fatigue

A diminished ability to voluntarily activate and/or contract muscles over the course of a long run, race, or other exhaustive exercise (as defined by Millet 2011), distinct from merely "feeling tired."
tags: type:definition level:all system:neuromuscular subtopic:fatigability subtopic:neuromuscular-fatigue sport:running
evidence: study (Millet 2011)
src: Ch 2 "Fatigability and Muscle Structure"

### [TEU-C05-028] Normalized Graded Pace (NGP) and Grade Adjusted Pace (GAP)

NGP (TrainingPeaks) and GAP (Strava) are algorithms that convert running on uphills and downhills to flat, level-equivalent pace, letting athletes compare paces across climbs and descents. NGP goes further: it also "normalizes" variable pacing to its physiological equivalent had the run been perfectly paced. Example: six sets of 3 min hard (6:00/mi) and 3 min easy (10:00/mi) averages just under 7:00/mi for 33 min, but is physiologically harder than 33 min at 7:00 pace; NGP reflects that.
Both compare climbing and flat running decently on similar surfaces and normal gradients.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:ngp-gap metric:pace
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6

### [TEU-C05-029] Oligomenorrhea

Menstrual cycles lasting longer than thirty-five days. A form of menstrual dysfunction linked to low energy availability and RED-S.
tags: type:definition level:all condition:salud_hormonal subtopic:oligomenorrhea subtopic:female-athletes subtopic:red-s
evidence: study (Jacobsson et al. 2014; Nazem et al. 2012)
src: Ch 2 "RED-S"

### [TEU-C05-030] Osmolality

The overall amount of stuff dissolved in a fluid (for a drink: the total molecular concentration of carbohydrate, electrolyte, flavoring, additives per unit volume). Water moves across a semipermeable membrane toward the higher-osmolality side.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:osmolality
src: Ch 13

### [TEU-C05-031] Overtraining Syndrome (OTS) / Under-Recovery

A state where cumulative fatigue progresses to a scenario where more than two weeks of complete rest are not enough to reverse extreme fatigue, disrupted sleep, irritability, and lack of desire to train. The author notes that training is not the root of the problem—recovery is—making the term "under-recovery" more accurate.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:overtraining-syndrome sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Consequences of Doing Too Much" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-032] Perimenopause

Also termed the "menopause transition": the stage when ovaries gradually produce less and less estrogen, typically starting between 36 and 45 years of age (on average four to five years before complete menopause). Characterized in its final one to two years by accelerated estrogen drops and symptoms including hot flashes, disrupted sleep, mood swings, increased subcutaneous/intramuscular fat, and performance decline.
tags: type:definition level:masters condition:menopausia stage:perimenopause subtopic:perimenopause subtopic:menopause
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Menopause"

### [TEU-C05-033] Preferred walking speed (PWS) and preferred transition speed (PTS)

PWS: the walking speed at the lowest point of the cost-of-transport curve (minimal oxygen consumption), ~1.5–1.6 m/s or about 16:45–18:00 min/mi. PTS: the speed at which people prefer to switch from walking to running, ~2 m/s (~13:30 min/mi), at which walking is still more economical than running. Cost of transport = the amount of oxygen it takes to go a given distance. Vertical speed = vertical gain per unit time.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:run-hike-transition
src: Ch 5

### [TEU-C05-034] Recovery

A broad term that encapsulates complete rest, restorative activities, and sometimes complex interventions, with the end goal of enhancing future performance in training and competition. It can be as simple and free as taking a nap, or extremely complicated and expensive; its effectiveness is often subjective and open to interpretation.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:recovery sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Goals of Recovery" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-035] Relative Effort

Strava's proprietary point-based training stress scoring system, inspired by Dr. Eric Bannister's TRIMP (Training Impulse) system (Bannister and Calvert 1980). It scores workouts using heart rate, duration of the run, and the time spent across different intensity zones relative to threshold heart rate.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:relative-effort metric:hr,load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Total Training Stress" | Table 6.2

### [TEU-C05-036] Respiratory Exchange Ratio (RER)

The ratio between the volume of carbon dioxide exhaled and the volume of oxygen inhaled (`VCO2 expired / VO2 inspired`). At rest or low activity levels, normal RER is 0.7 to 0.85, reflecting mixed fat and carbohydrate oxidation. As exercise intensity rises and glycolysis accelerates, RER approaches and can exceed 1.0 due to high CO2 production from carbohydrate breakdown.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:rer system:lactate
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "RPE and Breathing Rate"

### [TEU-C05-037] Running economy (as used in Ch 11)

The steady state rate of oxygen you consume and utilize at submaximal pace.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:running-economy
src: Ch 11 "Strength Training as an Ergogenic Aid"

### [TEU-C05-038] RunningStrides

Drills performed from a standing start focusing on a constant twenty-second acceleration. Prescribed during warm-ups (typically 4 to 8 repetitions of 20 seconds each) to develop the neuromuscular patterns for higher-speed running before the athlete possesses the fitness to sustain those speeds continuously, and to prime the neuromuscular system for hard interval sessions.
tags: type:definition sport:running metric:cadence level:all subtopic:running-strides
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Running Cadence and Stride Rate" | Ch 9 "The Right Warm-Up for Trail Running"

### [TEU-C05-039] Sleep Efficiency

Total sleep time divided by the time spent in bed. A key metric of sleep quality directly associated with immune resistance.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:sleep-efficiency metric:volume
evidence: clinical-trial (Cohen 2009)
src: Ch 8 "Steps for Better Sleep Habits" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-040] Step vs stride (and step length, step rate, stride length, stride frequency)

Step: one foot strike to the opposite foot strike, in length or time. Stride: one foot strike to the same foot striking again. Stride length = double step length (two steps = one stride). The author uses these terms correctly, unlike popular literature.
- Step length: distance between initial contact of one foot and initial contact of the opposite foot
- Step rate: total number of right and left foot strikes per minute
- Stride length: distance between initial contact of one foot and the next initial contact of the same foot
- Stride frequency: total number of same-foot strikes per minute (e.g., right foot strikes per minute)
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:step-stride
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | Figure 4.6

### [TEU-C05-041] Strength training vs. corrective exercise training

- "Strength training": training for strength; making muscles and tendons stronger so they produce more force, primarily by resistance training with progressively heavier loads to improve muscle strength, power, hypertrophy and/or endurance. It leaves you fatigued.
- "Corrective exercise training": focused on correcting an imbalance or movement pattern, mainly with lighter weights, resistance bands, body weight and unstable surfaces. Taxing to specific muscle groups but usually not fatiguing.
- Overlap exists; the distinction matters for programming.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-training subtopic:corrective-exercise
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | intro

### [TEU-C05-042] Supercompensation

A short-lived post-workout physiological phenomenon where, following an individual training session and adequate recovery, performance capacity temporarily increases above baseline. Proper training architecture sequences repeated training bouts to capitalize on this cycle.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:supercompensation sport:running metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Consequences of Doing Too Much" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-043] Training Stress Balance (TSB)

The difference between Chronic Training Load and Acute Training Load (`TSB = CTL - ATL`). Provides an indicator of readiness for performance, where the highest TSB values indicate that the athlete is freshest and most prepared to race.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:tsb metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Aggregating Total Training Stress"

### [TEU-C05-044] Training Stress Score (TSS)

A point-based scoring system used in TrainingPeaks to quantify the total training stress of a workout across different durations and intensities. Calibrated so that 60 minutes of running at lactate threshold (Functional Threshold Pace) equals 100 points. Exists in three intensity determinants:
- `TSS™`: derived from running power.
- `rTSS™`: derived from Normalized Graded Pace (NGP).
- `hrTSS™`: derived from threshold heart rate.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:tss metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Total Training Stress" | Table 6.2

### [TEU-C05-045] Unexplained Underperformance Syndrome (UUPS)

A modern diagnostic term that encourages athletes and coaches to look beyond the training plan when extreme underperformance occurs, considering all sources of life and physical stress and ruling out conditions such as RED-S, anemia, hypothyroidism, and acute or chronic illness (Lewis 2015).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:overtraining-syndrome sport:running
evidence: clinical-trial (Lewis 2015); author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Consequences of Doing Too Much" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C05-046] VO2 max

Maximum aerobic capacity: the point where the body delivers and utilizes as much oxygen as possible at absolute peak exercise intensity. Measured in liters of oxygen per minute; compared across athletes as mL/kg/min. Distinguish "pace at VO2 max" (pace run while utilizing max oxygen) from "absolute VO2 max" (L of oxygen utilized per minute).
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:vo2max system:vo2max
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "VO2 Max"

### [TEU-C05-047] Volume-at-intensity

The total accumulated time spent at a specific target physiological intensity during a workout or training period. Rather than attempting a single prolonged effort that cannot be sustained at the target intensity, intervals break the work into smaller bouts separated by recovery periods so the athlete accumulates a greater total volume at that specific intensity (e.g., six 10-minute intervals at lactate threshold pace to accumulate 60 minutes of volume-at-intensity).
tags: type:definition sport:running metric:volume level:all subtopic:volume-at-intensity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Volume"

### [TEU-C05-048] Workload-capped runner

A runner who has more available time to train, but for whom adding more running workload leads to injury.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:workload-capped
src: Ch 11 "A Place for Cross-Training"

### [TEU-C05-049] Workout terminology (CTS)

CTS uses endurance, tempo, steady state etc. with very specific definitions so coaches and athletes communicate precisely (durations for a "tempo run" among runners vary from 1 min to 2 hours). The five named workouts: RecoveryRun (RR), EnduranceRun (ER), SteadyStateRun (SSR), TempoRun (TR), RunningIntervals (RI). RunningStrides: 20-second accelerations from standing start.
tags: type:definition sport:running level:all subtopic:terminology
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Key Running Workouts"

### [TEU-C05-050] WUT diagram

Weight, Urine color, Thirst: a Venn diagram (Cheuvront and Sawka 2005) used first thing in the morning to judge likelihood of dehydration.
tags: type:definition level:all subtopic:wut-diagram
src: Ch 13

## C06 Quantitative Reference

### [TEU-C06-001] Figure 2.1 Energy system contributions during an all-out effort

Line graph (Y: % contribution to total energy supply 0–100; X: exercise duration in seconds, 0–300). Adapted from Gastin 2001. Values are read from the graph description, approximate:
- ATP-PCr: starts near 95% at 0 s, falls sharply over first 10–20 s, levels off toward 0% by 300 s.
- Glycolysis: 0% at 0 s, rises rapidly to a peak near 60% before 50 s, then declines steadily toward 15% at 300 s.
- Aerobic: 0% at 0 s, rises steadily to ~85% by 300 s.
Text companions: ATP-PCr dominates under ~8–10 s; glycolysis for efforts under ~2 minutes at full gas; beyond a minute or two of all-out effort nearly all energy is aerobic (per the ADS sidebar).
tags: type:mechanism type:zones level:all subtopic:energy-contribution system:aerobic system:anaerobic metric:power
evidence: study (Gastin 2001)
src: Ch 2 Figure 2.1

### [TEU-C06-002] Figure 2.2 Mean heart rate responses to 1-, 2-, 4- and 6-minute intervals

Line graphs, HR (bpm, axis 85–185) vs accumulated duration (min, 0–48); each protocol = 24 min of work at 1:1 work-rest. Values approximate from graph description. Adapted from Seiler and Sjusren [sic] 2004.
- 1-min: HR starts ~95; peaks rise from ~160 to ~175–180; troughs rise from ~105 to ~145 over ~38 min (one lower trough ~20 min).
- 2-min: peaks ~170 rising to ~180; troughs from <105 to ~125 over ~42 min.
- 4-min: 6 waves over ~40 min; peaks from ~172 to >180; troughs ~100 to ~120.
- 6-min: 4 waves over ~42 min; sustained plateau at peak; max HR ~173 rising to >180; troughs ~110–125.
tags: type:monitoring type:evidence level:all subtopic:intervals metric:hr system:vo2max
evidence: study (Seiler and Sjursen 2004)
src: Ch 2 Figure 2.2

### [TEU-C06-003] Figure 2.4 Fat oxidation rate by exercise intensity across sexes

Line graph plotting Fat Oxidation Rate (mg/kg Fat-free mass/min, axis 0.00 to 9.00) vs. Exercise Intensity (%VO2 max, axis 30 to 90%). Adapted from Venables, Achten, and Jeukendrup 2005.

Quantitative benchmarks:
- **Men:** Peak Maximal Fat Oxidation Rate occurs at ~45% VO2 max (peak rate ~6.0 mg/kg FFM/min on graph). Rate declines toward 0 as intensity approaches ~75–80% VO2 max.
- **Women:** Peak Maximal Fat Oxidation Rate occurs at ~52% VO2 max (peak rate ~8.5–9.0 mg/kg FFM/min on graph). Rate remains substantially higher across all submaximal intensities (30–80% VO2 max) and reaches zero at a higher intensity than in men (~85–90% VO2 max).
- **Sex difference summary:** Endurance-trained females demonstrate 24% to 56% higher fat oxidation rates than males even at rest (Melanson et al. 2002), utilize more fat as a percentage of total energy expenditure, and maintain lipid oxidation to higher relative exercise intensities.
tags: type:zones type:mechanism level:all sport:running system:fat-oxidation subtopic:fat-oxidation subtopic:female-athletes
evidence: study (Venables, Achten, and Jeukendrup 2005; Melanson et al. 2002)
src: Ch 2 Figure 2.4; "Substrate Utilization"

### [TEU-C06-004] VO2 max reference values (elite)

- Elite male long-distance runner (typical): about 77.4 ml/kg/min (Nevill et al. 2003).
- Successful elite male ultrarunners (Koop testing): approximately 60-70 ml/kg/min.
- Successful elite female ultrarunners: approximately 55-65 ml/kg/min.
- Untrained/novice improvement potential: well over 20 percent; elite: about 5 percent.
- Example of a value outside the club: 40 ml/kg/min has no realistic chance in elite distance running.
tags: type:threshold type:zones level:elite subtopic:vo2max system:vo2max sport:running
src: Ch 3 "Talent"
evidence: Nevill 2003; author-experience

| Vision problems | 2.1 | 3.6 |

| Other, not categorized | 11.7 | 26.6 |
Author's note: nausea, blisters, exhaustion and muscle pain top the list; inadequate training is only 13-15% of complaints.

### [TEU-C06-007] Figure 4.4 Friction of lubricants on skin over time (Nacht et al. 1981)

Line graph, y: percent change in friction coefficient vs baseline (0); x: hours after application (0* = immediately).
- Immediately after application: all lower friction: petroleum jelly lowest (below -20%), heavy mineral oil about -20%, glycerin between 0 and -10%.
- By 2-3 h all rise above baseline: glycerin exceeds 30% by hour 2; heavy mineral oil and petroleum jelly roughly 30-35% by hour 3.
- Hours 3-6: all remain +25% to +45%; heavy mineral oil dips at hour 4, peaks near 45% at hour 5, drops at hour 6; petroleum jelly plateaus about 35% (hours 4-6); glycerin 30-40%, about 40% at hour 6.
Values read from a chart description, approximate.
tags: type:evidence sport:running level:all subtopic:lubricants
evidence: study (Nacht et al. 1981)
src: Ch 4 | Figure 4.4

### [TEU-C06-008] Table 6.1 NGP and GAP for different grades and paces

Pace per mile on the grade shown converted to flat-equivalent pace (min/mi). NGP = TrainingPeaks Normalized Graded Pace; GAP = Strava Grade Adjusted Pace.

| GRADE | -10% NGP | -10% GAP | -6% NGP | -6% GAP | -2% NGP | -2% GAP | 0% NGP | 0% GAP | 2% NGP | 2% GAP | 6% NGP | 6% GAP | 10% NGP | 10% GAP |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8:00 | 15:18 | 11:27 | 11:13 | 10:05 | 8:51 | 8:40 | 8:00 | 8:00 | 7:19 | 7:22 | 6:14 | 6:16 | 5:26 | 5:21 |
| 10:00 | 19:07 | 14:19 | 14:00 | 12:37 | 11:04 | 10:50 | 10:00 | 10:00 | 9:09 | 9:13 | 7:48 | 7:50 | 6:47 | 6:42 |
