src: Ch 6 "Normalized Graded Pace and Grade Adjusted Pace" | Table 6.1

### [TEU-C06-009] Training stress scores: calibration and Tables 6.2 and 6.3

Training stress score lets different runs (hilly vs flat, intervals vs EnduranceRuns) be compared. TrainingPeaks calibration: a 60-minute run at lactate threshold (Functional Threshold Pace) = 100 points (about the maximum most people sustain; essentially an all-out time trial; elites sustain longer, beginners less). Recovery runs < 100; longer endurance runs typically > 100.

Table 6.2 Training stress scoring systems
| PLATFORM | NAME | INTENSITY DETERMINANT | METHOD OF SCORING |
|---|---|---|---|
| Strava | Relative Effort™ | Heart rate | Threshold heart rate, duration of run, time spent at different intensities |
| TrainingPeaks | TSS™ | Running Power | Threshold power, duration of run, NGP for run |
| TrainingPeaks | rTSS™ | NGP | Threshold pace, duration of run, NGP for run |
| TrainingPeaks | hrTSS™ | Heart rate | Threshold heart rate, duration of run, NGP for run |

Table 6.3 (printed title "Training stress scoring systems") example points
| TYPE OF RUN | TSS/rTSS/hrTSS POINTS |
|---|---|
| 60-min RecoveryRun | 50-80 |
| 90-min EnduranceRun with 3 x 10 min TempoRun | 100-150 |
| 90-min EnduranceRun with 6 x 3 min RunningIntervals | 100-150 |
| 2.5-hr EnduranceRun | 150-200 |
| 50-mile race | 400-600 |

Strava's Relative Effort was inspired by Bannister's TRIMP (Bannister and Calvert 1980); it uses heart rate and time at different intensities. Higher score = more stressful workout.
tags: type:zones sport:running level:all subtopic:tss-rtss metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Total Training Stress" | Tables 6.2, 6.3

### [TEU-C06-010] RPE scales: Borg 6-20 and 1-10 scale anchors

- Borg Scale 6-20 (6 = no exertion, 20 = maximum), used in the lab. Number × 10 ≈ actual HR (e.g., 16 → about 160 bpm); not true of all athletes but often accurate.
- Field scale 1-10 (1 = no exertion, 10 = maximum effort): endurance/"forever" pace = 5 or 6; challenging aerobic pace = 7; medium-intensity intervals approximating lactate threshold = about 8 or 9; high-intensity intervals (RunningIntervals) = only efforts reaching 10. Number × 10 seems to correlate closely with percentage of VO2 max being maintained.
- Recovery pace RPE 4 or 5 (breathing only a bit faster/deeper than brisk walk).
tags: type:zones sport:running level:all subtopic:rpe metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Why Ultrarunners Should Embrace Perceived Exertion"

### [TEU-C06-011] Table 6.4 RPE and the Talk Test

| RPE | ACTIVITY | TALKING ABILITY |
|---|---|---|
| 1–3 | Sitting on the couch | Uninhibited |
| 4–5 | Easy run | Story time! |
| 5–6 | Normal run | Comfortable conversation |
| 7–8 | Hard workout | 2–3 sentences at a time |
| 8–9 | Very hard workout | 5–7 words at a time |
| 9–10 | Extremely hard workout | Single word, probably four letters |

Notes: recovery pace = "story time" (tell a whole story without pausing). Endurance/"forever" pace: comfortable conversation but pauses. At or near lactate threshold: one sentence, maybe 5-7 words; anyone claiming threshold while chatting normally is mistaken. Near VO2 max: no talking. (Source labels table "Table 7.1" in text but prints 6.4.)
tags: type:zones sport:running level:all subtopic:talk-test metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 | Table 6.4

### [TEU-C06-012] Altitude exposure dose and thresholds

- Natural environment: chronic exposure should be above 5,900 ft (1,800 m) and below 9,840 ft (3,000 m). Nothing inherently negative above 9,840 ft, but no added hematological benefit and higher acute mountain sickness risk; the cap balances adaptation with safety.
- Dose: minimum 2 weeks, ideally up to 4 weeks, for maximum performance benefit (Constantini et al. 2017).
- One-week minimum if travelling to the A race: not for maximizing benefits, but to get past the 24-72 h worst-feeling period.
- Altitude tent: 3 to 4 weeks of exposure needed for hematological change; but the tent gives at most 8 to 10 h/day of hypoxia. Meaningful changes are hard to show for athletes living below 5,900 ft. For those living at ~5,500–6,500 ft, studies showed 9 h/night at simulated 8,200–9,840 ft produced positive adaptations (Carr et al. 2015; Garvican-Lewis et al. 2015).
- Coach's questions before recommending a tent: Can the athlete afford it? Does it reduce training quality via poorer sleep? At what elevation do they live? Can they reach the race at least 1 week prior? Verdict on tents "still very much out".
tags: type:dose level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:altitude-tent metric:volume sport:running
evidence: clinical-trial (Carr 2015; Garvican-Lewis 2015); review (Constantini 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Acclimation Protocol for Altitude"

### [TEU-C06-013] Table 8.1 AIS Sports Supplement Framework, Group A and Group B

Adapted from Sport Australia (ais.gov.au/nutrition/supplements), accessed September 2020. Categories C and D (everything else; performance-enhancing drugs and prohibited substances) were omitted by the author.

**GROUP A** – Evidence level: supported for use in specific situations in sport using evidence-based protocols
| Subcategory | Description | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Sports foods | Specialized products giving a convenient source of nutrients when it is impractical to consume everyday foods | Sports drink; sports gel; sports confectionery; sports bar; electrolyte supplement; isolated protein supplement; mixed macronutrient supplement (bar, powder, liquid meal) |
| Medical supplements | Used to prevent or treat clinical issues including diagnosed nutrient deficiencies. Best used with advice from an appropriate medical/nutrition practitioner | Iron supplement; calcium supplement; multivitamin supplement; vitamin D supplement; probiotics |
| Performance supplements | Supplements/ingredients that can support or achieve enhanced sports performance. Best used with an individualized, event-specific protocol and advice of an appropriate sports science/nutrition practitioner | Caffeine; B-alanine [sic, as printed]; bicarbonate; beetroot juice/nitrate; creatine; glycerol |

**GROUP B** – Evidence level: deserving of further research; could be considered under a research protocol or case-managed monitoring
| Subcategory | Description | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Food polyphenols | Food compounds with possible bioactivity incl. antioxidant and anti-inflammatory properties; food forms or isolated chemicals | Cherries, berries, and black currants; Quercitin [sic], ECGC [sic], epicatechins, and others |
| Other | Compounds of interest for potential benefits to body metabolism and function | Collagen support products; carnitine; HMB; ketone supplements; fish oils; phosphate; curcumin |
| Sick pack | Multi-supplement approach to a health or well-being issue. Best used with advice from an appropriate medical/nutrition practitioner | Zinc lozenges and Vitamin C |
| Amino acids | Constituents of protein which may have effects in isolation, or be taken to fortify a food/supplement lacking that amino acid | BCAA/Leucine; tyrosine |
| Antioxidants | Compounds protecting against oxidation or free-radical reactions; food forms or isolated chemicals | Vitamin C and E; N-acetyl cysteine |

### [TEU-C06-016] Race intensities in ultrarunning (sidebar: What intensity do I race at?)

Most common ultra race intensities are SteadyStateRun/EnduranceRun, with VO2 max intensity at the other, least-common end. Holds even for "shorter" ultras lasting four to six hours. Exceptions are rare: skyrunning races and 50K and 50-mile events for elite runners, who run shorter durations and can run at higher intensities for longer; they can run within 10 to 15 percent of their threshold intensity for much of the race.
tags: type:threshold sport:running level:elite subtopic:race-intensity metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 sidebar

### [TEU-C06-017] Taper models (Fig 12.7) and practical stance

Figure 12.7 (adapted from Mujika and Padilla 2003), percent of normal training over 14 taper days: linear taper (steady fall from >90% day 1 to 20-30% day 14); exponential slow decay (same start, ends 20-30%); exponential fast decay (ends ~10-15%); step taper (constant ~31%). Literature recommends a nonlinear fast-decay reduction. Example: fast decay puts Day 5 at 32.5% of daily volume (2-hour run = 39 min). The author considers this minutiae impractical: nothing magical about exactly 39 minutes; athletes are not machines; understand the science and apply it practically.
tags: type:periodization type:zones level:all phase:taper subtopic:taper-models sport:running
evidence: review (Mujika and Padilla 2003)
src: Ch 12 | Fig 12.7

### [TEU-C06-018] Figure 13.1 Fat and carbohydrate oxidation vs % VO2max (description)

Two line charts, x-axis % VO2max 30-90, with shaded zones RecoveryRun, EnduranceRun, SSR, TR, RI.
(A) g/min: Fat (left axis 0.0-0.6) starts ~0.4 g/min below 40%, peaks ~0.45 g/min around 50% VO2max, then declines toward 0.0 past 90%. Carbohydrate (right axis 0.0-4.0) starts ~0.2 g/min at 40%, rises steadily, crosses fat between 70% and 80% VO2max, reaches ~3.4 g/min around 90%.
(B) % of energy expenditure: Fat starts near 85% EE at 40% VO2max and falls to ~0% past 90%. Carbohydrate starts ~15% at 40% and rises to 100% at ~95%. Lines cross at 50% EE around 63% VO2max (within EnduranceRun zone).
Values are approximate readings of the chart.

### [TEU-C06-021] Table 13.1 Nutrition recommendations for ultrarunning in scientific literature

COSTA ET AL. 2019
- Carbohydrate: Upwards of 90 g/hr (2:1 glucose to fructose) although such a high level likely unnecessary.
- Fluid: Provided sufficient fluids/volumes are available, "drink to thirst," "ad libitum".
- Sodium: Avoid excessive sodium supplementation during running. Consume sodium based on food cravings. Do not use highly visible salt losses as a signal for increasing sodium intake.
- Other: Use trial and error with foods/fluid quality/quantity to determine what is optimal. Try to simulate race day (and know what foods are available at race) by eating similar foods/fluids in similar quantities in training. Use "B" or "C" races to determine how other factors (travel, weather, pacing, competition, stress, changes in normal food availability) influence optimal strategy. Consume slightly more early (first 2 hrs) as GI symptoms tend to develop later. In longer (greater than 8 hrs) races, avoid excess protein, fat, fiber, or FODMAP-heavy foods; can mouth rinse with carbohydrate beverage in longer events when consuming enough becomes an issue.

TILLER ET AL. 2019
- Carbohydrate: 30-50 g/hr.
- Fluid: 450-750 ml/hr (by drinking every 20 min), greater in hot and humid conditions.
- Sodium: > 575 mg/L.
- Other, in training: Individualized, periodized, food-first approach. Moderate-to-high carbohydrate diet (~60% of energy intake, 5-8 g/kg/d) to limit chronic glycogen depletion. Limit carbohydrate before occasional easy sessions and/or moderating daily carbohydrate intake may enhance fat oxidative capacity; this may compromise high-intensity efforts; if done, implement with sufficient time to permit adaptations. Protein ~1.6 g/kg/d up to 2.5 g/kg/d may be warranted during demanding training.
- Other, in racing: 5-10 g/hr of protein. Eat (carbohydrate and protein) from variety of sources, more savory foods in longer races. Use progressive gut training and/or low-FODMAP diets to minimize GI distress. Ketogenic diets and/or ketone esters to improve ultramarathon performance are not currently evidence based, but further research needed. Strategically use caffeine in latter stages, particularly with sleep deprivation. [Table text garbled at end ("d for") — recorded as printed]

ACSM POSITION STATEMENT 2016
- Carbohydrate: Up to 90 g/hr while exercising (ultra specific), 6-10 g/kg/d (for endurance athletes, not ultra specific).
- Fluid: Drink 5-10 ml/kg in the 2-4 hrs before exercise (pale yellow urine color). Drink enough during to limit the day's weight loss to < 2% of body weight. Drink 1.25-1.5 L for every kg of weight lost after; none of these are specific for ultra-endurance.
- Sodium: Keep blood sodium above 135 mmol/L; doesn't say how to achieve it, nor is it ultra specific.
- Other: Nitrates improve exercise tolerance, economy, and performance in at least non-elite athletes.

### [TEU-C06-023] Table 14.1 Carbohydrate content of different dietary strategies

| DIETARY STRATEGY | CARBOHYDRATE CONTENT |
| Very low-carbohydrate ketogenic diet | <50g carbohydrate/day |
| Low-carbohydrate diet | 15–30% of calories from carbohydrate |
| High-carbohydrate diet | 60–65% of calories from carbohydrate |

Adapted from Burke 2020; Tiller et al. 2019; Wylie-Rosette 2016.
Note: the text describes LCHF as 30 to 40 percent of calories from carbohydrate, differing from the table's 15–30% for low-carbohydrate; recorded as printed.
Ketogenic diet per text: 75–85 percent calories from fat, 15–20 percent from protein, carbohydrate under 50 g/day (equivalent to two to three bananas or two energy gels). Most sports science recommends 60–65 percent of an endurance athlete's calories from carbohydrate (Tiller et al. 2019).

### [TEU-C06-024] Table 14.4 How hydration and natremic states converge (tic-tac-toe grid)

Rows = hydration state; columns = natremic state.
| Hydration | Low (Hyponatremic) | Normal (Normonatremic) | High (Hypernatremic) |
| Low: Dehydrated | Dehydrated and hyponatremic | Dehydrated and normonatremic | Dehydrated and hypernatremic |
| Normal: Euhydrated | Euhydrated and hyponatremic | Euhydrated and normonatremic (center square, outlined) | Euhydrated and hypernatremic |
| High: Overhydrated | Overhydrated and hyponatremic | Overhydrated and normonatremic | Overhydrated and hypernatremic |
Center square = desired state: run faster, farther, tolerate heat better. Goal: stay in or return to it. No scenario beats euhydrated + normonatremic. Prevalence categories: extremely rare <1%, rare 3–6%, somewhat common 10–15%, common ≥30% (adapted from Hoffman, Hew-Butler, Stuempfle 2013).
Hyponatremia can also occur euhydrated: normal weight (<3% loss) but sodium not replaced against long sweat losses (dilution by sodium removal, not by excess water). Media-famous case: overhydrated + hyponatremic slow marathoner drinking too much plain water.

evidence: author-experience; Hoffman, Hew-Butler & Stuempfle 2013

Line chart, 2001–2018, y-axis percentage of athletes (0–45%). Values approximate, read from the chart description:
- Ultra: about 22% in 2001; slight fluctuation 2003–2008; significant rise 2008–2009; slight dip 2011; steady climb to about 40% by 2016–2018, the highest category at the end.
- 5K: about 24% in 2001; about 28–29% by 2007–2008; slight dip 2009–2010; about 31% by 2015; stable through 2018.
- Half marathon: under 10% in 2001; peak about 25% in 2014; declines to about 19% by 2018.
- Marathon: about 5% in 2001; peak about 16–17% in 2009; 13–15% through 2015; about 14% by 2018.
- 10K: about 3% in 2001; about 11% in 2004; about 9–11% through 2013; peak about 15% in 2017; slight decline in 2018.

### [TEU-C06-026] Optimal race frequency per twelve months (by distance)

For athletes whose racing values are rooted in optimal performance (toeing the line in the best shape possible). Ranges assume you race only at the specified distance within a twelve-month period:
- 100-mile: one or two times per twelve months
- 100K and 50-mile: two or three times per twelve months
- 50K: three or four times per twelve months
Example from the author: to maximize performance at 100 miles, do it one or two times per year if that is the only distance raced.

## C07 Testing & Assessment

### [TEU-C07-001] Using breathing to find lactate threshold (ventilatory cue)

- Progression: RPE 5-6 breathing deep, rhythmic, faster than recovery but not labored; RPE 7 (top of aerobic range) deep but starting to labor; RPE 8-9 lactate threshold.
- Easiest way to find threshold is to go beyond it: above threshold breathing shifts from deep, labored, controlled to short and rapid. The lactate threshold interval intensity is just below the point where breathing shifts to short and rapid. For RunningIntervals (RPE 10) exceed this ventilatory threshold: short, rapid breathing.
- After an interval, breathing should come down to the RPE 4-5 level in recovery, except in high-intensity interval workouts where recoveries are purposely too short for full recovery.
- Gives a good, not perfect, indication.
tags: type:test sport:running level:all subtopic:ventilatory-threshold system:lactate metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "RPE and Breathing Rate", "RPE and the Talk Test"

### [TEU-C07-002] Lactate threshold and VO2 max lab testing: author's use and finding

- Author has put most elite athletes and many runners through lab LT and VO2 max testing; value lies in the continuum of intensities, not one end point. Used as benchmarks/baseline and progress profile; NOT used to create training intensity ranges for ultrarunners (unlike other endurance athletes).
- Finding: cyclist LT power ≈ 78% of power at VO2 max; elite cyclist ≈ 85%; most marathoners similar; elite ultrarunners often record LT at 95 to 97 percent of VO2 max.
- Interpretation: ultrarunners have traditionally trained VO2 max poorly and lower intensities well; this shows where they are underdeveloped. VO2 max is the ceiling of the building: "raise the roof" to fit more endurance and performance capacity.
tags: type:test sport:running level:advanced system:vo2max system:lactate subtopic:lab-testing
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 sidebar "What About Lactate Threshold and VO2 Max Testing?"

### [TEU-C07-003] Figure 13.5 The Sweat Test

1. Weigh yourself nude right before a run.
2. Do a one-hour run at EnduranceRun intensity.
3. After the run, strip down, wipe off sweat, weigh nude again.
4. Subtract end weight from beginning weight; convert to ounces (1 lb = 16 oz). This is your hourly sweat rate for those conditions.
5. Aim to replace ~95-98% (not 100%) of those fluids during a race in those conditions. Reason: in an ultra, weight loss from water stored with fat and carbohydrate (metabolic water) is significant and need not be replaced; exactly how much to replace is not known, only that it isn't 100%. Replacing 100% of sweat loss in an ultra can lead to hyponatremia.
6. Repeat in different conditions, using steps of 10 degrees Fahrenheit.
Use: map your replacement rate against race conditions using an hour-by-hour weather app.
tags: type:test type:protocol level:all focus:hydration metric:load subtopic:sweat-rate sport:running
src: Ch 13 | Figure 13.5
evidence: author-experience

## C08 Methodology & Progression

### [TEU-C08-001] Interval design: duration and work-rest ratio for VO2 max

Pop quiz: three sets with equal total work, A. 12 x 1 min maximum with 1 min recovery; B. 4 x 3 min maximum with 3 min recovery; C. 2 x 6 min maximum with 6 min recovery. Answer: B (4 x 3 minutes).
- A is too short to reach VO2 max and stay there long enough. C is so long most athletes pace at too low an intensity to train VO2 max optimally.
- Effective intervals are long enough to stress the desired physiology and separated by recovery that lets the effort be repeated; ineffective ones are too short/long with recoveries too short/long.
- Purpose: maximize exposure to intensity over time. For VO2 max, intervals must be long enough to reach and sustain effort above 90 percent of VO2 max (oxygen consumption), which takes 60 to 90 seconds.
- Study (Seiler and Sjursen 2004; Figure 2.2): 1-, 2-, 4- and 6-minute intervals with 1:1 work-rest, each 24 minutes of interval work. Highest oxygen consumption was during 4-minute intervals. In practice, intervals of 2 to 4 minutes are long enough to ramp to VO2 max intensity and hold it; in 6-minute intervals athletes pace themselves and do not reach as high an intensity.
- Work-rest ratio: for VO2 max intervals of 2 to 4 minutes, 1:1 works best (maximizes recovery without extending the workout and compromising later intensity). As intervals lengthen and intensity drops, less recovery relative to interval is needed: lactate threshold intervals (TempoRun) 2:1, even greater for high-end aerobic intervals (SteadyStateRun). See Chapter 9.
tags: type:methodology type:dose level:all subtopic:intervals subtopic:work-rest-ratio system:vo2max metric:hr
evidence: study (Seiler and Sjursen 2004)
src: Ch 2 sidebar "How Long Should Intervals Be?"

### [TEU-C08-002] Principle 2: Progression

- Continued gains require training to move progressively forward. Time and intensity are the two main variables to raise workload (more hours = volume; higher intensity), in near limitless combinations, producing a stimulus great enough to adapt heart, muscles, vessels, bones, gut, connective tissue. Adaptations are not static; a stronger system needs a bigger workload to overload.
- Common novice marathon architecture: weekly progression with periodic recovery weeks (e.g., 30-min Tempo, then 40, then 45). Flaws: (1) cardiovascular/endurance adaptations take weeks, not days, so little meaningful improvement between weeks 1, 2, 3; (2) the hardest Tempo (45 min) lands in the week of most cumulative fatigue ("worst of both worlds"); (3) the easiest hard workout happens when freshest (week one after recovery week).
- Author's preference: volume and/or volume of intensity generally DECREASE through a phase, then increase (progress) after a recovery week. Example: 45-minute Tempo in week one, 40-minute week two, 30-minute week three (most cumulative fatigue). Progression is viewed over a few or several weeks (see Chapter 12).
- Limits: professionals are nearly maxed out in annual hours. Some case studies show adding low-intensity volume continues to help highly trained/elite athletes (Solli et al. 2017); others suggest for already-trained athletes more volume may not improve VO2 max, power at lactate threshold or mitochondrial density (Laursen et al. 2002).
- Amateurs have limited time, so to progress within available weekly hours manipulate all tools at some point: overall training time, types of intensity, and volume of intensity.
tags: type:principle type:methodology level:all subtopic:progression subtopic:tempo-run subtopic:recovery-week metric:volume metric:load
evidence: study (Solli et al. 2017; Laursen et al. 2002)
src: Ch 2 "Progression"

### [TEU-C08-003] Sequence of training: progression is not 'faster'

- Progression is often confused with moving to higher and higher intensities across the year (aerobic base, then higher intensities, then race-specific intervals), the norm for 5K, 10K, marathon.
- For ultrarunners this low-to-medium-to-high order is a stereotypical and flawed way to organize training. Progression should occur within each intensity (system gets stronger and needs bigger volume/intensity stimulus). Training should move from developing the LEAST specific aspects of ultramarathon physiology to the MOST specific (see Chapter 10).
tags: type:methodology type:principle level:all subtopic:sequence-of-training subtopic:specificity sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 sidebar "Sequence of Training"

### [TEU-C08-004] Training volume metric: hours per week versus miles per week

Koop specifies that throughout the book, training volume is referred to in terms of hours per week as opposed to miles per week.
Reasoning: A time-based method of capturing volume is far more consistent compared to a mile-based method for trail and ultrarunners. The time and effort it takes to run a single mile varies wildly depending on whether the runner is going uphill, downhill, or traversing technical terrain.
tags: type:methodology type:principle level:all subtopic:volume-metric sport:running metric:volume
src: Ch 3 intro | Note on training volume
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C08-005] Training for the four disciplines: matching course grade and hiking

- Determine the event's average elevation change per mile (see formula entry). Recreate it over a week of training, particularly on any non-recovery run and in the last several weeks before the event (see Chapter 10).
- Matching the weekly average is described as being on the right path.
- Refinements: break the course into major climbs and descents, find their average grades, and look for analogous local trails. If you can anticipate how much you will power-hike (from climb steepness and anticipated pace), include that percentage of power-hiking in training.
- Not everyone lives near the course. Background research plus simple math lets you tailor training.
- Workout nomenclature (CTS): TempoRuns (TR), SteadyStateRuns (SSR) and RunningIntervals (RI) can be done at a variety of grades depending on the goal of the training phase (see Chapter 9).
- Early season fitness-building: do these intervals uphill, to maximize aerobic benefit.
- Later, during event-specific training: use a mixture of uphills, downhills and flats, particularly for TR and SSR intensity.
- RunningIntervals: almost always best on flat or uphill ground. It is hard to reach the required intensity running downhill, and doing so carries high injury risk.
- Flat running, uphill running, power-hiking and some downhill running can all be done as specific workouts.
- Keep it practical: the match is not meant to be perfect.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all phase:base phase:build subtopic:specificity subtopic:uphill-intervals
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "How to Train for the Four Disciplines"

### [TEU-C08-006] The coach's tracking system (five-part)

1. Use TrainingPeaks to schedule workouts (short-range plan, Ch 12).
2. Use NGP, rTSS and Strava segments to evaluate and compare workouts.
3. Use rTSS/CTL/ATL to track training load.
4. Use subjective feedback from the athlete to gauge fitness, fatigue, motivation.
5. Use knowledge and experience to synthesize the information and drive action.
Key points: choose the most accurate recording settings for intervals; know TSS limits; RPE calibrates intensity in workouts and races.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:tracking-system
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "What the Coach Uses"; Key Points

### [TEU-C08-007] Altitude acclimation protocols (Table 7.1)

Two primary contenders: "live high, train high" and "live high, train low". Research consensus: "live high, train low" gives the greatest hematologic and performance benefits (Constantini, Wilhite, and Chapman 2017), since it combines long altitude exposure (day and night) with higher-quality workouts at lower elevation. "Live high, train high/train low" (low-intensity high, high-intensity low) is the ideal if available; the book's key points call it the most efficacious.

TABLE 7.1 Different altitude training protocols
| | LIVE HIGH, TRAIN HIGH | LIVE HIGH, TRAIN LOW | LIVE HIGH, TRAIN HIGH / LOW |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Living | Living between 6,840–8,050 ft | Living between 6,840–8,050 ft | Living between 6,840–8,050 ft |
| Training | Training at or above 6,000 ft | Training below 4,100 ft | Train at low intensities between 6,840–8,050 ft, and high intensity workouts below 4,100 ft* |
| Duration of Protocol | ~21–28 days | ~21–28 days | ~21–28 days |

*Living between 6,840–8,050 ft is optimal for hematological responses while not impairing recovery and sleep. Moving high-intensity runs below 4,100 ft preserves oxygen delivery to working muscles, maintaining quality of higher-intensity sessions.

Sites allowing convenient high-living/low-training: Park City UT; Flagstaff AZ; Mammoth Lakes CA; Big Bear Lake CA; Lake Tahoe NV/CA; Cloudcroft NM; Sierra Nevada, Spain.
tags: type:protocol level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:live-high-train-low sport:running
evidence: meta-analysis/review (Constantini, Wilhite, and Chapman 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Acclimation Protocol for Altitude" | Table 7.1

### [TEU-C08-008] Heat acclimation methods and hierarchy (Figures 7.6, 7.7)

Common methods (Daanen, Racinais, Périard 2017): natural heat acclimation (exercising in hot ambient conditions), controlled hyperthermia (overdressing), controlled intensity (exercise in climatic chamber), passive heating (post-exercise sauna or hot-water immersion). All work; target core temperature ~101°F (38.5°C), held about 90 minutes. Studies since the 1960s (50 to 100 min daily, twice daily, 5 to 27 days): results more or less the same, all work, more is not better.

FIGURE 7.6 methods
| METHOD | ACTIVITY EXAMPLE | WORK RATE | CONDITIONS | DURATION |
| Self-paced exercise | Football, or running | Variable, or self-selected | Variable outdoor, or indoor 40°C/104°F, 40% relative humidity | 60–90 min |
| Constant work-rate exercise | Cycling ergometer, or marching | 60% VO₂ max (185 W) or 6 km/h | Indoor 40°C/104°F, 40% RH, or variable outdoor | 60–90 min |
| Passive heating | Water immersion, or sauna bathing | N/A | Water 40–42°C/104–107.6°F or sauna 70–90°C/158–194°F | 45–60 min, or intermittent for 30 min |
| Controlled hyperthermia | Resting and/or exercising to maintain core temperature at 38.5°C | Variable | 40°C/104°F, 40% RH | 60–90 min |
| Controlled intensity | Cycle ergometer | 65% VO₂ max heart rate (145 bpm) | 40°C/104°F, 40% RH | 60–90 min |
(Natural/outdoor: self-paced and constant work-rate; artificial/lab: all five.)

FIGURE 7.7 hierarchy: Passive heating: dry sauna > hot water immersion; wet sauna not equal to hot-water immersion. Passive > active (climatic chamber > overdressing).

Reasoning: best protocol "Goldilocks" = the one preserving highest-quality training with smallest dose. Active heating (overdressing, chamber) likely sacrifices volume and intensity. Sauna, steam room or hot tub 60–90 min (broken up as needed) adds time and stress, but passive heating immediately post-exercise ranks top: workout quality is kept and less time in heat is needed because you enter pre-warmed.
tags: type:zones level:all subtopic:heat-acclimation subtopic:passive-heating sport:running metric:hr
evidence: review (Daanen, Racinais, Périard 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Acclimation Methods", "Priority of Methods" | Fig 7.6, 7.7

### [TEU-C08-009] The five workout components

Five variables govern every workout; changing one changes its goal:
1. Intensity
2. Volume
3. Frequency and repetition
4. Environment (terrain, surface, amount of vertical relief)
5. Running cadence and stride rate
Example: two ten-minute intervals target different physiology by pace alone: high end of aerobic intensity for aerobic endurance, or lactate threshold pace to improve producing and processing lactate. Changing environment (uphill vs flat) also changes purpose.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:workout-components
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "The Five Workout Components"

### [TEU-C08-010] Intensity measurement in trail running

- Intensity = how hard you work; a workout's impact is directly related to it.
- Measuring is difficult in trail running: no power meters (cycling), pace is unreliable (road running). Trail runners can at best observe the body's response as RPE. Heart rate is also problematic (see Ch 6).
- Power or normalized pace in running may come, but until then RPE is the best tool.
tags: type:principle sport:running metric:rpe level:all subtopic:intensity-measurement
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Intensity"

### [TEU-C08-011] Volume and volume-at-intensity

- Volume = total exercise in a workout, week, month, year or career.
- Key concept: volume-at-intensity. Intervals break hard efforts into smaller bites so you accumulate more time at a specific intensity than one continuous effort allows.
- Example: goal of 60 min at lactate threshold but you can sustain only 40 min: do six 10-min intervals separated by recovery. Two or three such workouts per week accumulate up to 180 min of volume-at-intensity.
- Quantify volume by time, not miles, in ultra/trail running: a five-mile run can take one, two or three hours depending on terrain.
tags: type:principle sport:running metric:volume level:all subtopic:volume-at-intensity subtopic:time-based-volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Volume"

### [TEU-C08-012] Frequency and repetition (worked example)

- Frequency = times a workout is performed in a training period; repetition = times an exercise is repeated in a session. They ensure session quality.
- Intervals work only at the prescribed intensity, volume, frequency and repetition for current fitness; the correct combination matters, not dominance of one variable.
- Example: LT pace 9:00 min/mi sustainable 40 min. You might run 8:30 pace for 3 min (closer to VO2max pace). A single 20-min RunningInterval is pointless: pace would collapse after the first 3-5 min and no longer be VO2max-type intensity (still hard, wrong target). Instead do seven 3-min RunningIntervals at 8:30 min/mi with 3 min easy recovery = 21 min at 8:30 pace.
- Frequency adds workload: two such workouts in a week = 42 min at 8:30 pace.
- The harder the intervals, the more recovery needed before another high-quality session; space workouts for adequate recovery.
tags: type:principle sport:running metric:load level:all subtopic:frequency subtopic:repetition
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Frequency and Repetition"

### [TEU-C08-013] Environment: terrain, surface, treadmill

- Uphill intervals increase workload; author prefers certain intervals, especially RunningIntervals, uphill to maximize aerobic benefit and race specificity (esp. hilly goal races). Uphill also hedges against injury and helps flagging motivation (hill adds resistance and a visible challenge).
- Surface changes pace and stress on the body. Ultras mix trail of varying technicality, dirt roads, some pavement. Training also prepares musculature, joints, bones and connective tissue, not just cardiovascular fitness.
- Treadmill: suspension gives less impact than outdoor running, even less than some trails; useful with foot/knee history to run more with less pain. Convenient for intervals: program paces and durations, it holds pace steady, helps those who struggle to self-motivate. You still must find motivation for race day, but using the treadmill is OK. Incline can be set for any workout (intervals, EnduranceRuns, RecoveryRuns).
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:terrain subtopic:treadmill subtopic:specificity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Environment"

### [TEU-C08-014] Running cadence and stride rate

- Cavanaugh and Williams (1982): runners naturally gravitate to the most economical stride length (oxygen consumption); other economy studies agree. Once reasonably proficient, biomechanics settle into a personal sweet spot.
- For ultrarunners it is still useful to develop the ability to run at different stride lengths/rates, because changing stride and foot strike changes how the body is stressed.
- A faster rate with shorter stride (even if not natural) changes stress on leg muscles and ligaments; useful for holding solid pace early while sparing the legs for harder terrain later.
- Drills: RunningStrides = 20-second drills from a standing start, focused on a constant 20-second acceleration. In a warm-up they develop the neuromuscular pattern for higher-speed running before the fitness to sustain it, and prepare the body for hard interval work.
tags: type:principle sport:running metric:cadence level:all subtopic:stride-rate subtopic:running-strides
evidence: study (Cavanaugh and Williams 1982)
src: Ch 9 "Running Cadence and Stride Rate"

### [TEU-C08-015] Why workout construction matters

- The critical element of efficacy is time at intensity. For SSR, TR and RI the coaches determined how much exposure elicits each adaptation.
- Too much time at intensity: the intended intensity drops. Too little: not enough exposure for adaptation.
- Tampering with a workout's structure risks missing the desired adaptation.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:time-at-intensity
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "Why Workout Construction Matters"

### [TEU-C08-016] Warm-up and cooldown for trail running

- Fifteen-minute warm-up/cooldown is track-derived and arbitrary; impractical on trails, where specificity of intervals (intensity plus suitable terrain) matters more and reaching the right trail may take 17, 19 or 22 min.
- Prescription: the EnduranceRun duration defines total workout time and intervals are done within it. Example: 2-hr ER with 3 x 10-min TempoRuns and 5 min recovery = run 2 hours total, complete three 10-min TR efforts separated by 5 min at RecoveryRun pace.
- Parameters: warm-up 10–30 min; cooldown at least 10 min. Often 4–8 RunningStrides of 20 seconds each after the warm-up, before intervals.
- Figure 9.2: warm-up 15–30 min; workout 6 x 3 min RI with 3 min recovery; cooldown/EnduranceRun; 2 hours total.
- Warm-up includes easy running, dynamic stretching and strides.
tags: type:protocol sport:running level:all subtopic:warm-up subtopic:cooldown
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "The Right Warm-Up for Trail Running" Fig 9.2

### [TEU-C08-017] Choosing your event and bringing your people into the loop

- Organizing training starts with one question: "What events deeply resonate with me?" You cannot control lottery outcomes, but you can control which events you focus on.
- Events differ in management/support level, speed ("fast"/"slow"), terrain and environment (alpine, desert). Choose events you have a deeper connection with (difficulty, history in the region, flora/fauna), because this enhances emotional engagement with the training process. The race must be meaningful emotionally, not a box to check.
- After choosing, bring in the people you live and run with (family, friends, colleagues, running groups). Rarely does one run an ultra without support; they may crew and pace on race day. Tell them what you are training for and your goals. A peer group helps even if they never run with you.
tags: type:methodology level:all subtopic:event-selection subtopic:support-network
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Choosing Your Event"

### [TEU-C08-018] Mixed-intensity vs block periodization and the author's preference

- College example (mixed-intensity micro-periodization cycle, repeated ~9 months through cross-country, indoor and outdoor seasons): Mon rest; Tue 5 x 1000 m repeats (RunningIntervals); Wed 75-min EnduranceRun; Thu 6-mile TempoRun; Fri 60-min RecoveryRun; Sat 8K cross-country or 5K track race; Sun 90-min EnduranceRun. Minor wrinkles only (8-mile tempo, 400s instead of 1000s).
- Mixed-intensity = key workouts in a week are at a variety of intensities. Works well for college-aged athletes (early in training arc, young physiology adapts to anything). Passed down to marathon/ultra training.
- Block structure: each month or phase focuses on one type of intensity. Cyclists have leaned on blocks for ~20 years; triathletes use a hybrid. All endurance sports share the same requirement (robust cardiovascular engine), so structure should logically be universal.
- Arguments: mixed advocates = "use it or lose it" (not stressing e.g. VO2 max for a few weeks loses fitness there). Block advocates = concentrating stress in one area yields greater adaptation.
- Research slightly favors block in lab and field for cycling, kayaking, cross-country skiing (García-Pallarés 2010; Mølmen et al. 2019), with biological support (Goutianos 2016; Issurin 2018); extrapolation to ultrarunners remains debated.
- Author's choice: favors block-style structure for the rest of the book. Experientially superior for well-trained, experienced athletes (nearly every ultrarunner). Most ultras are run in a very narrow intensity range, so maximize development across that range even at the expense of other physiology. The "lose it" criticism is acceptable because what is lost is not deterministic in ultrarunning, provided the LRP is structured correctly.
- Figure 10.1 (mixed example week): Mon rest; Tue 15 min warm-up, 5 x 3 min RI, 3 min RBI, 15 min cooldown; Wed 45 min RecoveryRun; Thu 15 min warm-up, 30 min TempoRun, 15 min cooldown; Fri rest; Sat 60 min EnduranceRun; Sun 60 min EnduranceRun. (RBI = rest between intervals)
- Figure 10.2 (block example week): same as 10.1 but Thursday is also 15 min warm-up, 5 x 3 min RI, 3 min RBI, 15 min cooldown (similar intensity twice in the week).
tags: type:periodization type:methodology sport:running level:advanced subtopic:block-periodization subtopic:mixed-intensity
evidence: meta-analysis (García-Pallarés 2010; Mølmen et al. 2019; Goutianos 2016; Issurin 2018) / author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Strategies for Long-Range Planning" | Figs 10.1, 10.2

### [TEU-C08-019] LRP Principle 1: specificity close to the event (Hardrock, Western States examples)

- Analyze event demands from cardiovascular physiology down to trail/road surface, aid-station spacing, environmental conditions and coping with eating/drinking stress. If an aspect is highly specific, develop it close to the event; least specific aspects as far away as logistically possible.
- Hardrock 100: almost nobody runs the uphills; even the fastest hike most uphill and run descents. So athletes do most hiking training in the several weeks before the event. Least specific: velocity at VO2 max (least important, but NOT unimportant), so many Hardrockers do flat, fast, high-intensity running very early in the season.
- Recon of a course in the weeks before the event applies the principle correctly (grades, surface, conditions).
- Western States 100 example: typically >15 hours for the best athletes; temperatures commonly exceed 100°F mid-course; 400-plus feet of elevation change per mile. Most specific physiology: running at relatively low intensity (EnduranceRun for this athlete), heat tolerance, coping with climbing and descending. VO2 max RunningIntervals are least specific (very little chance of running near that intensity).
- Application: SteadyStateRuns and EnduranceRuns closest to the race, RunningIntervals furthest away; heat acclimation and race-specific nutrition strategies closest to the race. Lacking mountains, the athlete used the Western States training camp, Silver State 50 as a training race, and 100–200 ft local climbs; this targeting of intensity, heat and terrain in the last eight weeks optimally prepared her.
tags: type:principle type:methodology sport:running level:advanced subtopic:specificity subtopic:hiking subtopic:heat-acclimation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Principle 1 and "Applying the Three Principles"

### [TEU-C08-020] Training phase design: lengths and construction steps

- Generally each phase ≈ 8 weeks (the example LRP fits this except the intentionally shortened early RunningInterval phase). In reality athletes switch intensity every 4 to 12 weeks, based on intensity, significance and rate of adaptation.
- Steps: (1) Identify the last phase: the intensity most specific to the race and/or your strength(s); make this final training two phases of four weeks each. (2) Plan the first phase with the least important intensity and/or the weakness to work on; two phases of four weeks each. (3) Fill the middle with the intensity left over. (4) Adjust each phase's length per intensity, significance and adaptation rate. (5) Add nuance for nutrition training, heat/altitude acclimation, training camps.
- Key points version: start with eight-week blocks, divided in half with a recovery week every fourth week, one intensity at a time.
- Intensity of the phase: higher-intensity phases can be shorter (VO2 max phase as short as 2–3 weeks); an endurance phase with SteadyStateRuns and EnduranceRuns commonly lasts 12 weeks, with rest at appropriate times. High-intensity physiology adapts over a shorter time course.
- Significance: more critical aspects are developed closer to the race and longer (typically eight weeks or more); less important for a shorter period (eight weeks or less) and further from the event.
- Rule summary: shorten a block if intensity is high or less important; lengthen if intensity is low or more important.
tags: type:periodization type:decision-rule sport:running level:advanced phase:base phase:build subtopic:phase-length
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Training Phases"; Key points

### [TEU-C08-021] Hierarchy tier details: volume, rest, nutrition, mental skills, team

- Total volume: "train more over long periods of time"; annual volume matters more than a single long run or week and mitigates injury. Coaching: add sessions per week/month; for time-crunched athletes, consolidate two shorter sessions into one longer run (same weekly hours, longer individual stimulus).
- Adequate rest: separately listed because athletes find rest harder than going full gas; CTS coaches' number one wish for athletes is more rest.
- Nutrition ("wholesome, mostly plants, periodized A plus"): no particular diet preached; diets that eliminate or demonize food groups/macronutrients are problematic to harmful. Guidelines: (1) consume adequate energy first; (2) eat more plants and fewer animals (not necessarily plant-based; animal protein fine, but all protein can come from plants); (3) don't fear carbohydrates: more fresh fruit and vegetables, save concentrated carbohydrate sources like processed grains for times when training energy expenditure is highest; (4) periodize nutrition: calories (and to lesser extent food choices) change with training volume/intensity.
- Mental skills: physiology sets maximum capacity; psychological skills determine how much is used (see Ch 15).
- Building a team: family, friends, training partners, coach, PT, massage therapist, nutrition professional, physician. Many athletes resist thinking they're "not enough of an athlete"; without support, athletes get swept off track when things go sideways.
tags: type:methodology type:nutrition level:all subtopic:volume subtopic:rest subtopic:periodized-nutrition subtopic:support-team condition:general focus:energy-balance focus:macronutrients
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Koop's hierarchy

### [TEU-C08-022] Hierarchy tier details: intensity distribution, planning, gut, terrain, enhancement, taper

- High-intensity and TID: ~20% of training sessions and only ~10% of total annual training hours. Example: 10 h/week (600 min) → average 60 min above lactate threshold just to hit 10%. Prescribed distribution is heavily weighted to low intensity with strategic high-intensity workouts.
- Planning: variation to change stimulus; annually, less specific aspects far out, more specific closer; on phase/weekly basis, arrangement of workouts. Don't give rigid schemes more importance than due.
- Train the gut: GI distress is a leading cause of DNFs, seen as a consequence of inadequate preparation; gut can and should be trained to handle food/fluid volume over 6, 12 or 30 hours. The gut becomes the weakest link only if training, rest and support are done right.
- Terrain-specific: Kaci Lickteig (2016 Western States winner) trains in Nebraska without mountains yet wins big-climbing ultras. Time in mountains doesn't make a mountain runner if fundamentals are lacking. Goal: put the fittest athlete on the start line; match event elevation if possible, but build the biggest aerobic engine first.
- Training stimuli enhancement: altitude works only if athlete is healthy, already fit and well rested; heat acclimation important for hot races but doesn't take long or much effort, so shouldn't displace fundamental training months out; same for strength training (Ch 11). Marginal gains are "sexier" than e.g. 4 x 10-min TempoRun, and coaches fall for them too.
- Taper: "taper tantrums" derail athletes; coach's key role in final two weeks is keeping the athlete on track, not the potential 1–2% gain (Dylan Bowman won 2018 Ultra-Trail Mt. Fuji by a couple of minutes). Most important: don't undo the work. Example of what not to do: training on the track in Chamonix days before UTMB (least event-specific). Ideal: athlete enters taper confident, relaxed, stress reduced, arrives rested.
tags: type:methodology type:caution level:all subtopic:intensity-distribution subtopic:gut-training subtopic:taper subtopic:marginal-gains metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Koop's hierarchy

### [TEU-C08-023] Key points for long-range planning (chapter summary rules)

- Block periodization will be superior to mixed for most ultrarunners.
- Start at the end (goal event) and work backward chronologically.
- Most specific closest to race, least specific furthest; typically moves from high intensity/low volume to high volume/low intensity as the season progresses.
- Include one training phase of each critical intensity (SteadyStateRun, TempoRun, RunningIntervals).
- Weaknesses earlier, strengths later.
- Eight-week blocks, halved with a recovery week every fourth week, one intensity at a time.
- Shorten block if intensity high or less important; lengthen if low or more important.
- Self-coached: short cycles, switch intensity more frequently.
- Hierarchy: total work and adequate rest are most important.
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:long-range-planning
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Key Points

### [TEU-C08-024] Table 11.1 Overview of strength-training programming

| PART OF TRAINING | SETS | REPS | REST | FREQUENCY |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Early | 1–5 | 1–5 | 90+ sec | 3x week |
| Mid | 3 | 8–12 | 45–60 sec | 2–3x week |
| Late | 2–3 | 10–15 or time-based | As needed | 1–2x week |

Intensity for all phases: set/rep range at ~70 to 80 percent of perceived max; e.g., on a set of five reps you should feel you could do one or two more before failure.
tags: type:periodization sport:running level:all phase:base phase:build phase:peak subtopic:strength-training metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | Table 11.1

### [TEU-C08-025] Organizing strength training across the season (early, mid, late)

- Divide the season before the event into thirds. Example: start January, race September (9 months): early = months 1–3; mid = months 4–7; late = months 8–9 (last two months).
- Early season: before run volume or intensity rises dramatically; foundations; often after a break. 1–5 sets of 1–5 reps of big compound movements, heavier weight, longer recoveries. More time to balance strength stress with running.
- Mid season: run intensity or volume increases and uses more physiological bandwidth. Moderate sets/reps: 3 sets of 8–12 reps, challenging weight leaving 1–2 reps in the tank (not to failure).
- Late season: zero in on the event; rest/recovery at a premium; reduce or eliminate unnecessary stress. Concentrate on corrective and restorative exercises: shore up weaknesses, mobility, more recovery instead of long intense strength sessions. Higher reps, can be time-based (e.g., 30 seconds per exercise or stretch). 1–2 days per week complementing run training.
tags: type:periodization sport:running level:all phase:base phase:build phase:peak subtopic:strength-training
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | "Organizing Strength Training Throughout the Season"

### [TEU-C08-026] Scheduling strength days within the week

- Place strength days as far as possible from the next long run and/or hard runs, so you enter them fresh.
- Pair strength with the evenings of days with intense work, so easy days stay truly easy.
- When lifting and running on the same day, prioritize running: run first, strength after.
- If long runs must be in the evenings or another day, the strategy of maximal recovery between strength and key runs still prevails.
- A third strength day is tricky; the author normally does not advise it for athletes focused on ultramarathon performance.
- Two- or three-day programs: alternate Day A and Day B; for three days repeat your preferred day.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-scheduling
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | "Prescription for Strength Training"

### [TEU-C08-027] Concept 1: Do the biggest training load when you are most rested

- You run best when fresh (hence tapers; best workouts follow a rest week). So you are most ready for the biggest load right after a recovery phase.
- Start the SRP with the workout(s) of biggest training load. Regardless of phase, do the hardest workout of the phase right at the start. Example: an athlete who can handle at most 60 minutes of SteadyStateRun does that workout at the beginning of the phase; later in the phase they may not manage that caliber, which is fine.
- Often the hardest workout is a "B" race: the race stimulus exceeds anything achievable on remaining training days.
- Fatigue from the initial workouts eventually produces the adaptation. You must accept that you get worse before you get better; if training is done right this is intentional.
- Consequence: volume of intensity should decrease as the phase progresses (see Key Points).
- Figure 12.1 (RunningIntervals phase, hardest workouts first four): see library entry.
tags: type:principle type:periodization level:all subtopic:front-loading subtopic:short-range-plan sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Concepts for the Short-Range Plan" | Fig 12.1

### [TEU-C08-028] Concepts 2 and 3: intensity vs adaptation time, recovery and workout density

Higher intensity (e.g., RunningIntervals):
- Little of it is needed to produce an adaptation: time at intensity per workout and total consecutive weeks needed are small relative to moderate/low-intensity work (see Figures 8.2 and 9.1, time course of adaptation). So time-crunched athletes can still get excellent high-intensity adaptations.
- Takes longer to recover, so more rest between sessions is needed. Most high-intensity phases, particularly RunningIntervals, need only 2-3 weeks to elicit adaptation. Total number of hard workouts is smaller (shorter phase, lower density).
Lower intensity (EnduranceRuns, SteadyStateRuns, to a lesser extent TempoRuns):
- Many weeks needed to accumulate enough stress. Phases can be longer than 3 weeks and have higher workout density.
- TempoRun: 2-3 times per week, often two on consecutive days. SteadyStateRun: up to 4 times per week in certain weeks. Back-to-back SteadyStateRun and TempoRun days are manageable for at least part of the phase.
- Figure 12.2: SteadyStateRun phase is longer with less recovery between workouts than a RunningInterval phase.
Hardest phase: the author tells athletes in the long-range plan which phase will be hardest. Ninety percent of the time it is the TempoRun (lactate threshold) phase: not the most intense nor the biggest volume, but the balance of volume and intensity grinds the athlete down more than higher intensity/less volume or lower intensity/more volume. During it: prioritize recovery and sleep, reschedule big work projects, avoid other life stressors, and do not attempt body-composition change (do it at another point in the season).
tags: type:principle type:periodization level:all system:threshold system:vo2max subtopic:workout-density subtopic:hardest-phase sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 | Fig 12.2

### [TEU-C08-029] Back-to-back hard days (load concentration)

- Author uses back-to-back strategy in nearly every phase: back-to-back long runs, and also back-to-back SteadyStateRuns, TempoRuns and, in certain circumstances, RunningIntervals.
- Expect to be more tired on day 2 (legs like bricks); if day 1 was done correctly you cannot perform quite at day-1 level.
- Reason is NOT to "teach running on tired legs" but because the effect on the aerobic engine is greater when training load is concentrated, and because it allows one or two more high-quality workouts per phase (Fig 12.3 back-to-back = ten hard workouts vs Fig 12.4 non-back-to-back = nine). Consider total workload of a phase, not only weekly volume, while maintaining appropriate rest.
- Borrowed from cross-country skiing and cycling block-style load concentration (Rønnestad, Hansen, Ellefsen 2014; Rønnestad et al. 2015); running should still be treated as its own sport.
- Tricky; requires careful monitoring of fatigue and performance.
- If designing your own: first try back-to-back at SteadyStateRun intensity, once in a phase, preferably at the beginning. If you react well, try across the entire phase, then at other intensities.
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule level:advanced subtopic:back-to-back subtopic:load-concentration sport:running
evidence: author-experience; cross-sport research (Rønnestad 2014, 2015)
src: Ch 12 "The Case for Back-to-Back Hard Days" | Figs 12.3, 12.4

### [TEU-C08-030] Architecture of the recovery phase and resting early

- Recovery phase: 4-6 days of 45- to 60-minute RecoveryRuns, perhaps an extra rest day. Nearly universal from elites to recreational athletes.
- It is almost impossible to over-recover from runs being too short or too easy. Over-recovery occurs at 7 or more easy days, as the benefit of more rest no longer outweighs gradual fitness decay.
- Don't be afraid to rest early: SRP is an educated forecast of how you will feel 2-6 weeks ahead; life stress (work, kids) intervenes, so adapt in real time. Err on the side of resting early; undershooting a phase lets you extend the next one. It evens out if you work hard when you can and back off at one of the three thresholds.
tags: type:periodization type:decision-rule type:recovery level:all subtopic:recovery-phase sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Architecture of the Recovery Phase", "Don't Be Afraid to Rest Early"

### [TEU-C08-031] Phase lengths by intensity (Key Points for short-range planning)

- RunningInterval phases: 2-4 weeks, followed by a 4-5 day recovery phase.
- TempoRun phases: 3-4 weeks, followed by a 4-5 day recovery phase.
- SteadyStateRun phases: 3-6 weeks, followed by a 4-5 day recovery phase.
- Rest phases of 4-6 days when: performance declines 3-8% on two of last three workouts; feel worse during runs on three consecutive days; or in the same phase for four weeks (RunningIntervals), four weeks (TempoRuns), six weeks (SteadyStateRuns).
- Higher intensity = shorter phase. Hardest workouts when freshest (typically after a recovery week); volume of intensity decreases as phase progresses.
[Note: the source lists 4-5 day recovery in the key points and 4-6 days in the body; recorded as printed.]
tags: type:periodization type:dose level:all subtopic:phase-length system:vo2max system:threshold sport:running phase:build
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Key Points for Short-Range Planning"

### [TEU-C08-032] Tapering: definition, expectations and basics

- Mujika definition: "a progressive nonlinear reduction of the training load during a variable period of time, in an attempt to reduce the physiological and psychological stress of daily training and optimize sports performance" (Mujika and Padilla 2000a).
- Expectation: a proper taper gains on average 3% (Mujika and Padilla 2003) versus no taper; simple reasonable rest may give ~2%, so the optimal taper adds only ~1%. Still taper, but be realistic, reasonable about duration, and don't stress (stress contradicts the taper's goal).
- Before tapering: train. Athletes enter events lacking confidence in fitness; each later workout gives fewer adaptations (Fig 10.4), so put work in early; in the final two months it is fine-tuning.
- Purpose: simultaneously reduce fatigue and enhance fitness; reducing training variables comes first, adaptation follows. Tapering is NOT maintenance; it improves physiology.
- Training variables: volume, intensity, frequency, environment.
- Start tapering 2-3 weeks before the event.
tags: type:periodization type:principle level:all phase:taper subtopic:taper sport:running
evidence: clinical-trial/review (Mujika and Padilla 2000a, 2003)
src: Ch 12 "Tapering for Your Event"

### [TEU-C08-033] Taper prescription by variable (3-week taper, 10 h/week runner)

Calibration: 3-week taper, runner training 10 hours/week.
- Volume: reduce first, fast decay (quick drop then level off). Week 1: 4-5 h (40-50% of normal); Week 2: 2-3 h (20-30%); Week 3: 2 h maximum (<20%).
- Intensity: reduce the amount (volume of intensity), not the type; follow fast decay. Example: normal 4 x 10 min TempoRun -> Taper Week 1: 3 x 5 min (37.5% of normal TempoRun volume); Week 2: 2 x 5 min (25%); Week 3: 1 x 5 min (12.5%). Keep intensity because the small amount preserves hard-earned benefits (Hickson et al. 1985; Sheply et al. 1992).
- Frequency: maintain or slightly reduce. If running 5 days/week keep 5; keep the same number of specific workouts per week (TempoRun, SteadyStateRun, etc.) with reduced volume of intensity; if reducing frequency, only by one day per week.
- Environment: maintain terrain and vertical specificity (trails stay trails, roads stay roads). Mistake: training on event-specific trails then switching (e.g., running the track in Chamonix before UTMB), violating specificity.
tags: type:protocol type:dose level:all phase:taper subtopic:taper metric:volume metric:load sport:running
evidence: clinical-trial (Hickson 1985; Sheply 1992); author-experience
src: Ch 12 "The Basics of Tapering"

### [TEU-C08-034] Mindfulness 5-step progression (MAC adaptation)

Author's interpretation of Gardner and Moore; whole process takes a few months; move slowly, a few weeks per step; earlier steps may be integrated concurrently.
1. Identify what matters in competition and what can lead you astray: two columns, pick a few items (can be revisited later). See Table 15.1.
2. Become acquainted with mindfulness in benign daily routines (cutting food, brushing teeth, folding clothes) or via apps (Calm, Headspace; basic subscription). Laundry exercise: quiet uninterrupted space; feel fabric textures, buttons, seams; notice detergent scent; notice weight balance on feet; note intruding thoughts as they come and go and when attention returns. Only noticing, no action.
3. Learn to accept: thoughts are neither good nor bad; if you notice judging a thought, notice the label, let it go, accept it, redirect to the task. Steps 2 and 3 repeated daily, at least two weeks before step 4.
4. Move to the field: during a RecoveryRun pick a section bounded by two landmarks approximately 2-3 minutes apart; note thoughts without judging or labeling. Repeat on subsequent RecoveryRuns for up to two weeks.
5. Put into play: review step-1 list; on a demanding run day or set of intervals (during intervals or on a climb) note thoughts without judgment; direct beneficial thoughts into action (breathing rate climbing uncontrollably on a climb -> slow down); for distracting urges (looking at watch) accept the thought and redirect to something productive (sensation in legs).
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:mindfulness phase:base
evidence: study (Gardner and Moore 2004, 2015; Moore 2009) adapted by author
src: Ch 15 "Mindfulness Strategies"

### [TEU-C08-035] Table 15.1 Inventory of attentional cues and distractions

| THINGS TO BE MINDFUL OF | THINGS THAT WILL DISTRACT YOU |
| Rate of perceived exertion | Looking at the pace on your watch |
| Internal confidence | Where you are compared to others |
| Taking things one mile at a time | Calculating the distance to the next aid station |
Example inventory; if pacing by RPE, watch is a distraction (what RPE-relevant information comes from current elevation?).
tags: type:template sport:running level:all subtopic:mindfulness metric:rpe
src: Ch 15 | Table 15.1

### [TEU-C08-036] Imagery: purpose and progression

- Imagery = mentally rehearsing a real scenario in training so movements, reactions, sensations and psychological responses are familiar when encountered (the "been there, done that" strategy). Applications: calmer when lost, confidence at big climbs not experienced in training (e.g., 4,000-foot pass), mastering technical skills (scrabbly descent).
- Evidence: Orlick and Partington 1988: 99 percent of 235 Canadian 1984 Olympians used imagery in preparation. Amateurs and age-groupers rank it low.
- Progression (Fig 15.6): (1) at home, imagery of training situations (effort of intervals, running smooth); (2) during training, imagery of simple race situations (running up a climb); (3) at home, complex race situations (solving puking, blisters, falling behind pace); (4) during training, complex race situations. Home setup: sit in chair, think of scenario, close eyes, rehearse; on runs, eyes open.
- Two categories: rehearsing critical race sections (smooth aid station, grinding a climb) and contingency planning for when things go awry.
- Example (Jane, Hardrock 100, trains in Minneapolis with 200-ft climbs, runs Silver State 50 beforehand): rehearses workouts at home; uses local 200-ft climb to imagine Silver State climbs; researches Handies Peak (14,000 ft) on YouTube, rehearses its two pitches separated by a talus field, sharp rock turning to loose gravel and how her feet will feel; then does the imagery during runs on the local hill and at Silver State 50. Move gradually from simple contrived imagery to complex real-world imagery.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:imagery
evidence: study (Orlick and Partington 1988) + author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Imagery" | Fig 15.6

### [TEU-C08-037] Self-talk: IMPACT approach (Hatzigeorgiadis)

Self-talk is instructional ("Plant your poles in line with your feet") or motivational ("You've got this"); match to the desired outcome. IMPACT:
- IDENTIFY what you want to achieve (specific goal).
- MATCH self-talk to needs (cues that facilitate the goal).
- PRACTICE cues with consistency in training.
- ASCERTAIN what worked; refine.
- CREATE A PLAN from what worked; for a race, map strategies to race stages.
- TRAIN: practice.
Cues must differ by race stage: "You've got this" may be poor early in a 100K because it encourages digging deep/going harder when the goal is to conserve energy and relax. Structure mirrors mindfulness, imagery and training at large: find plan, stress-test, train.
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:self-talk
evidence: study (Hatzigeorgiadis et al.)
src: Ch 15 "Self-Talk"

### [TEU-C08-038] Table 15.2 Matching self-talk strategies to what you want to achieve

| IDENTIFY WHAT YOU WANT TO ACHIEVE | MATCH SELF-TALK |
| Keep pushing when the race gets hard | "You've trained hard enough," "You've got this" |
| Run your own race | "Relax, focus on your effort" |
| Maximize effort during a training session | "Keep pushing, almost there" |
| Pole-strike effectively | "Plant your pole firmly, follow all the way through" |
tags: type:methodology sport:running level:all subtopic:self-talk
src: Ch 15 | Table 15.2

### [TEU-C08-039] Sequence of mental skills development

- Mental skills are chronic like endurance training: a single imagery session, one 5-minute mindfulness session or a few self-talk sentences will not have meaningful effect; deliberate repetitive practice over weeks/months.
- Follows periodization logic: general to specific, with progressive loading (each task gradually harder); can overlay on the LRP in the notes/other section (e.g., from sitting at home thinking about pacing an interval set to conquering Hope Pass at Leadville).
- Sequence (Fig 15.9, staggered start, all continue to race): (1) associative and dissociative focus (foundation: focus inward/outward and move between; details of thoughts not yet needed), (2) mindfulness, (3) imagery (integrates all to rehearse scenarios and decisions), (4) self-talk. Mindfulness and self-talk add meaning/depth/context to the focus. Note: the text says mindfulness and self-talk are introduced together, while the figure and key points list imagery before self-talk.
tags: type:periodization sport:running level:all subtopic:mental-skills-sequence
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Sequence of Mental Skills Development" | Fig 15.9

### [TEU-C08-040] Effort-based race-day strategy: pace, heart rate, RPE options

Three ways to gauge effort/speed in a race: pace, heart rate, perceived exertion; use one or a combination. Wrist GPS data can mislead if applied incorrectly.
Evidence: Western States 100 data (Hoffman 2014; Fig 16.6): CV in speed (percent) vs finish time for the ten fastest finishers (finish-time axis 14.8-15.8 h, CV axis 8-13 percent): the fastest finishers had the lowest variation in speed, positive trend line; better performers vary less and slow down less. Author notes it is unanswered whether they paced better or were simply better prepared.
OPTION 1 PACE. Good when: (a) terrain is flat and level without technical elements for 50K and 50-mile distances (race pace can mirror your TempoRun, SteadyStateRun, EnduranceRun paces; also for flat sections inside trail races); (b) you can train on the racecourse (e.g. SteadyStateRun intervals up a climb in training that is used in the race; check GPS at points on the climb). For 50K and 50-mile races many athletes can climb segments at SteadyStateRun or TempoRun intensity; if the race climbing segments match the training interval lengths in Table 9.1, the same intensities can be reproduced if properly trained.
Pace is poor when: terrain differs from home training ground (more technical slows you, benign speeds you; at all intensities/grades); altitude differs (highly individual, unwise to adjust by formula); 100 km and 100-mile events (much slower and easier than day-to-day training paces).
OPTION 2 HEART RATE. Generally poor (sleep, temperature, hydration, fatigue, time of day affect it; early adrenaline elevates it, fatigue depresses it); also violates "try nothing new on race day" since training is RPE-based. Only good scenario: athlete consistently overenthusiastic at the start. Use HR monitor as a governor for the first 25 percent of the race: take the athlete's normal SteadyStateRun HR range in training (intensity still set by RPE in the workouts) as the upper limit not to exceed. Example: training range 160 to 165 bpm for SteadyStateRun intervals -> ceiling 165 bpm for the first 25 percent. This holds the athlete at the lower end of SteadyState or higher end of EnduranceRun, since race-day HR is higher early due to freshness and adrenaline.
OPTION 3 RPE (the author's choice). Racing should reflect training; RPE (1 to 10 scale, see Ch 6) is independent of factors that affect pace and HR and is what the athlete used daily in training.
tags: type:methodology type:decision-rule level:all sport:running metric:pace metric:hr metric:rpe subtopic:pacing
src: Ch 16 "Creating an Effort-Based Race-Day Strategy", Options 1-3, Fig 16.6 | no page markers
evidence: study (Hoffman 2014)

### [TEU-C08-041] Calibrating RPE for race day (Figure 16.7 example)

If properly trained, you can recreate in the race the time-at-intensities accomplished in training. E.g. consistent 4 x 10-minute TempoRun efforts in training -> accumulate forty to sixty minutes of that intensity in the race. Same for EnduranceRun and SteadyStateRun. Map target RPE for different parts of the race based on race attributes.
Example (athlete, 2014 North Face Endurance Challenge):
(A) Training TempoRun, 3 intervals (RPE 8 to 9), Normalized Graded Pace (NGP): Tempo 1 11:21 at 5:58 min/mi; Tempo 2 11:30 at 5:44 min/mi; Tempo 3 11:44 at 5:30 min/mi (stated range 5:30-6:00 min/mi).
(B) Race, first four major climbs: Climb 1 17:26 at 5:43 min/mi; Climb 2 18:01 at 5:40; Climb 3 8:43 at 5:34; Climb 4 57:15 at 6:17 min/mi. NGP of first three climbs similar to training TempoRun; climb 4 (~60 min) indicative of a SteadyStateRun effort. Calibration worked because climb lengths were similar to training intervals.
tags: type:methodology type:workout level:advanced sport:running metric:rpe metric:pace system:threshold subtopic:ngp subtopic:rpe-calibration
src: Ch 16 sidebar "Calibrating RPE for Race Day", Fig 16.7 | no page markers
evidence: case-study

## C09 Decision Rules & Heuristics

### [TEU-C09-001] Aerobic Deficiency Syndrome (ADS): author's rebuttal

- Term coined by Phil Maffetone (2015), described as devastating, causing reduced endurance, quickened fatigue, loss of aerobic speed and chronic conditions; used to justify avoiding high-intensity intervals and doing only very low-intensity exercise.
- Author's position: the term appears nowhere in scientific literature (no textbook or journal article), unlike overtraining (underperformance) syndrome, which is extensively described. Beyond a minute or two of all-out effort nearly 100 percent of energy is aerobic (Figure 2.1), so even high-intensity intervals derive almost all energy aerobically; the idea of a "deficient aerobic system" from too much high-intensity work is called nonsense and a "made-up term".
- Rule: do not avoid high-intensity work because of this syndrome.
tags: type:decision-rule type:philosophy level:all subtopic:aerobic-deficiency-syndrome subtopic:high-intensity system:vo2max
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 sidebar "What About Aerobic Deficiency Syndrome?"

### [TEU-C09-002] Four-to-one rule for elite athletes

Rule of thumb: for every four workouts given to an elite athlete, hold them back in some way on one. Why: elites are very mentally tough, often too tough for their own good, tending to push through injury and illness until problems become unnecessarily serious. Toughness is valuable on race day but can harm day-to-day training.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution level:elite subtopic:holding-back subtopic:overtraining
src: Ch 3 "Mental Skills"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C09-003] Queasy stomach: slow down and cool off

Rule: when the stomach is queasy, "slow down and cool off". Slowing reduces oxygen/nutrient demand of working muscle and heat production; cooling reduces blood needed by skin; both redistribute blood to the stomach. Doing both gives the biggest effect.
tags: type:decision-rule focus:gut-health sport:running level:all subtopic:nausea
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "Your stomach and intestines are made of muscle, too!"

### [TEU-C09-004] Blister prevention hierarchy: training, gear, race-day measures, treatment

Priority order: (1) training, (2) gear (shoes and socks), (3) race-day preventive measures, (4) treatment.
- Training: gradual exposure to friction (hikes, marches; military studies: Allan 1964; Hodges 1975; Knapik 1995) reduces susceptibility; epidermis thickens and becomes more cohesive; callus forms from rapid skin-cell turnover (Kim 2010).
- Shoe/sock: reduce friction between skin-sock and sock-shoe, and manage moisture transfer to air. Toe socks reduce toe skin-to-skin friction. A well-fitting wicking sock is the best strategy (Herring and Richie 1990; Knapik 1995, 1996). Trial shoe/sock combos early in the season; buy enough shoes and socks for the whole season since designs change.
- Race-day: little scientific evidence tape, lubricants, powders or antiperspirants work; some may add moisture and friction (Figure 4.4; Allan 1964; Allan and Macmillan 1963; Knapik 1995, 1998; Nacht 1981; Quinn 1967; Reynolds 1995). Less is more: training and an effective shoe/sock combo are the starting points; extras add variables and may worsen things. Race conditions differ from home trails, so training combo may not hold.
- No gear or training eliminates blister risk; learn field repair.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:blister-prevention
evidence: study (multiple cited)
src: Ch 4 | "Training, Gear, and Protection"

### [TEU-C09-005] Callus management: shave down, do not remove

Thick callus can act as the outer layer and separate from the dermis, causing a blister under the callus and loss of the whole callus. Keep calluses shaved flush with surrounding skin using a pumice stone or metal callus shaver, for a smooth uniform surface. Maintenance, not removal.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:calluses
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "Shave down your calluses"

### [TEU-C09-006] Blister or hot spot during a race: fix early; do not change gait

Decide whether to stop based on goals, performance expectations, safety, race situation. The more time left and the bigger the problem could become, the more worthwhile to spend a few minutes. Err on the side of caution and fix early, particularly at 100K and 100 miles.
If you cannot treat it (no products or far from aid): keep your gait as normal as possible. Do not "run around" the blister; altered gait causes pain/injury up the kinetic chain (foot, ankle, knee, hip) that cannot be treated mid-race. Not a strategy to rely on.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all phase:race subtopic:blisters
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "Blister Patch and Repair"

### [TEU-C09-007] Combating muscular breakdown: eccentric training, cost-benefit, match race grade and speed

Tools ultrarunners use: trekking poles, highly cushioned shoes, strength training, hard downhill repeats; author questions their science and practicality for time invested.
- Research suggests a little eccentric training goes a long way and adaptations last long (Clarkson 1992; Nosaka 2001); the optimal amount and the diminishing-returns curve are unknown.
- Downhill prowess may depend more on movement coordination than oxygen consumption or force tolerance (Minetti 2002); training downhill technique may matter more than running downhills harder.
- Recovery: the more you descend and the faster you descend (higher forces), the longer recovery needed; for the same foot of elevation change, descending takes longer to recover from than ascending, though ascending costs more energy and time.
- Cost-benefit: little descending is needed for adaptation; more requires disproportionately long recovery. Author's philosophy: match the grade and speed of training to what you will experience in the race (see Chapter 5).
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:eccentric-training
evidence: study (Clarkson 1992; Nosaka 2001; Minetti 2002)
src: Ch 4 | "How to combat muscular breakdown"

### [TEU-C09-008] Varying stride to spread muscle damage

Research suggests varying stride during a race (heel-striker periodically forefoot strikes and vice versa; Bontemps 2020; Rowlands 2001) may spread work and damage across muscle groups. Happens naturally on varied trail terrain; on homogeneous terrain consciously altering gait could help. Contrast: do not alter gait to avoid a blister.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:stride-variation
evidence: study (Bontemps 2020; Rowlands 2001)
src: Ch 4 | "Should I change my stride?"

### [TEU-C09-009] Run vs hike decision rules (take-home messages)

- Certain grade/speed combinations make walking more economical than running and vice versa, but they are not yet known.
- On flat ground, a good run-to-walk transition point is ~13:30–14 min/mi. Walking faster than that: running is more economical (less oxygen and energy). Running slower than that: walking is more economical.
- GPS watches cannot yet tell you when to walk or run in an ultra.
Practical recommendations:
1. Practice walking in training. Walking is a big part of the race plan for longer, higher-gain ultras. If climbs are available, sometimes dial down and hike them even though you could run.
2. Use RPE during the race. Have an RPE plan. Hike (slower if needed) when RPE exceeds the plan; run when you can keep RPE in the desired range. "Simple, but not easy!"
3. When in doubt, prioritize hiking if the race or climb is longer, and running if shorter. On short climbs or races you can run the uphill slightly harder, since the effort is short and a downhill (likely) follows for recovery.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all metric:rpe subtopic:run-hike-transition subtopic:power-hiking
