evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "The Take-Home Messages"

### [TEU-C09-010] Flaws of NGP/GAP (surface and descents)

- Flaw 1, surface: neither accounts for work differences across surfaces (sand vs track, technical vs smooth terrain).
- Flaw 2, descents: they use energy/oxygen cost differences only. Downhill stress also involves foot speed, coordination and musculoskeletal stress, which are not captured, so both underestimate the stress of downhill running. Minor in a single/short run but a bigger flaw when summing cumulative stress over long runs or long periods.
- Implication: interpret NGP-based metrics (rTSS, ATL, CTL) with caution when surface or descending varies a lot.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:ngp-gap metric:pace
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Flaw 1", "Flaw 2"

### [TEU-C09-011] Limitations of TSS/rTSS/hrTSS, ATL, CTL

- Flaws mirror NGP (used in equations): surface changes and heavy descending make run-to-run comparison difficult. If training has consistent surface and climbing/descending, rTSS/ATL/CTL give a ballpark of relative difficulty and cumulative load.
- Points are not equal over time: 50 TSS at the start of a 1-hour run is far easier than 50 TSS at the end of a 6-hour run, yet scored the same.
- Endurance and intensity are combined in one metric: a 60-min TempoRun (essentially all-out) scores about the same as a 2.5-hour EnduranceRun but has different effects and needs more recovery.
- Do not let TSS dictate training; use with volume.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:tss-rtss metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Beware the Flaws"; Key Points

### [TEU-C09-012] Heart-rate-based training errors from fatigue and heat/caffeine

- If HR is boosted 5-8 bpm by heat and caffeine, holding a target of e.g. 150 bpm yields a slower actual pace. If fatigue suppresses HR, athletes push harder to hit the number, digging the hole deeper.
- Fig 6.3 (100K race): first three climbs at similar NGP; HR highest on climb 1 (freshness/adrenaline; avg 149 on climb 2 and 143 on climb 3 in the figure) and suppressed on climb 3 from fatigue. Targeting climb-1 HR would be too slow early; matching early HR on climb 3 would be far too hard.
- Fig 6.4 (back-to-back TempoRun interval days): similar NGP (day 1 intervals 5:22, 5:10, 5:20, 5:06; day 2 5:27, 5:15, 5:19, 5:09) but lower HR on day 2 (avg 174/171/168/172 vs 169/166/168/168). A HR-driven athlete either could not complete the workout or would push too hard.
- A suppressed HR does not mean less work is being done.
- Use HR after the fact in analysis only, knowing the confounders.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:heart-rate-confounders metric:hr
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 | Figs 6.3, 6.4

### [TEU-C09-013] RPE as fatigue early-warning and progress indicator

- RPE gives context to HR/GPS files. Same pace (e.g., 9:00 min/mi) feels harder when fatigued: body can still do the job but effort is greater, an early warning of fatigue.
- Progress: early season a 10-mile run at 9:00/mi may rate 7-8; later the same course/pace may feel like 6, and RPE 7-8 may need 8:30/mi.
- Author bases the vast majority of athlete training on perceived exertion; the athlete's job is to master gauging intensity and workload by RPE, which requires understanding what each intensity aims at, its bodily impact and how to detect it.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:rpe metric:rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6

### [TEU-C09-014] Running power meters: author's assessment

- Cycling power meters measure force on cranks/pedals/hub and speed (power = force × velocity); revolutionized cycling coaching.
- Running power meters (Stryd, RunScribe, Polar) derive power by algorithms: foot-movement mechanical work or reverse-engineered metabolic (oxygen cost) power. None directly measures force and speed; "running power" is essentially meaningless biomechanically and practically.
- In trail running they cannot account for surface (pavement, sand, alpine ridge), so as an intensity surrogate they are meaningless. Foot-level devices may help with technique/form analysis. Promise as intensity tool not yet proven.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:running-power metric:power
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "What About Running Power Meters?"

### [TEU-C09-015] Is an altitude intervention a good idea? Ferritin and individual variation

- Individual variation ("responders vs nonresponders") often reflects an underlying cause that blunts the expected EPO and hemoglobin increase; context matters.
- Inhibitors: injury, illness (inflammation lowers hemoglobin at sea level; raised cortisol blunts adaptation) and low iron status.
- Avoid high-intensity training (raises cortisol) in the days before traveling to altitude.
- Serum ferritin < 35 ng/mL: athletes in altitude camps could not increase red blood cell mass even after 4 weeks of exposure (Constantini et al. 2017).
- Decision rule (Figure 7.2): Ferritin >= 35 ng/mL? No -> talk with doctor about iron supplementation; if iron improving -> proceed to next question, if not -> keep talking to doctor. Yes -> Capacity to add training stress? Yes -> Can you afford to live at altitude 3–4 weeks? Yes -> live high (6,840–8,050 ft) 21–28 days and do hard training below 4,100 ft. No -> Can you afford a tent? Yes -> live above 5,600 ft? Yes -> aim for 10 h/night for 21–28 days; No (below 5,600 ft) -> consider extending the 28-day minimum exposure with a gradual ramp up in nighttime altitude. No tent -> Can you travel to the race 1 week early? No -> consider traveling in the night before.
- Key points: athletes with low ferritin (<35 ng/mL) have room to improve through normal training; athletes who cannot logistically do a camp should improve through other means.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:ferritin subtopic:iron metric:load sport:running
evidence: review (Constantini, Wilhite, and Chapman 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Is an Altitude Intervention a Good Idea?" | Fig 7.2

### [TEU-C09-016] Pre-race altitude strategies

Best thing: be as fit as possible. Then three actions:
1. Iron panel three months out: see primary care provider; do it before taking any iron supplement (too much iron can be toxic). Three months allows time to benefit from increased iron intake, for a race, camp or tent.
2. Arrive early: if not possible, arrive the night before or morning of the event, fitting the race into the 24-hour window before the miserable responses ramp up. General recommendations (Chapman, Stickford, and Levine 2010): 3–5 days early for races at 1,640–6,561 ft; 1–2 weeks for 6,561–9,842 ft; at least 3 weeks for races over 9,842 ft.
3. Trial run: attend an altitude camp before the A race. Benefits mostly experiential: learn body response, adjust fueling and pacing to avoid surprises.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:altitude subtopic:race-arrival subtopic:iron-panel sport:running phase:race
evidence: guideline/review (Chapman, Stickford, Levine 2010)
src: Ch 7 "Pre-Race Altitude Strategies"

### [TEU-C09-017] Symptoms of OTS/UUPS

- Unrelenting feeling of fatigue
- Acute performance decline
- Sleep disturbances
- Elevated resting heart rate
- Low HRV
- Mood disturbances
- Loss of libido
- Low motivation to train
- Loss of appetite
- Frequent or persistent minor injuries and illnesses

Caveat: individual symptoms can be part of a normal response to hard, effective training. The tipping point from functional overreaching is individual. Rule out RED-S, anemia, hypothyroidism and illness before blaming the training plan.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:overtraining-syndrome metric:hrv sport:running
evidence: author-experience; Lewis 2015
src: Ch 8 | p. n/a

### [TEU-C09-018] Criteria for evaluating any recovery method

Recovery is very individual. A few products are utter nonsense, but most give varying effectiveness for most athletes. Placebo is real, and athletes must be encouraged to trust science over intuition and emotion. Test a method against these questions:
- Will it promote muscle repair and growth, or any other adaptive response?
- Will it replenish something that training has depleted?
- Will it enhance your readiness for your next workout?
- Will it support immune system function?
- Will it be any more effective than getting more sleep? (Perhaps the most important question.)

For many athletes, adding activities in order to get more or better rest is counterproductive. The best thing they can do is optimize sleep behaviors.
Time between training sessions and phases (scheduling) is the most basic form of recovery. A good plan fails if it is poorly structured and rest-deficient. See Ch 9 (key workouts), Ch 10 (long-range plan) and Ch 12 (short-range plan).
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle level:all subtopic:recovery-evaluation
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "The Goals of Recovery"; "Time Between Training" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C09-019] Intervals shorter than one minute

- Have a time and place; good for physical tools and mental skills, but not superior to longer intervals.
- Same time at intensity is achievable: e.g. 6 x 3 min on/3 off (18 min) = 36 x 30 s on/30 s off, if you can count that high.
- Fine for a different or novel stimulus; benefit likely negligible when time at intensity is equal.
- Author prefers 1–3 min intervals in trail running: more sport-specific and easier to manage.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running system:vo2max level:all subtopic:short-intervals
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "What about Intervals Shorter Than One Minute?"

### [TEU-C09-020] Recovery intervals: easier rest yields stronger intervals

Do work periods hard and easy periods easy. Recovery between intervals can be easy running or even walking. Higher-intensity recovery lowers work-interval intensity, shifting the whole workout easier and out of the target time at intensity.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:interval-recovery
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "A Word on Recovery"

### [TEU-C09-021] LRP Principle 2: why every intensity must be visited, even for slow races

- Reason: over months any one adaptation reaches diminishing returns; intensities build on each other through the year.
- Counter-argument (colleague: train only at the very low specific intensity for a ~30-hour Leadville): flawed because all energy systems are connected; improvements at easy aerobic pace come only by raising maximum sustainable intensity (lactate threshold) and maximum aerobic capacity (VO2 max).
- Lab observation: ultrarunners tested at start of coaching often have sustainable intensity (lactate threshold) very close to maximum (VO2 max) intensity, sometimes only ~3% apart; only a handful of seconds per mile separates a pace held for hours from one held for minutes. Normally coveted (Velocity = F × VO2 max / Cr, where F = fraction of VO2 max sustained, Cr = cost of running; some elite marathoners sustain 80–90% of VO2 max up to two hours), but with no gap there is nowhere to go since sustainable pace cannot exceed maximum.
- Protocol in that situation: first raise VO2 max, then work lactate threshold and lower intensities, giving room between VO2 max and other intensities before returning to other aspects. Each gain in one area provides tools for improving the others.
- Application: early-season RunningIntervals and late-season EnduranceRun/SteadyStateRun bookend the plan; TempoRun fills the middle. In 5- to 7-month LRPs: figure out the bookends first, then fill the middle with what is left.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule sport:running system:threshold system:vo2max level:advanced subtopic:diminishing-returns
evidence: author-experience (lab data from coached athletes)
src: Ch 10 Principle 2

### [TEU-C09-022] LRP Principle 3: strengths late, weaknesses early

- Runners know strengths/weaknesses from group runs and races (e.g., passing on climbs, dropped on technical descents → strength climbing, weakness technical running). Improve technical running far from the key event, climbing close to it.
- More applicable to elite athletes, who generally win races aligned with their strengths. For others, focus on improving weaknesses as far out as possible.
- Example athlete: strength at EnduranceRun intensity (better at 100 miles than marathon/50K; tolerates high volume; runs forever at low intensity); weak at 5K/10K (VO2 max reliant) and fatigues quickly in RunningInterval phases. Her strengths/weaknesses aligned with most/least important physiology, so flow RunningIntervals → TempoRun → SteadyStateRun was natural; this is not always the case.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:strengths-weaknesses
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 Principle 3

### [TEU-C09-023] Balancing significance and diminishing returns; when to change phase

- Planning stage: significance takes priority in setting phase duration (reality not yet observed; blocks are educated guesses and may change).
- Once training unfolds, prioritize by observed diminishing returns. The coach evaluates daily freshness/fatigue and, above all, whether the athlete is better after a rest phase; then keeps or alters the LRP.
- Athletes must fatigue/get worse during training and then improve after recovery; stress is needed to adapt, but it can only be taken so far before it is too great.
- Rule: if an athlete fails to improve shortly after a recovery phase, it indicates readiness for a different stimulus.
- Self-coached athletes: switch in and out of phases more quickly rather than wringing out every last improvement; errs on the side of caution, keeps stimulus novel, helps prevent burnout. Key point: err on the side of short cycles and switching intensity more frequently.
- Variables the coach uses to decide a change: interval metrics, cumulative training load, acute training load, training stress scores, qualitative feedback, workout comments in training logs (see Chapter 6).
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:phase-change subtopic:diminishing-returns subtopic:burnout-prevention
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Balancing Significance and Diminishing Returns"

### [TEU-C09-024] Flowchart 11.1: Are you a good candidate for strength training?

Answer in order; any No leads to "Focus on rest, recovery, and corrective exercises (if needed)":
1. Are you at least 2 months out from your event?
2. Do you have enough rest and recovery in your current program?
3. Do you have time in your schedule to add strength training?
4. Do you know how to lift properly or have access to someone who can help?
5. Will the gains from strength training be greater than those from resting or running more?
All Yes: you are a good candidate for strength training. The author notes some athletes qualify, but many do not.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-candidacy
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | Figure 11.1, p. n/a

### [TEU-C09-025] Strength-training rule: avoid high-rep low-weight leg circuits as a substitute

- Leg circuits, body-weight squats and other high-volume/low-weight programs (sometimes timed, cleverly named, done daily) work only very temporarily and mainly in a corrective setting.
- Reasons: after initial adaptation (a few weeks) there is no progressive overload; resistance is too low for strength adaptation and cardiopulmonary stress is not specific to running ("worst of both worlds"); timed circuits emphasize speed/volume as a score, sacrificing technique and raising injury risk.
- Rule: if crunched for time, do a few heavy squats instead of several dozen body-weight squats, lunges and matrixes. Strength training should be purposeful: hard and requiring recovery to reap adaptation.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:leg-circuits subtopic:progressive-overload
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | sidebar "What about leg circuits and body-weight exercises?"

### [TEU-C09-026] Weekly progressive overloads have consequences (do not build the hardest last)

Taking a plan like Figure 12.1 and flipping it (each week adds progressively more time at intensity) is common, even among elites. Problem, underappreciated: athletes then attempt the hardest/longest workouts when most fatigued. This can cause insufficient recovery, maladaptation or injury. Rule: avoid weekly progressive overload structures that put the hardest workout last in a phase.
tags: type:caution type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:progressive-overload sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 sidebar "Weekly Progressive Overloads Have Consequences"

### [TEU-C09-027] Recovery-phase checklist: when to rest (three points)

Principle: training makes performance deteriorate short-term (days/weeks) before it improves long-term (weeks/months); both hard-enough stress and recovery are required. Trend is upward over long periods, not every week; you cannot set PRs every week (if you do, you are new or training too easy and will stagnate). Expect moderate worsening before rest brings improvement (Fig 12.5: performance dips after training, adequate recovery gives adaptation and improvement above baseline; insufficient recovery leaves performance below).
Take a recovery phase if ANY is met:
1. Performance declines: a 3-8% drop (compare Normalized Graded Pace or Grade Adjusted Pace of intervals/weekly hard runs) on more than two out of the last three workouts [as printed; elsewhere "two out of the last three"]. A drop <3% is normal day-to-day noise: keep training. A single day with a 3-8% drop is expected: keep training.
2. Feeling worse for three days in a row during runs. Subjective: e.g., RPE 1-2 points higher than normal for an EnduranceRun effort, or training comments with words like "sluggish," "flat." Do not seek hard metrics; a single worse day should not deter you.
3. Phase gets long: even if feeling great and improving, apply rest after a maximum of six weeks of training. Basis: research shows 4-6 weeks for training to translate into meaningful performance/physiology improvement; rest near the end uses natural buildup/breakdown.
Flowchart (Fig 12.6): Q1 performance down 3-8% in 2 of last 3 workouts? Yes->recovery. Q2 feeling "flat" or "slow" 3 days in a row? Yes->recovery. Q3 training hard >6 weeks? Yes->recovery. Else keep training. Side note: if HRV, sleep or other factors indicate fatigue, move closer to "Yes".
tags: type:decision-rule type:threshold type:recovery level:all subtopic:recovery-phase-checklist metric:rpe metric:pace sport:running
evidence: author-experience (4-6 week adaptation time frame cited generally)
src: Ch 12 "How to Schedule Recovery Phases" | Figs 12.5, 12.6

### [TEU-C09-028] Fueling decision rules (Ch 13)

- If a run is >90 minutes: take carbohydrate 60-90 g/hr; start at 60 g/hr, adjust to intensity and tolerance.
- If hot: raise fluid per hour (separate from calories); if cool: reduce fluid.
- If WUT shows 2 of 3 indicators for 2-3 days: increase water through the day.
- If sodium sweat is high (salt-caked): somewhat more sodium; consider dietitian-led sweat sodium test; do not aim for 1:1 replacement.
- If drink sodium is light: make up with gels, chews, food.
- If recovery meal is not available within 2 h after a long (>3 h or >2 h with intensity) run: use recovery beverage.
- Rehearse race nutrition rates in training and use report card to check.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all focus:meal-timing focus:hydration subtopic:fueling-rules
src: Ch 13
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C09-029] Ultra-specific nutrition challenges and adaptations

1) PROLONGED DURATION. Challenge: being outdoors, awake and on your feet for many hours. Shorter-event athletes can eat a pre-race meal, deplete stores faster, take risks (dehydration, GI distress) and finish before performance declines; ultra athletes cannot, because consequences arrive well before the finish. Adaptation: develop the ability to consume and digest a steady stream of calories over many hours. Some calories must come from solid foods with more bulk, fat and protein than carbohydrate-only gels/drinks. Focus primarily on carbohydrate calories, but race foods also replace meals. Fat and protein are satiating; responding to hunger by overconsuming carbohydrate raises the likelihood of gastric distress. With solid foods containing fat and protein, base carbohydrate intake on caloric expenditure rather than a growling stomach.

2) LONG DISTANCES/TIMES BETWEEN AID STATIONS. Urban marathons have aid nearly every mile. Western States 100: 21 aid stations in 100 miles; 5–7 miles apart in the first third, 3–4 miles in the final third; can mean well over an hour between stations, longer if you stop due to trouble. Adaptation: carry gear that lets you fuel along the way; train with the exact gear planned for race day (hydration pack, bottles, fuel belt); if gear is mandatory, check it fits in the pack, the pack stays comfortable and you can run well fully loaded; make sure pacers/crew can refill your pack quickly.

3) DRAMATIC ENVIRONMENTAL CHANGES. Day-into-night events: temperature swings of 40+ degrees, high-altitude rain/snow even in summer; these change sweat rate and hunger/thirst perception. Adaptation: use equipment to limit core temperature change. Hotter: lighter clothing, remove layers, soak clothing, cool drinks. Colder: more layers, swap wet for dry. Stay hydrated in both. As temperatures climb, anticipate higher sweat rate and start drinking more; the longer you wait, the further behind you get. In cold, sweat rate declines: reduce fluid intake to avoid overhydration. If already behind on hydration, cooler temperatures can be used to gradually recover. Know your sweat rate for a range of temperatures. Environmental disruption can make you forget to eat (thunderstorm, predawn cold); practice and a knowledgeable crew help.

4) GENERAL FATIGUE AND DIMINISHED DECISION-MAKING. Tired athletes leave aid stations without refilling packs, go miles without consuming, scrap the plan and scarf cookies until sick. Adaptation: habits and routines consistent from everyday training to longest races. Routine each time you pick up the pack: check water, standard food choices, standard equipment (e.g., rain shell). Always leave an aid station with at least your bull's-eye foods. Keep routines simple: always have something to eat, something to drink, something to protect from the elements.

5) FOOD FATIGUE. Eating small amounts repeatedly for many hours is exhausting; athletes stop wanting to expend effort or lose taste for available foods. Rare in shorter events; ultra athletes need a wider range of foods that work without upsetting the stomach. Strategy: Bull's-Eye Nutrition (see separate entry).
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle level:all sport:running focus:meal-timing focus:hydration subtopic:ultra-challenges subtopic:aid-stations condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Ultra-Specific Challenges of Ultradistance Events" | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-030] Author's position on fat adaptation: reasons against and acceptable low-risk tools

Reasons the author advises against aggressive fat-adaptation:
1. Short-term energy deficits and RED-S. Strategies rely on energy deficits; RED-S is problematic for endurance athletes (deficit from workout compounded by underfueling). Effects (Fig 14.2, adapted from Mountjoy 2018): decreased endurance performance, increased injury risk, decreased training response, impaired judgement, decreased coordination, decreased concentration, irritability, depression, decreased glycogen stores, decreased muscle strength. Negatives can quickly outweigh positives.
2. Ultrarunners are already good at fat burning: 2019 IAAF consensus: "high fat oxidation rates also appear to be inherent in ultramarathon runners regardless of background macronutrient dietary modifications." Doubling down is high-risk, low/no reward.
3. Adherence is difficult, effect transient, expertise scarce: ketogenic diet needs at least a few weeks for maximum fat oxidation benefit, and the effect loses potency within a few days of reintroducing carbohydrate; other strategies likely similar. Safe implementation requires a registered dietitian managing energy and micronutrients; fat-adapted friends/promoters are not experts.
4. Athletes thriving on low-carb: benefits are almost entirely due to weight loss, not fat adaptation; keto "as a tool" really means a tool for energy restriction.
5. Not a good health message: too much fixation on what is left out (skipped gels, breakfast) rather than what goes in. Focus on calorie quality and adequate quantity over composition/restriction. Critiques "strategic tuber/fruit": "If a simple 'not-so-strategic' banana ... is going to destroy your nutrition strategy, there's something wrong with your nutrition strategy." "Just eat the sweet potatoes, bananas, and apples."
Key point: fat metabolism enhancement is a natural byproduct of endurance training.
tags: type:philosophy type:decision-rule level:all sport:running system:fat-oxidation focus:energy-balance subtopic:red-s subtopic:fat-adaptation condition:general
evidence: guideline (Mountjoy 2018; IAAF 2019 consensus); author-experience
src: Ch 14 "Short-Term Energy Deficits and RED-S" through "I Don't Think It's a Good Health Message", Fig 14.2 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-031] Low-risk carbohydrate periodization and low-carb session options (author's recommendation)

If you want to double down on natural fat burning, low-risk/medium-reward tools:
1. Periodize macronutrient composition: during EnduranceRun and SteadyStateRun phases consume ~50 percent of daily calories from carbohydrate; during TempoRun or RunningInterval phases raise to ~60 percent (akin to PCHO).
2. Early morning EnduranceRun up to two hours after an overnight fast: likely low-risk.
3. Two-a-day: interval session (TempoRun or RunningIntervals) with adequate carbohydrate availability followed by an EnduranceRun with lower carbohydrate availability; promotes fat oxidation without many deleterious effects, provided there is time for two sessions and extra logistics do not add non-workout stress.
Key point: if adopting a strategy, keep it low-risk (e.g., some EnduranceRun sessions with low carbohydrate availability).
tags: type:decision-rule type:nutrition level:advanced sport:running system:fat-oxidation phase:base subtopic:periodized-carbohydrate condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 "There Is Room for Periodizing Carbohydrate Availability..." | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-032] Combination 1: Dehydrated and hyponatremic

Hydration low, sodium low. Occurrence: rare (3–6% of 100-mile finishers).
How you got here: lost much electrolyte through sweat and gradually fell behind in both sodium and fluid; rising temperature increases sweat rate and accelerates loss.
Symptoms: dry skin and mouth, thirst, craving salty foods, dizziness on standing, weight loss, low/no urine. At risk for heat stress in warm conditions.
Fix: sports drink, salty broth, and/or water plus salty foods. Don't consume only water. In warm/hot conditions slow down, wet clothing, douse with water.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hyponatremia subtopic:dehydration condition:general
evidence: author-experience; Hoffman 2013
src: Ch 14 Combination 1 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-033] Combination 2: Euhydrated and hyponatremic

Hydration normal, sodium low. Occurrence: rare (3–6%).
How: relatively easy to reach; food fatigue with gradually less food or fewer sodium-containing drinks; salty sweaters with higher sweat losses. No elevated heat stress risk (plenty of fluid) but can become much more serious if sodium not corrected.
Symptoms: normal weight and thirst, moist mouth, craving salty foods but nausea upon eating, normal urine output.
Fix: sports drink, salty broth, and/or water plus salty foods. Try ginger or another remedy for nausea.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hyponatremia condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 2 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-034] Combination 3: Overhydrated and hyponatremic (dangerous)

Hydration high, sodium low. Occurrence: rare (3–6%). Dangerous; requires immediate action.
How: too much plain water and too little sodium; even knowledgeable athletes when food fatigue/fatigue impair decisions; stop eating due to nausea but keep drinking plain water hoping to restart the gut; hot conditions plus frequent aid stations where sips of low-sodium fluids (cola, ginger ale, water) add up.
Symptoms: weight gain, puffy hands, high output of clear urine, cognitive impairment, disorientation, sloshing stomach, nausea, possible vomiting. Low heat stress risk.
Fix: if mild and caught early, consume salty foods and restrict fluid intake. Even if mild, stay at an aid station with medical professionals. Crew must monitor closely; can deteriorate quickly. Frequent vomiting, seizures or significant neurological impairment need immediate medical attention. When in doubt, seek medical help.
tags: type:caution type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hyponatremia subtopic:overhydration condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 3 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-035] Combination 4: Dehydrated and normonatremic

Hydration low, sodium normal. Occurrence: somewhat common (10–15%).
How: plenty of sodium from foods but too little fluid or underestimated sweat rate. At altitude, dry air evaporates sweat quickly (skin seems dry) and respiratory fluid loss rises. In warm environments athletes run low on carried fluid; rationing is better than running dry but eventually leads to dehydration.
Symptoms: weight loss, dry mouth, strong thirst, normal appetite, possible dizziness on standing, fatigue, loss of focus, low urine output, urine apple-juice color or darker. Elevated heat stress risk.
Fix: consume water; sports drink/salty broth allowed but not necessary. In heat slow down, wet clothing, douse with water.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:dehydration subtopic:altitude condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 4 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-036] Combination 5: Overhydrated and normonatremic

Hydration high, sodium normal. Occurrence: common (>30%). Per Hoffman, Hew-Butler, Stuempfle 2013 (Western States 100, Rio Del Lago 100) the most common state aside from euhydrated/normonatremic.
How: cooler weather with low sweat rates, plenty of sodium-rich food but more fluid than needed; regimented fluid schedules not reduced when cool, rain or wet clothing lower sweat rate; also in heat from paranoia about dehydration.
Symptoms: nausea, sloshing stomach, bloating, low thirst, slight weight gain or absence of expected minor loss, puffy hands, normal taste for salty foods but diminished hunger from fullness.
Fix: restrict fluid to just moisten the mouth; consider swishing and spitting. No increased heat stress risk.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:overhydration condition:general
evidence: author-experience; Hoffman 2013
src: Ch 14 Combination 5 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-037] Combination 6: Dehydrated and hypernatremic

Hydration low, sodium high. Occurrence: extremely rare (<1%).
How: differs from dehydrated/normonatremic by possible absence of urination (body holds water). Overconsumption of sodium via salt tablets or losing track of sodium from multiple sources (chips + salt tablet + electrolyte drink). High sodium may cause nausea, reducing desire to drink.
Symptoms: weight loss, dry mouth, dry skin, cessation of urination, strong thirst, salty foods taste bad. Elevated heat stress risk.
Fix: plain water, restrict sodium; low-sodium carbohydrate sources if calories needed; sip small amounts of water for nausea. In heat slow down, wet clothing, douse with water.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hypernatremia subtopic:salt-tablets condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 6 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-038] Combination 7: Euhydrated and hypernatremic

Hydration normal, sodium high. Occurrence: extremely rare (<1%).
How: too much sodium in too short a time; salt tablets taken when not needed. Reason the author recommends food and fluid sources of sodium rather than salt tablets.
Symptoms: normal weight, strong thirst, no dry mouth, salty foods may taste bad but appetite generally normal, normal urination frequency but urine apple-juice color or darker. Heat stress risk not elevated.
Fix: restrict sodium, continue normal water; low-sodium carbohydrate sources if calories needed. Improves as sodium is lost via sweat and urine, but do not restrict sodium so long that you swing to euhydrated and hyponatremic.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hypernatremia subtopic:salt-tablets condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 7 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-039] Combination 8: Overhydrated and hypernatremic

Hydration high, sodium high. Occurrence: extremely rare (<1%).
How: hard to achieve; overconsume fluid and sodium, e.g., moving slowly in cool weather (low sweat rate) with large amounts of salty food, salty broth or high-sodium sports drinks; slow pace makes large volumes easier to tolerate.
Symptoms: high thirst, slight weight gain, salty foods taste bad, high urine output, puffy hands, possible confusion/poor decisions. Resembles overhydrated/hyponatremic (puffy hands, confusion, weight gain, high urine output); key differences: here you are thirsty, salty foods taste bad, urine has more color (still light).
Fix: restrict fluid to moistening the mouth (swish and spit); restrict sodium; low-sodium carbohydrate sources if calories needed. No increased heat stress risk.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hypernatremia subtopic:overhydration condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 8 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-040] Combination 9: Euhydrated and normonatremic (center square) and how to stay there

Hydration normal, sodium normal. Occurrence: common (>30%).
How: adjusting fluid and sodium appropriately for changing conditions.
Symptoms: weight stable or slightly low, within 3 percent of starting weight; normal appetite, sweating and urine output; moist mouth; no nausea.
How to stay: anticipate conditions for the next few hours. If fluid loss will accelerate (rising temperature, higher elevation, greater intensity), carry more fluid and gradually increase sodium (or at least carry sodium sources).
Preferable errors when moving forward: slight dehydration and slight hypernatremia. Toward overhydration/hyponatremia: nausea, puffy hands, bloating, sloshing stomach. Toward dehydration/hypernatremia: thirst, dry mouth, dry skin. The latter are more easily solved (water, wet skin for heat) and less likely to stop you completely.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all sport:running focus:hydration subtopic:hydration-sodium-matrix condition:general
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 14 Combination 9 | no page markers

### [TEU-C09-041] Using and switching between associative and dissociative focus

- Elite-only associative focus is not the goal; athletes need to move between associative and dissociative states depending on situation. More important in ultrarunning.
- Dissociative (counting, conversing with fellow runner) lowers perceived exertion (Fillingim et al. 1989; Johnson and Siegel 1992). Use in middle stages when fatigue creeps in and effort perception must be minimized ("I have a long way to go but I feel fine"); also when feedback like pain must be reduced (e.g., feet at Badwater 135, Western States 100).
- Caution: too much or reckless dissociation causes missed perceptual cues (tripping on rocks, hitting branches). Deploy only where useful.
- Associative: use at race start to pace correctly, late in a long climb, while eating/drinking (enough calories without overloading the stomach), fast technical downhills (precise foot placement), noticing labored breathing early on a climb and adjusting. Mostly temporary check-ins: minutes, up to ~1 hour for a very long technical downhill.
- Practice in training the skill of switching, then deploy on race day. Schedule dissociation at deliberate times (a time or feature such as a trail turn) so it occurs on your terms rather than when the mind wanders.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:associative-dissociative metric:rpe
evidence: study (Fillingim 1989; Johnson and Siegel 1992) + author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Associative and Dissociative Focus Strategies"

### [TEU-C09-042] Music is passive; still train active focus switching

Music/podcasts are passive dissociation/motivation. Because passive, using them in training does not teach the skill of switching between associative and dissociative strategies. Fine to listen, but carve out training time for active interventions.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:music
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 sidebar "What about music?"

### [TEU-C09-043] Outcome goals: three characteristics and wording rules

Properly set outcome goals: (1) describe the outcome, (2) are achievable, (3) are challenging.
- Must describe the outcome: can be times, places or actions; specific (time, placing) or broad (time range). Examples: place in the top third of the field; finish the race; run under nine hours for a 50-miler.
- Wording: never "I just want to finish". "Just" demeans the process and "I want" is not an outcome of the event. Correct: "Finish the race."
- Number: think simple and few; typically one outcome goal emerges after a few conversations; earlier iterations become process goals.
- A/B/C goals for a time/place: e.g. outcome 9 hours at American River 50 -> A 8:45, B 9:00, C 9:15 (all challenging but within normal performance range). Others: A 9:00 and B finish. Some have a single goal ("8:45 or bust"); the coach then checks it is reasonable and balanced with risk affinity, then gives 100 percent support.
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle level:all sport:running subtopic:outcome-goals subtopic:abc-goals
src: Ch 16 "Outcome Goals" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C09-044] The DNF: the author's view and when it is acceptable

Most experienced ultrarunners have at least one DNF. The author is never upset with an athlete who takes one. Three cases:
- Random acts (rolled ankle, off course): part of the game; dumb luck on race day.
- Lack of physical or mental preparation despite the athlete doing the work: the coach's responsibility.
- Athletes racing at the edge of capability: a DNF can be the right personal choice once an aggressive goal slips away, even if finishing remains possible (limping or a three-hour aid-station nap). No shame, as long as goals are clear, challenge/risk balanced, and goals crafted meaningfully; then you will make the right decision when facing a DNF.
tags: type:philosophy type:decision-rule level:all sport:running subtopic:dnf
src: Ch 16 "The Dreaded DNF" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C09-045] Sleeping during a race by event duration

Evidence is thin; this is educated guesswork. Sleep restriction (withholding sleep a few hours for at least a night) can impair endurance performance by as much as 4 percent; total sleep deprivation by over 11 percent (Cullen et al. 2019; Fullagar et al. 2015). Naps may curb deterioration to an unclear extent.
Author's three segments:
1. Races shorter than 48 hours: no sleep needed, provided sleep extension was done and the athlete responds normally to short-term sleep deprivation.
2. Races 48 to 72 hours: short 20- to 40-minute naps (trail-side or at aid station) close to sunrise on the second day and each morning after. Theory (pioneered by adventure racers): waking at sunrise aligns with circadian rhythm.
3. Races longer than 72 hours: at least 90 to 120 minutes of sleep, beginning during the second night; few can stay awake the whole event, and performance deteriorates so much that sleep outweighs the time lost.
General advice: sleep more rather than less, provided cutoffs can be made; extreme sleep deprivation can cost hours via poor decisions, forgotten nutrition, falls; performance preserved by sleeping, sometimes 10 percent or more per the literature.
tags: type:decision-rule type:recovery level:advanced sport:running subtopic:sleep subtopic:200-miler subtopic:naps
src: Ch 16 "Sleeping During a Race" | no page markers
evidence: study (Cullen et al. 2019; Fullagar et al. 2015); author-experience

### [TEU-C09-046] Crew and pacer decisions

- A crew is not necessary (comprehensive aid stations); if preferred crew cannot attend, the race will take care of you.
- Best crew: people who know you as a runner and person and have only a small amount of sympathy (willing to tell you to suck it up). Loved ones can but need not be crew. Wrong crew drains energy, makes errors, can cause a DNF.
- Align crew with goals first (outcome and process goals), then tactical details. Crew are not merely gel-handers; goals matter more than the number of potatoes at mile 30. Beginners rely on worksheets and minute-by-minute instructions for confidence; the aim is to progress to crew armed with an index card, a duffel of gear/nutrition and driving directions.
- Pacers (allowed in many 100K/100-mile races): give motivation and safety; also an introduction to the sport. Some undo the runner's race via personality conflicts, goal misalignment, lack of preparation. Use is personal preference.
- Success is always the runner's responsibility; crew/pacers catalyze, not linchpins.
tags: type:decision-rule type:principle level:all sport:running subtopic:crew subtopic:pacers
src: Ch 16 "Recruiting and Instructing Your Support Crew" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C09-047] ADAPT system for problem-solving in a race

Applies when the race goes badly (Tyson: "Everyone has a plan until they get punched in the mouth"). Most cutoffs are generous enough for a bad patch or two; greater fitness gives more bandwidth.
Steps: ACCEPT, DIAGNOSE, ANALYZE, PLAN, TAKE ACTION.
- Accept: accept things as they are (primary outcome goal may go out the window; may sit at an aid station an hour or take a trail nap; water bottle/pack as pillow). Emotion clouds judgment; acceptance moves you from past to present and enables rational action.
- Diagnose: quick, simple statement of what is wrong ("I have an upset stomach," "I am lightheaded," "I am frustrated"); more than one is fine; don't jump to analysis/planning, otherwise you solve the wrong problem.
- Analyze: where is the next aid station, time to next cutoff, tools/food/supplies/gear available; mental inventory.
- Plan: concrete steps from the analysis; may be simply get to the next aid station and figure it out; share diagnosis/analysis with crew to help; one step at a time.
- Take action: problems don't fix themselves; act deliberately.
Example, rolled ankle: Accept "My ankle will hurt, I'll be slower, fine." Diagnose "injured ankle." Analyze "60 min from next aid station, now ~90 min walking; not enough food/water; crew at next aid." Plan: walk to aid station, ask other runners for food/fluid, find medical help/tape/brace, crew helps. Action: walk now; ask passing runner for fluid.
Intentionally omitted: PREDICT (calculate only time to the next aid station; do not forecast the following aid stations or finish); RADICALLY CHANGE RACE STRATEGY ("ADAPT, not PANIC": small incremental changes beat drastic revision); GUESS (last resort; use crew, aid station workers, fellow runners).
tags: type:decision-rule type:methodology level:all sport:running subtopic:adapt subtopic:problem-solving subtopic:troubleshooting
src: Ch 16 "The ADAPT System for Problem-Solving" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C09-048] Suboptimal racing is OK (suboptimal ≠ bad)

- You can race four 100-milers in a calendar year (or in a few short months, as in the Grand Slam of Ultrarunning), but you cannot prepare optimally for each one.
- How suboptimal the third, fourth or fifth 100-miler in twelve months is cannot be known; it depends on personal physiology and psychology.
- Rule: if your racing values are to optimize the training process, limit your racing. Racing more is not bad; keep in mind the practical ramifications and drawbacks of choosing too many races.
- Suboptimal racing is fine as long as you identify the goals of each race during the training process.
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:race-frequency subtopic:race-emphasis
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 "Is Suboptimal Racing OK?" and Key Points

### [TEU-C09-049] No prerequisite races or prerequisite long-run distances for ultramarathons

- There are no prerequisite races necessary to succeed at any ultramarathon distance.
- The idea that you must complete a set percentage of goal distance in training comes from marathon training (the 20-mile final long run before taper). Nothing is magical about it, nor about running 50 miles before 100, or 30 miles before 100K. The single long run is not a prerequisite for success in marathons or ultras.
- Ideal if possible: very long runs reinforce pacing, nutrition and hydration strategies and give opportunities to face adversity and work through rough patches.
- But the physiology needed to finish an ultra is not significantly affected by whether you have completed one very long run. If one cannot fit in, focus on developing the cardiovascular system and on strategies to manage effort level and fueling; you can still successfully complete an ultramarathon.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:long-run-prerequisite subtopic:race-selection
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 17 "Prerequisite Training Distances for Ultramarathons"

### [TEU-C09-050] Recurring race-day rules across the major ultras (pacing and mental traps)

Patterns repeated across the race profiles:
- Easy-looking or fast early terrain causes too-fast starts (American River 50 first 25 miles, Javelina Jundred, JFK 50 towpath, Vermont 100, Comrades crowd). Rule: force yourself to go slow early; hold to the race plan and ignore people around you.
- Gradual, easy downhills late in the race wreck quads (Western States Cal Street, Hardrock Pole Creek/Telluride descent, Lake Sonoma short downhills, Leadville Sheep's Gulch/Hope Pass descents, Wasatch Irv's Torture Chamber and Catherine Pass, Comrades down years). Rule: hold speed in check on descents.
- Do not assume the hard part is over (JFK towpath with 35 miles to go; Leadville Boulevard at mile 97 - push until the finish line is visible).
- Aid station/party environments trap tired runners (Javelina start/finish, Wasatch Brighton Lodge "the morgue"): do not sit down; get food/gear and leave, or have crew wait outside.
- Groups of shared misery slow everyone to the slowest pace (Leadville miles 60-85): recognize it and accelerate out immediately.
- In competitive international fields (Western States), do not react to other racers' moves; stick to your own strategy.
- At altitude (Hardrock Handies Peak) gauge effort by exertion rather than speed.
- Where walking is more effective (Comrades climbs, Vermont late climbs), power-hike and save legs; ignore the stigma of walking on the road.
- Crew: plan only a few strategic crew meeting points plus drop bags where parking battles occur (American River, JFK, Leadville).
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running subtopic:pacing subtopic:course-strategy level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 (multiple races)

## C10 Applied Guidance

### [TEU-C10-001] Hydration is a higher priority than fueling

Hydration status and fuel availability fundamentally determine much race-day duress; hydration is far more important. Fueling errors are easy and fast to fix (gel, Coke, noodles; sugar gets into blood within minutes even with the "wrong" food). Hydration/blood-volume errors involve hormonal regulation and electrochemical gradients, are slower (hours to rectify) and the steps are complicated. Penalty is more severe: low energy means slowing and eating (at worst disorientation, nausea, vomiting); a drop in blood volume can mean hospital or death. Hence hydration, sodium and thermoregulation are the most important nutritional aspects to understand.
Water, sodium and carbohydrate must be considered simultaneously; the question "when to drink" starts with "what to drink". Debate "drink early and often" vs "drink only in response to thirst". Body-weight checks (used for years at Western States and Wasatch Front 100, since discontinued) illustrate how complicated monitoring is. Details in Chapter 13.
tags: type:principle focus:hydration sport:running level:all subtopic:hydration-sodium
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "Hydration, Sodium, and Thermoregulation"

### [TEU-C10-002] When to eat: bigger portions on slow uphills

Tolerance is better with less jostling and more blood flow. Smaller portions need less gut blood flow and less bouncing stomach content. Take bigger caloric portions during slower uphill hikes (most blood flow, least jostling) so food is digested before returning to higher intensity or jostling. Depending on climb length, finish eating at least ten minutes before the summit. Think about the ideal time before each cookie, pretzel or gel.
tags: type:protocol focus:meal-timing sport:running level:all subtopic:eating-on-climbs
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4 | "To eat on the uphills or downhills?"

### [TEU-C10-003] Using trekking poles in ultras

Poles aid propulsion and stability and redistribute some of the uphill and downhill force from the legs to the lesser-used arms. Use is a personal choice. Considerations:
- The more power-hiking and vertical change on the course, the more aid from poles.
- Train with poles for at least four weeks before the race. This builds arm skill, strength and stamina, and lets you decide whether to use them only uphill or on both uphill and downhill.
- In all but the steepest instances poles are less economical (more energy and oxygen). Consistent training with poles mitigates some of this penalty (Figard-Fabre et al. 2009).
- The penalty may be worth it if poles save the legs from muscular damage or localized fatigue.
- Uses: stability (up and down), propulsion (uphill only), and coping with downhill forces. Most benefit comes from learning all three.
- Pole size: with elbows at your sides and poles touching the ground, the elbow should be bent at 90 degrees or slightly more. Grasp the grip higher or lower as the situation demands. Alternative from research: 68 percent of your height in cm (Hansen and Smith 2009; Pellegrini et al.; source prints "2105").
- Propulsion: align and time the pole strike with the foot strike, step for step or on alternating steps, to maximize upper-body work.
tags: type:protocol sport:running level:all subtopic:trekking-poles
evidence: study (Figard-Fabre et al. 2009; Hansen and Smith 2009)
src: Ch 5 "Should I Use Poles?"

### [TEU-C10-004] GPS watch selection: accuracy, battery, recording rate

- GPS watches are the primary tool for tracking day-to-day ultrarunning training. Focus on features, not brands.
- GPS accuracy matters because training specificity depends on matching grades and surfaces to race day. GPS data quality depends on satellite signal strength, number of satellites acquired, antenna position in the watch, and how frequently satellite data are collected. Altitude accuracy also matters. Most traditional manufacturers do well.
- Battery life depends on battery quality/size and the amount/quality of data samples. Options to save battery: record at less frequent intervals or alter how GPS is acquired; the watch fills gaps with non-GPS movement data. This trades accuracy for battery (and sometimes price). Options to improve accuracy: use GLONASS and GALILEO satellites and other signal-strengthening tools, which reduce battery life.
- Recommendation: a one-second recording interval is necessary for any trail-running GPS watch (matters especially for intervals of just a few minutes; trees and terrain interfere with signals). In day-to-day training use the highest-quality data and most frequent collection rate. Lower-quality, battery-saving modes are acceptable especially for longer races.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:gps-watch metric:pace
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Tools to Track Your Training"

### [TEU-C10-005] Training log software: Strava and TrainingPeaks

- Third-party software companies do a better job analyzing device data than device manufacturers (Garmin, Suunto, COROS, Polar). The author considers Strava and TrainingPeaks the go-to platforms for endurance athletes (Final Surge is also named in Key Points as able to make watch data informative).
- Athletes need not analyze data as deeply as a coach; understanding total workload, work rate, fitness and fatigue plus personal feedback suffices.
- Strava's aggregation tool ("Fitness and Freshness") and similar Garmin tools are more rudimentary; athletes are better served by TrainingPeaks ATL/CTL/TSB.
- Track volume by time, not miles.
tags: type:principle sport:running level:all subtopic:training-software metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Logging Your Training"; Key Points

### [TEU-C10-006] Race-day cooling strategies

- Goal of during-event cooling (Bongers, Hopman, Eijsvogels 2017): rapidly remove excess heat to create heat-storage capacity; not so much lower core temperature as keep skin temperature low enough to maintain core-to-skin gradient so vasodilation sends blood to skin to release heat. Delicate "tightrope".
- Strategies (Tyler, Sunderland, Cheung 2013): ice on torso; ice neck and head (bandanas, collars, hats); ice peripheral arteries (ice in arm sleeves); loose, light-colored clothing; increase evaporation (dump water on self); drink cool fluids.
- Maintain hydration to keep the sweat response intact for evaporative cooling and keep core temperature below critical levels.
- Stopping/walking slowly cools you (running produces heat): can save a race by stopping 10, 20 or 30 minutes to take fluids and food with ice or cold water on the body.
- Even heat-acclimated athletes should use these.
tags: type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:cooling subtopic:heat-stroke-prevention phase:race sport:running focus:hydration
evidence: review (Bongers 2017; Tyler 2013)
src: Ch 7 "Race-Day Considerations"

### [TEU-C10-007] Postworkout cooldown / active recovery

- Ultrarunners with limited time sometimes shorten or drop warm-up or cooldown. From a recovery view, an active cooldown (light jog or walk for ten minutes at the end of the workout) is time well spent, especially after higher-intensity sessions.
- Rationale: muscle contractions help pump blood. Blood delivers oxygen and fuel, dissipates heat, produces sweat, redistributes lactate and removes waste. Demand does not drop to baseline when you stop the watch. Active recovery keeps circulation somewhat elevated and eases the return to baseline.
- Van Hooren and Peake 2018 (review): active recovery speeds blood lactate recovery and returns core temperature to normal faster (particularly with airflow over the skin). However, active recovery, including stretching, did not improve next-day performance in training or competition compared to passive recovery. Benefits come sooner but even out by the next day.
- Advantages are more pronounced after high-intensity training than EnduranceRuns.
- The author still has athletes do active cooldowns, mainly as a mental transition from the intensity of a hard workout to the rest of the day. No specific cooldown set is prescribed, since none is more useful. The performance penalty for skipping or shortening the cooldown is likely minimal.
tags: type:recovery type:protocol level:all subtopic:cooldown subtopic:active-recovery sport:running
evidence: meta-analysis (Van Hooren and Peake 2018)
src: Ch 8 "Postworkout Cooldown" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C10-008] Compression, massage and foam rolling

- All apply pressure. Compression clothing and pneumatic devices aim to aid venous return and reduce swelling. Massage aims to push out lactate/waste, increase blood flow, and address fascial adhesions. Foam rolling has virtually identical goals, with possible added neural effects on soreness from self-massage.
- Research shows no clear physiological benefit that consistently improves performance (Groner 2015; Wilke 2017; Zaleski 2016).
- Compression is effective after surgery, for bedridden people, or with clinically poor circulation. Healthy individuals do not need extra circulatory help.
- Lactate clears naturally whether or not you rub the muscle, and the clearance rate is not important for recovery. Fascial adhesions are plausible, but the benefit of breaking them has not been proven.
- They make people feel good, enhance mind-body connection, give a relaxing pause, and carry a big placebo effect. These benefits count (they reduce stress and aid readiness), but skipping them if you dislike them or lack access puts you at no distinct disadvantage.
tags: type:recovery level:all subtopic:massage subtopic:compression subtopic:foam-rolling
evidence: meta-analysis (Groner 2015; Wilke 2017; Zaleski 2016)
src: Ch 8 "Compression/Massage/Foam Roller" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C10-009] Cold therapies (ice baths, cold immersion, cryotherapy)

- **Use for heat:** before and during exercise in hot weather, ice baths, cold water immersion, ice vests, ice socks, ice slurry drinks and cold wet towels effectively lower skin and core temperature and prevent overheating. After exercise they can lower body temperature if necessary.
- **Recovery:** if body temperature is normal or only slightly elevated, they do not enhance recovery in a way that improves next-day performance.
- **Inflammation:** ice baths, cold water immersion and a few minutes in a chamber at -140°F lower skin temperature quickly and cause dramatic vasoconstriction in the extremities. Cold can lessen exercise-induced inflammation, but this is counterproductive for long-term adaptation because the response to training inflammation is integral to fitness gains (Peake 2017).
- **Analgesia:** numbs pain temporarily. For repeated bouts with limited recovery (e.g., track heats) it gives temporary relief but may reduce force output in later efforts. Less of a concern for ultrarunners, who rarely do multiple high-intensity sessions in a day.
- Ice and contrast baths are so traditional that athletes will keep using them, and people like them because they believe they work and for the ritual.
tags: type:recovery type:caution level:all subtopic:cold-therapy subtopic:ice-bath subtopic:adaptation sport:running
evidence: review (Peake 2017)
src: Ch 8 "Cold Therapies" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C10-010] Cannabidiol (CBD): author's position and caution

- CBD is a non-psychoactive phytocannabinoid from Cannabis plants (hemp and marijuana), unlike THC. The endocannabinoid system is thought to modulate neurons by inhibiting release of certain neurotransmitters. E.g., in epilepsy CBD may reduce seizures partly by reducing glutamate buildup (Grewer et al. 2009).
- It is marketed to athletes as a pain reliever, anti-inflammatory, sleep aid and anti-anxiety aid, i.e., a recovery aid. Research is still evolving. The author is skeptical: mechanisms are bioplausible, but there is not enough evidence of meaningful athlete benefit. If you believe it works and it makes you feel better, that may be enough for a positive effect on recovery, as with other debatable modalities.
- Reasons for caution:
  1. You don't know what you're getting. A 2017 JAMA study tested 84 CBD products from 31 companies: 21.4 percent contained appreciable THC and only 31 percent had accurate CBD amounts versus the label (Bonn-Miller et al. 2017). WADA raised the legal THC limit, but exceeding it, even accidentally, still means a ban. Look for companies that publish a third-party lab certificate of analysis (COA).
  2. You don't know who you're getting it from. Choose a manufacturer who can say where the CBD comes from. States with legal recreational/medical marijuana (Colorado, California, Oregon) have higher regulatory standards.
  3. It's marketed as a panacea, which is a red flag for CBD or any supplement.
- Key point: not enough high-quality research yet to say whether CBD is an effective, consistent recovery aid.
tags: type:caution type:evidence level:all subtopic:cbd subtopic:anti-doping focus:supplements
evidence: clinical-trial (Bonn-Miller et al. 2017); Grewer et al. 2009; author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Cannabidiol (CBD)" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C10-011] Nutritional supplements: evidence stance and AIS ABCD framework

- Supplementation is necessary to correct clinical nutrient deficiencies, but the consensus of reputable federations is that few supplements have solid performance-enhancing support (IOC Consensus Statement 2018). The 2016 position paper of the Academy of Nutrition and Dietetics, ACSM and Dietitians of Canada (Thomas, Erdman and Burke 2016): "Relatively few supplements that claim ergogenic benefits are supported by sound evidence."
- **2019 AIS Sports Supplement Framework (ABCD):**
  - **A:** "supported for use in specific situations in sport using evidence-based protocols."
  - **B:** "deserving of further research and could be considered for provision to athletes under a research protocol or case-managed monitoring situation" (might work, not proven).
  - **C:** "have little meaningful proof of beneficial effects"; the vast majority. AIS: "If you can't find an ingredient/product in Groups A, B or D, it probably deserves to be here."
  - **D:** performance-enhancing drugs and supplements "banned or at high risk of contamination with substances that could lead to a positive drug test."
- The author's main concern: supplements give false confidence and reduce vigilance about things known to work (food choices, sleep, fundamental training). Poor food choices will outweigh any supplement benefit. His athletes use supplements only after thoroughly investigating physiological necessity, motivation and anticipated outcome.
- Key point: the vast majority of supplements have little to no solid science for claims about performance, recovery or health.
tags: type:principle type:evidence level:all subtopic:supplements focus:supplements condition:general
evidence: guideline (IOC 2018; Thomas, Erdman and Burke 2016; AIS 2019)
src: Ch 8 "Nutritional Supplements" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C10-012] Nutrient timing: five rules the author gives athletes

- Nutrient timing is oversold. Like active recovery, the anabolic window gives faster early benefit but not a different outcome by the next day. Carbohydrate and protein within 30 to 60 minutes after exercise can raise the initial rate of muscle glycogen replenishment, but the window does not slam shut after 60 minutes. With adequate total energy and varied macronutrients, muscle glycogen is fully restored by the next day (see Ch 13).
- Research on breakfast, skipping breakfast, big dinners, fewer late calories, frequent small meals, infrequent big meals, or restricted feeding hours is mixed (some flawed or small). Collectively, none promotes better performance or recovery for everyone all the time.
- What the author tells athletes:
  1. Eating enough total energy each day matters more than how it is distributed.
  2. When possible, spread protein across the day rather than in one or two big feedings. Protein is used around the clock for recovery, adaptation and immune function, and unlike carbohydrate and fat cannot be easily stored.
  3. Rapid postworkout carbohydrate replenishment (glycogen window) matters more if there are fewer than 18 to 24 hours before the next training session or competition. Otherwise eat as normal and results are the same.
  4. Specific carbohydrate-availability strategies (carbohydrate loading, or training with low carbohydrate availability) can play a role but should not take priority over fundamental, high-quality, fully fueled training.
  5. The eating pattern that best fits lifestyle, preferences and performance is the one to keep. Try different patterns with an open mind and trust that what feels right probably is right.
tags: type:nutrition type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:nutrient-timing subtopic:glycogen-window focus:meal-timing focus:macronutrients condition:general metric:g
evidence: author-experience; reviews of mixed research
src: Ch 8 "Nutrient Timing" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C10-013] Weighted vests and hiking: guidelines

- Purpose: ultras involve much hiking; weighted pack helps deliver aerobic stress during hiking/walking, esp. for time-limited athletes in flat regions.
- Use your event running pack: more water in bladder, full bottles in front, heavier gear; more sport-specific weight distribution than a tactical vest.
- Don't overload: heavy loads cause forward bend and slower cadence, altering biomechanics and raising injury risk.
- Target <10 percent of body weight (generally under 20 lb) or just enough to induce a hike when you would normally run. Beginners start ~5 percent and adjust by tolerance.
- Above 20 percent of body weight (>30 lb for 150-lb runner) sport-specific benefit usually falls and injury risk rises.
- Downhill: be conservative with a pack heavier than race pack; downhill is more musculoskeletally stressful (Ch 4) and weight adds stress. Option: load extra water for uphills, dump it going downhill, repeat at streams/refills.
- Skip pushing sleds and pulling tires: biomechanics differ greatly from ultrarunning; for strength use heavy squats or reverse lunges (Ch 11).
tags: type:protocol sport:running metric:load level:all subtopic:weighted-vest subtopic:hiking
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "What about Weight Vests or Other Contraptions?"

### [TEU-C10-014] Corrective exercise training: rationale, candidacy, prescription

- Most common form of strength work in endurance community; akin to physical therapy; targets minor weaknesses/imbalances specific to anatomy, movement patterns and strength. Low effort cost, less time than traditional strength; little impact on quality of intervals and long runs; low-risk addition that "could" give minor injury prevention (unclear if equal to traditional strength for injury risk).
- Typical exercises: body-weight work, clamshells, banded walking drills, balance balls/unstable surfaces.
- Candidacy: nearly all runners could, but there is a time cost. If constantly injured, investing in a corrective program plus managing overall running workload is essential; seek a qualified physical therapist.
- Areas: posterior chain (glutes, hips, hamstrings): ball bridges, traditional bridges, banded glute work. Weak feet: barefoot balance work, foot stretches and activation, top-of-foot and calf stretches. Hips and pelvic stability: monster band walks, lateral step-ups, the pelvic list (as printed).
- Dose: alternating days, up to three days per week; work up to 2–3 sets of up to 12 reps or 15–30 seconds. Beginners: pick one exercise from each category and rotate exercises each session.
tags: type:protocol sport:running level:all subtopic:corrective-exercise metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | "Corrective Exercise Training"

### [TEU-C10-015] Train the gut: race-day intake rates must match training

The goal is supplying energy, fluid and electrolytes for optimal performance: too little causes underperformance; too much generally causes gastric distress. Many athletes take in e.g. 100 cal/h on long training runs and then try 200-300 cal/h in a race, equivalent to training at 10 min/mi and racing at 8 or 6 min/mi. Training should prepare the stomach for race-day stressors, so the recommendations below apply to both training and racing.
tags: type:principle type:nutrition level:all focus:gut-health focus:meal-timing subtopic:gut-training
src: Ch 13 "During-Workout and Race Nutrition"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-016] Carbohydrate during ultras: 60-90 g/hr and the author's reasoning

- Sports science recommendation: 60-90 g/hour for endurance activities >90 minutes, based on absorbing/using ~1 g exogenous carbohydrate per minute; with training and combined sources (glucose + fructose) possibly ~1.4 g/min (~84 g/hr).
- Emerging evidence that with gut training >90 g/hr can be used (Costa et al. 2017; Cox et al. 2010; Urdampilleta et al. 2020), but utility in ultrarunning is likely limited because demand is lower: lower caloric output and lower carbohydrate percentage than traditional endurance settings.
- Math: ultras are run at <65% VO2max (elites may climb at slightly higher; a 50K world record maybe 70%). Crossover at 65% VO2max is common in trained athletes, so ~50% of fuel is fat (nearly unlimited) and 50% carbohydrate; may vary ~10 points either way. A 70 kg (154 lb) runner at 9:40 min/mi (10 K/hr) on flat terrain uses ~700 cal/hr, so 350 cal/hr carbohydrate. With ~2,000-2,800 cal glycogen and blood glucose onboard (fed state), they could run over 5 h 42 min or 57 km without fuel (author: do not do this). Bigger or faster shortens it; slower or smaller lengthens it. Thus very high rates (up to 90 g/hr) are likely unnecessary.
- Higher intensity needs more carbohydrate; a very fit runner just beating cutoffs needs less.
- Author's advice: start at 60 g carbohydrate per hour and move up or down based on race intensity and tolerance.
tags: type:dose type:nutrition level:all focus:macronutrients metric:g subtopic:carbohydrate-per-hour system:fat-oxidation
src: Ch 13 "Carbohydrate"
evidence: Costa et al. 2017; Cox et al. 2010; Urdampilleta et al. 2020; author-experience

### [TEU-C10-017] Protein and fat during ultras

- Protein: limited role in energy; may help mitigate race muscle damage but findings are equivocal (Knechtle et al. 2011). In training ingest ~40 g protein every four hours throughout the day to aid muscle protein synthesis; particularly important when training runs exceed four hours.
- Fat: its purpose in ultra racing/training is food satiety. Ingesting fat will not materially add to fat oxidation. Most people cannot consume more than 30 g (270 calories) of commercial fat supplements like MCT oil without GI distress.
tags: type:nutrition type:dose level:all focus:macronutrients metric:g subtopic:protein subtopic:mct-oil
src: Ch 13 "Protein", "Fat"
evidence: Knechtle et al. 2011; author-experience

### [TEU-C10-018] Fluid consumption: why not drink-all-you-can or drink-to-thirst

- Two outdated/polar approaches: "consume as much fluid as tolerable" (driven by the ubiquitous 2% weight-loss-hurts-performance notion; likely contributed to hyponatremia cases) and "drink to thirst". Both fail in ultras.
- Some evidence athletes can lose ≥2% body weight with no performance decline (sometimes improved due to lower weight), but in training there is no benefit to exceeding 2% weight loss during workouts.
- In-race weighing is unreliable (error from sweat, dirt on clothing, scale on dirt exceeds the <2% change). Thirst perception is distorted (e.g., Badwater: dry hot air, sand, dust).
- Better solution: the sweat test (see C07).
- Weight-change checks pre/post workout exclude runs >3 hours, because water released from oxidizing fuel confounds results.
- Intensity affects sweat rate, but most ultras are near EnduranceRun intensity, so the sweat test is a good starting point.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all focus:hydration subtopic:drink-to-thirst subtopic:hyponatremia subtopic:dehydration-2-percent
src: Ch 13 "Fluid Consumption"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-019] Sodium: targets, sweat variation, salt tablets

- Sodium need is a function of sweat sodium loss; think in mg sodium per unit of fluid ingested. Individual variation is large ("salty sweater"). Commercial sweat sodium testing exists; work with a registered dietitian who works with endurance athletes.
- Do not replace 100% of sweat sodium: reserves exist, notably in skin. Best estimates: replace 50-80% of sodium lost in sweat; goal is close, not 1:1.
- Target: 600-800 mg sodium per liter of fluid consumed. Sodium need not be in the drink; plain water plus sports nutrition products or real food can meet it. Most high-quality sports drinks mixed per instructions land in range; if light, make up with products/foods.
- Salt tablets: generally unnecessary and can encourage overdrinking due to the fact that they create an "extremely hypotonic environment in your stomach (too much sodium, not enough fluid)" [Likely typo in source — text states "hypotonic" despite explaining "too much sodium, not enough fluid", which describes hypertonicity; recorded as printed]. Therefore, avoid them and get sodium from electrolyte drinks, gels, chews, and real food.
tags: type:dose type:nutrition level:all focus:hydration metric:mg subtopic:sodium subtopic:salt-tablets
src: Ch 13 "Sodium"
evidence: Baker 2017 (Figure 13.6); author-experience

### [TEU-C10-020] What is in my sports drink: osmolality, flavor, dilution

- A sports drink is water with solutes; fewer ingredients leave room for carbohydrate and sodium. Simplest drinks are best on the gut and aid transport of sugar/electrolyte.
- Osmolality (total molecular concentration of everything in the drink) affects mixture in the stomach; too high from additives may slow gastric emptying; lower osmolality tends to speed emptying and gets into the intestine quickly on an empty stomach.
- Primary ingredients: sugar, sodium, potassium, flavoring. Electrolytes and flavoring make you drink more frequently and more per drink.
- Light flavor preferable (strong flavor becomes overwhelming in a long slug); a drink that tastes almost watered down at rest tastes right when running. Slightly tart beats overly sweet; citrus flavors increase drive to drink; the drink should clear the mouth (no film).
- Rather than dilute, find a lighter-tasting drink to use at full strength. With high-quality drinks (Skratch Labs, Osmo, Clif Hydration) diluting is generally unnecessary and can be counterproductive.
tags: type:principle type:nutrition level:all focus:hydration subtopic:sports-drink subtopic:osmolality
src: Ch 13 sidebar "What the Heck Is in My Sports Drink?"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-021] Calories in your pocket, hydration in your bottles

For most runs a high-carbohydrate drink is unnecessary. Separate calories from hydration: bottle/pack serves hydration, pocket food serves fueling.
- Fluid and calorie needs change independently (hot: sometimes twice the fluid per hour of a cool run, e.g., cool morning into hot day).
- Example: fluids with ≥35 g carbohydrate per 20 oz give 35 g with one bottle and 70 g with two bottles in an hour, the top of the 60-90 g/hr range; more can cause gastric distress, which makes you delay drinking and dig a dehydration hole taking hours to recover. A caloric deficit is fixed within minutes by eating; rehydration takes far longer and dehydration hurts performance more.
- Separation lets you raise fluid in heat and lower it in cold without changing calories, and vary carbohydrate sources; food can supply much of the sodium, so the sodium target can be met entirely from food while drinking plain water.
tags: type:principle type:decision-rule level:all focus:hydration focus:gut-health subtopic:bottle-vs-pocket
src: Ch 13 sidebar "Calories in Your Pocket, Hydration in Your Bottles"
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-022] Key nutrition targets for runs greater than two hours

- 16 to 32 oz fluid per hour, adjusted to individual needs, environment and intensity.
- 200 to 300 calories per hour (~90% from carbohydrate), adjusted per tolerance; the first hour may be subtracted if a meal was just eaten.
- 600 to 800 mg sodium per 32 oz (about a liter) fluid.
Ranges are then adjusted to personal needs; fluid varies most with environment.
tags: type:dose type:nutrition level:all focus:energy-balance focus:hydration metric:kcal metric:mg subtopic:hourly-targets
src: Ch 13 "Making Sure You're Getting It Right"; Key Points
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-023] Preworkout nutrition

- The last full meal before training should be out of the stomach and digested before starting, especially before intervals (hard efforts are unpleasant on a full stomach).
- A relatively light meal rich in carbohydrate (preferably ~70% of calories); fat and protein stay longer in stomach. Examples: pasta, turkey sandwich, oatmeal with fruit.
- Meal size shrinks as workout approaches: 1.5 g carbohydrate/kg when final meal is three hours before; closer to 1 g/kg if training two hours after the last significant meal (see Table 13.2).
- Do not try to fill daily fiber requirement (AHA: 25-30 g/day to reduce LDL) at this meal; fiber slows digestion.
tags: type:nutrition type:dose level:all focus:meal-timing metric:g-per-kg subtopic:pre-workout-meal
src: Ch 13 "Preworkout Nutrition"
evidence: author-experience; American Heart Association (fiber)

### [TEU-C10-024] Postworkout nutrition and recovery drink rule

- Postworkout nutrition is "the most oversold concept in sports nutrition." The old 4:1 carbohydrate:protein recovery-drink ratio (Endurox R4) within 20 minutes is not needed; no laser-guided precision required.
- Start with full glycogen at workouts/races: ~1,600-2,000 cal glycogen in muscle and liver plus a bit in blood; carbohydrate is the only fuel realistically depleted in one bout.
- Glycogen window: first 30-45 minutes after exercise muscle cells are most receptive. But glycogen replenishment reaches 100% within 24 hours with high-quality meals.
- Use a specific recovery beverage only when BOTH: (workout >3 hours OR >2 hours with structured intensity) AND no access to a high-quality meal right after (e.g., an hour's drive home). Runs <2 hours (with or without intensity): recovery beverage not necessary; 2-3 hours with intensity or >3 hours: recovery beverage if a meal cannot be consumed within 2 hours (Figure 13.8). A shake because you like it is fine but is not substantially more effective than a balanced meal.
- Rehydration: within the first two hours drink 1.5 times the water weight lost, with ~500 mg sodium per liter of fluid (example: lost 2 lb = 32 oz, drink 48 oz within two hours). Applies to any run, not just those >2 h or with intensity.
tags: type:nutrition type:decision-rule level:all focus:meal-timing focus:hydration metric:mg subtopic:recovery-drink subtopic:glycogen-window
src: Ch 13 "Postworkout Nutrition" | Figure 13.8
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-025] Dietary manipulation strategies: ketosis, LCHF, periodized carbohydrate

- DIETARY KETOSIS: most restrictive; uses ketones for fuel in absence of carbohydrate. Originally a treatment for children with severe epilepsy, then adapted to sports/weight control. 75–85% calories fat, 15–20% protein, carbohydrate <50 g/day.
- LOW-CARBOHYDRATE HIGH-FAT (LCHF): less restrictive; 30 to 40 percent of calories from carbohydrate (definitions vary). Both contradict the usual 60–65% carbohydrate recommendation.
- PERIODIZED CARBOHYDRATE (PCHO): blend of ketogenic, LCHF and high-carbohydrate low-fat, plus approaching specific sessions with low carbohydrate availability.
