Use high carbohydrate availability (top up glycogen, fuel during workouts) for higher-intensity periods/workouts; do less intense workouts with intentionally low availability via diet manipulation, overnight fasting, or a low-carbohydrate day delaying post-exercise glycogen replenishment. "Best of both worlds": improves fat oxidation without limiting high-intensity work.

Table 14.2 Advantages and disadvantages (Adapted from Burke 2020; Tiller 2019; Wylie-Rosette 2016):
| STRATEGY | ADVANTAGES | DISADVANTAGES |
| Low-carbohydrate high-fat or Ketogenic (<50g CHO/day) | Increased fat oxidation, sparing endogenous glucose; lower need for exogenous carbohydrates during activity; decreased body fat percentage | Inability to train and race at higher intensities; less efficient at transporting carbohydrates across gut membrane; increased risk of bone-stress injuries from hormonal changes affecting bone remodeling; restricted food sources, specifically fruits and vegetables; can lead to low energy availability |
| High-carbohydrate low-fat | Consistently high training quality; trains the gut to facilitate more glucose absorption | Possible overreliance on carbohydrate as a fuel source |
| Periodized carbohydrates | Matches training intensity/duration to substrate needs; enhanced fat oxidation due to cellular changes in the muscle; no diminished training quality | Logistically difficult to implement |
tags: type:nutrition type:methodology level:all sport:running focus:macronutrients subtopic:periodized-carbohydrate subtopic:ketogenic condition:general
evidence: guideline (Burke 2020; Tiller 2019; Wylie-Rosette 2016)
src: Ch 14 "Dietary Manipulation", Table 14.2 | no page markers

### [TEU-C10-026] Nappuccino: caffeine before a short nap

If a 20- to 40-minute power nap is expected, consume caffeine just prior to the nap. The benefit lies in the delay for the stimulant to take effect; on waking you are more refreshed and alert. Findings not specific to ultras; modify for planned or unplanned trail naps.
tags: type:protocol type:nutrition focus:supplements condition:general level:all sport:running subtopic:caffeine subtopic:nap
src: Ch 16 sidebar "The Nappuccino" | no page markers
evidence: study (Hayashi, Masuda, Hori 2003; Horne and Reyner 1996; Reyner and Horne 1997)

### [TEU-C10-027] Race-day nutrition: strategy first, then (optional) simple plan

If training was done right, race nutrition should be very similar to training nutrition; a simple plan is as effective and easier to execute. Overcomplicated plans (food/fluid in 30-, 20-, even 10-minute increments, exact 83 oz over 21 miles) confuse crew; athletes don't train that way.
Strategy = target calories, fluid, and foodstuffs tried and tested in training; any written plan from it should be executable with an elementary-school education.
Table 16.1 (example of an OVERCOMPLICATED plan, not to follow):
| RACE SECTION | FOOD | FLUID | SUPPLEMENTS |
| Start to aid station 1 | 2 gels | 1 drink mix in bottle 1; 1 drink mix in bottle 2 | 1 salt tab; 1 amino acid capsule |
| Aid station 1 to aid station 2 | 2 gels; 1 energy bar | Water in bottle 1; 1 electrolyte tablet in bottle 2; Coke in aid station | 1 salt tab |
| Aid station 2 to aid station 3 | 1 pack energy chews; 1/2 pack energy chews | 1/2 drink mix, 1/2 scoop whey protein in bottle 1; Water in bottle 2; Ginger ale in aid station | 2 salt tabs |
| Aid station 3 to finish | 2 gels; 1 pack energy chews | 1/2 drink mix, 1/2 Coke in bottle 1; 1 electrolyte tablet in bottle 2 | 1 salt tab; 1 amino acid capsule |
Strategy components:
- Eat and drink what you have tried in training.
- Target calories per hour.
- Target fluid per hour, adjusted for event temperature range.
- Know when/how to use caffeine or stomach-calming tricks (antacids, ginger).
FLUIDS FIRST: nutrition depends on hydration status (Ch 13); with good hydration food can be absorbed/metabolized. Start with fluid by race section considering time between aid stations, temperature, intensity; calories in fluid set the start of the caloric range.
CALORIE RANGE: target a range rather than timed feeding; 200 to 300 calories per hour after the first sixty minutes for most people; some elites up toward 400 calories per hour (Ch 13). Specific foods are developed in day-to-day training.
tags: type:nutrition type:methodology level:all sport:running focus:energy-balance focus:hydration metric:kcal subtopic:race-nutrition-strategy
src: Ch 16 "Creating a Race-Day Nutrition Strategy", Table 16.1 | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-028] Bull's-eye and outer-ring foods in the race (Figures 16.12-16.13)

Use target calorie ranges in long training runs to experiment with foods and build a core group of three to five bull's-eye foods; also develop outer-ring foods as backup if core foods fail/are unavailable (typical fallback: aid station cookies, soup, fruit, sandwiches). Maintain lists of target and off-target foods. Test backup foods in training too. In the race, stay on target, rotating through the 3-5 bull's-eye foods as long as possible; move to outer rings when tired of them.
Customize by distance and effort: blood flow is balanced among digestion, cooling and working muscles. Higher intensity = rely on more easily digestible calories; longer/lower intensity = more complex foods. Example: only gels from bull's-eye in a 50K; gels and pretzels for a 50-mile; the entire rotation for a 100-miler.
Fig 16.12 example: Bull's-eye: vanilla gel (engineered, sweet); Koop's bacon & egg rice ball (real food, savory and salty). Second ring: mixed nuts, banana, Oreo cookies, energy [chews]. Outer ring: cashews, cantaloupe, chocolate chip cookies, other. Off target: turkey sandwich, yogurt, potatoes.
Fig 16.13 (shorter, more intense ultra): three foodstuffs only: bull's-eye vanilla gel and Koop's rice ball (real + engineered, sweet/salty/savory); outer ring banana, energy chews.
See library: Koop's rice ball recipes (Ch 13).
tags: type:nutrition type:methodology level:all sport:running focus:macronutrients subtopic:bulls-eye-nutrition
src: Ch 16 "Develop Your Bull's-Eye and Outer-Ring Foods", Figs 16.12-16.13 | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-029] Supplements and ergogenic aids: test in training; keep the list short

Anything planned for race day (caffeine pill, crystallized ginger) must be tested in training, even on a four-hour run when unnecessary. Have a set to turn to when needed because race duration exceeds training runs. Beyond real food, engineered sport foods/drinks and the items listed here, there should be no reason to use anything else. Warning: supplements are not FDA-regulated; may contain unlisted ingredients, listed ones may be absent or in different amounts, and may include substances prohibited by USADA and WADA.
tags: type:nutrition type:caution level:all sport:running focus:supplements subtopic:ergogenic-aids
src: Ch 16 "Supplements and Ergogenic Aids" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C10-030] Caffeine on race day: performance vs staying awake

Caffeine is a psychoactive drug and can enhance performance. Two uses:
PERFORMANCE ENHANCER: begin with normal morning coffee (1-2 cups if habitual). For races shorter than six hours: occasional caffeine up to 50 mg per hour for the entire race, mainly from caffeinated gels, chews, tablets, colas. Morning coffee covers the first two to three hours, so start supplementing after the second or third hour. Dose response is not linear: moderate doses likely give the same effect as higher doses (Graham and Spriet 1995; Pasman et al. 1995); be cautious and conservative.
STAY AWAKE/ALERT (events longer than twenty-four hours): treat as a stimulant; timing matters more than form (pills, colas, chocolate-covered espresso beans, energy drinks, sports nutrition). Normal morning routine incl. coffee; avoid caffeine at the start (noncaffeinated gels, drinks, foods). After midnight, when the sleep monster is expected, begin; doses as high as 100 mg/hr for three to four hours; cease shortly after sunrise, which helps reset circadian rhythms.
Anecdote: a runner's pack of twelve 200 mg pills = 2,400 mg, noted as risk of an "amped-up arrhythmic runner."
tags: type:dose type:nutrition level:all sport:running focus:supplements metric:mg subtopic:caffeine
src: Ch 16 "Caffeine" | no page markers
evidence: study (Graham and Spriet 1995; Pasman et al. 1995); author-experience

### [TEU-C10-031] Stomach settlers: ginger, antacids, hard candies

GI distress is a leading ultrarunner problem (Ch 4). Test settlers in training even with a calm stomach, to rule out unforeseen issues.
- Ginger: candy, crystallized, ginger beer/brew; used for nausea by herbalists, doctors, cancer patients, pregnant women. Research shows benefit or is inconclusive; small doses reported to have the most benefit (Lien 2003; Lohsiriwat 2010; Pongrojpaw 2007; Ryan 2012). Most generic aid-station ginger ales contain no actual ginger.
- Antacids (Tums or generic): eating raises gastric acid, which can overflow into the esophagus and irritate/damage the esophageal and stomach lining; calcium carbonate neutralizes it. Chew one or two at a time.
- Hard candies (peppermints, butterscotch): sucking motion thought to relieve distress; little scientific evidence; worth a shot in desperate times; adds sugar. Author's favorite: butterscotch.
tags: type:nutrition type:dose level:all sport:running focus:gut-health focus:supplements subtopic:ginger subtopic:antacids subtopic:hard-candy
src: Ch 16 "Stomach Settlers" | no page markers
evidence: study (Lien et al. 2003; Lohsiriwat et al. 2010; Pongrojpaw et al. 2007; Ryan 2012); author-experience

### [TEU-C10-032] Table 16.2 A simplified nutrition plan (example)

Focus on calorie and fluid ranges, not exact foodstuffs; replicates training (a five-hour training run, like the five hours between aid stations 1 and 3: 80 to 120 oz sports drink and water with 1,000 to 1,250 calories of gels, bars, rice balls, energy chews).
| RACE SECTION | FOOD | FLUID | SUPPLEMENTS |
| Start to aid station 1 (2 hours) | 100 calories total (1 gel) | 20-30 oz. total (water) | None |
| Aid station 1 to aid station 1 [sic; likely aid station 2] (2 hours) | 400-500 calories total (gels and prepackaged bar) | 30-50 oz. total (water and drink mix) | None |
| Aid station 2 to aid station 3 (3 hours) | 600-750 calories total (rice balls, gels, energy chews) | 50-70 oz. total (water and drink mix) | 1 salt tab |
| Aid station 3 to finish (2 hours) | 400-500 calories total (gels and prepackaged bar) | ~50 oz. total (water and drink mix) | Ginger chews or antacid if necessary |
Flexibility: e.g. 100 calories of a bar or equivalent chews.
tags: type:nutrition type:dose level:all sport:running focus:hydration metric:kcal subtopic:race-nutrition-plan
src: Ch 16 Table 16.2 | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

## C11 Recovery, Monitoring & Lifestyle

### [TEU-C11-001] ATL, CTL, TSB aggregation of training load

- Acute Training Load (ATL): seven-day rolling weighted average of daily TSS/rTSS/hrTSS. Chronic Training Load (CTL): same over forty-two days. Time frames can be customized.
- Training Stress Balance (TSB) = difference between CTL and ATL.
- Interpretation: highest CTL = most fit; highest ATL = most fatigued; highest TSB = most ready for performance.
- Example (Fig 6.1, Western States 100 athlete): CTL highest just before the race (plateau ~120-125 in May to early June), ramping fastest during the tempo phases (the most stressful phases); drops sharply after the race.
- Upload training files daily to chart these.
tags: type:monitoring sport:running level:all subtopic:atl-ctl-tsb metric:load
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Aggregating Total Training Stress" | Fig 6.1

### [TEU-C11-002] Monitoring improvement: Strava segments and NGP between efforts

- Tools give trends, not stoplight answers; judge fitness over weeks or months.
- Strava segments: user-created trail/road sections (create your own for common interval sections); times auto-recorded on upload and ranked. Do not try to set a PR every time; the trend line (Fig 6.2: times improving from ~21:00 in May to better than a 16:40 best effort line by September) indicates fitness, especially for frequently used segments. Privacy can be set.
- NGP between efforts (TrainingPeaks): create laps after upload and compare NGP of specific intervals within a workout and across days/weeks to gauge workout quality.
- In trail running, interval work should be done on trails; answering "am I fitter" needs more investigation than on roads.
tags: type:monitoring sport:running level:all subtopic:strava-segments metric:pace
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Monitoring Improvement" | Fig 6.2

### [TEU-C11-003] Heart rate variability (HRV) use

- HRV = variation in inter-heartbeat timing (R-R interval); window into autonomic nervous system function (homeostasis regulator). Fatigue makes beat intervals more regular (negative). Negative long-term adaptation associated with reduced HRV; fitness gain with increased HRV indices (Dong 2016).
- Establish a baseline: single readings mean little; trends matter.
- Effective readings: take upon waking first thing in the morning; prefer longer than the default 60-second measurement; clinicians use five-minute readings (cleaner).
- HRV is not cut-and-dried: increase not always good, decrease not always bad. Taper peaking, an artificial rebound value 48 hours after a hard effort, or an overworked immune system fighting illness can distort readings.
- Gather at least four weeks of data before making big decisions.
tags: type:monitoring sport:running level:all subtopic:hrv metric:hrv
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Heart Rate Variability (HRV)"; Dong (2016)

### [TEU-C11-004] Subjective feedback log

Most important tool the author uses daily. Record in training log consistently, at minimum: how you felt (hard, easy, particularly fast, etc.), challenges encountered, and significant nutrition strategies used. Uses: decide when to take a recovery phase (Ch 10), build nutrition plan (Ch 14), reveal patterns/trends; also raises in-run awareness, aiding mental development (Ch 15).
tags: type:monitoring sport:running level:all subtopic:subjective-feedback
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Subjective Feedback"

### [TEU-C11-005] Sleep: gold standard of recovery, research and effects

- Sleep fulfills all the criteria for effective recovery. Physiological repair and growth happen during rest, predominantly sleep. Sleep improves motor learning and cognitive function and supports emotional well-being. Lack of sleep lowers optimism and mood.
- **Acute performance:** early studies found a few days of a few hours of sleep restriction had little to no impact on strength and endurance measures. Fullagar et al. 2015 (review): skill and precision sports are affected more by sleep loss than endurance sports. One or two terrible nights: you can probably suck it up and get in a decent workout, and a poor night is no excuse for a disastrous race.
- **Coaching practice:** the author rarely changes a workout after one poor night unless there is a confounder (on the verge of injury or illness, end of a long training block, or other negative combination of objective and subjective measures).
- **Illness and injury (long-term matters most, via week-to-week consistency):**
  - von Rosen et al. 2016: sleeping more than eight hours per night reduced injury chance by 61 percent in adolescent elite athletes. Strength training reduces injury by about the same amount (Lauersen et al. 2018 meta-analysis). The studies are not apples-to-apples.
  - Cohen et al. 2009: adults sleeping fewer than seven hours per night for a prolonged period are nearly three times more likely to develop a cold.
  - Sleep under six hours may reduce the acute response to, and lifetime protection from, standard vaccines such as influenza and hepatitis B (Irwin 2015; Walsh 2018).
- Takeaway: sleep matters a little for acute performance, much more for long-term health, injury prevention and training consistency. Long-term habits matter more than last night, so treat sleep hygiene as habits to adopt.
tags: type:recovery type:evidence level:all subtopic:sleep subtopic:injury-risk subtopic:immune-function metric:volume
evidence: meta-analysis (Lauersen 2018); clinical-trial (von Rosen 2016; Cohen 2009); review (Fullagar 2015; Irwin 2015; Walsh 2018)
src: Ch 8 "Sleep" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C11-006] Steps for better sleep habits (room, bedtime, wake-up, morning routines)

**Step 1 – Set up your room: calm, cool, dark, quiet (Venter 2012).** This helps get to sleep faster and raises sleep efficiency. Cohen 2009: "participants with less than 92% efficiency were 5.50 times more likely to develop a cold than those with 98% or more efficiency."
- CALM: minimize clutter; remove TVs, handheld screens and bright alarm clocks.
- COOL: ideal room temperature 60°F to 67°F. It may feel cold but facilitates the natural body-temperature drop. Blankets in a cooler room establish and maintain sleep-conducive core temperature better than a warm room with lighter sheets.
- DARK AND QUIET: light has the biggest effect on telling the body when to sleep and wake. Use blackout curtains to reduce light and sound. Remove or cover indicator and power lights.

**Step 2 – Bedtime routine:**
- Consistent sleep/wake time with enough sleep (more than eight hours).
- Turn off electronics, and your brain, ninety minutes before bedtime.
- Blue light from phones suppresses melatonin, and blue-light filters only go so far. Bright lighting also suppresses melatonin, so lower the lights or partially darken the room before getting ready for bed.
- Instead of e-mail or Twitter, journal, read for pleasure, meditate, or take a warm shower just before bed.

**Step 3 – Wake-up routine:**
- Wake at the same time every day regardless of workout schedule or time-zone travel.
- If you stay up late, take a nap to maintain bedtime and wake routine. If you cannot nap, modify your wake time as little as possible so it stays consistent.

**Step 4 – Morning routine (seek light, move, nourish):**
- Seek light, preferably sunlight, on rising. If before sunrise, turn up lights where you get ready.
- Move for ten to twenty minutes first (workout, preworkout routine or chores) before reading the paper or heading out for a run.
- During or after that movement, take fluids (you are dehydrated after sleep) and your preworkout snack or breakfast.
tags: type:protocol type:dose level:all subtopic:sleep-hygiene subtopic:light-exposure metric:volume
evidence: clinical-trial (Cohen 2009); Venter 2012; author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Steps for Better Sleep Habits" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C11-007] Energy balance as second priority of recovery; RED-S

- After sleep, energy balance is next in priority. Total energy intake is the linchpin of adequate recovery and positive training response. Whatever the macronutrient composition, running performance will not improve significantly unless total intake supports basic bodily functions, daily activity and training expenditure.
- Problems arise from undereating, overexercising, or both. The mismatch often stems from efforts to lose weight or stay light, but may be more complicated than not eating enough after workouts. Pressure in lightweight-valued sports can drive negative self-image, disordered eating and overexercising.
- RED-S (Relative Energy Deficiency in Sport; see Ch 2, applies to males and females) poses the greatest risk to athletes who chronically or acutely undereat, overexercise or both. It was originally the Female Athlete Triad (amenorrhea, low energy availability, decreased bone-mineral density). The change reflects that energy deficiency affects both sexes with far-ranging consequences.
- Figure 8.2 (adapted from Mountjoy et al. 2018) effects of RED-S: decreased endurance performance, increased injury risk, decreased training response, impaired judgement, decreased coordination, decreased concentration, irritability, depression, decreased glycogen stores, decreased muscle strength. (Text also lists decreased bone mineral density.)
- An athlete need not have every RED-S symptom to be harmed. Under-supporting the workload with calories and nutrients loses both fat and lean muscle. Muscle matters for coping with the metabolic stress of long-distance running, so losing weight by losing muscle is a problem.
- Athletes who are too lean recover more slowly from hard efforts and have more immune stress, so they are more susceptible to illness.
- Diagnostic heuristic: when a runner does not respond to training, struggles to finish races or sessions, and constantly has small colds, GI bugs and nagging injuries, the author first looks at the nutrition program. The likely cause is too few calories for the workload.
tags: type:principle type:caution level:all subtopic:energy-balance subtopic:red-s condition:general focus:energy-balance
evidence: guideline (Mountjoy et al. 2018); author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Energy Balance"; Figure 8.2 | p. n/a

### [TEU-C11-008] Stress relief, SNS/PSNS and HRV

- Recovery is not mere absence of activity. Sitting at peace differs from sitting still while stressed or anxious.
- Psychological stress (life, career, relationships, finances, and anxiety or runaway thoughts about past or upcoming workouts or the event) adds to total stress that rest must balance.
- Stress leads to frequent or continuous stimulation of the sympathetic nervous system (SNS, fight or flight) and chronically elevated hormones such as cortisol.
- The parasympathetic nervous system (PSNS, rest and digest) unwinds SNS responses.
- HRV: higher variability between heartbeat intervals is the desired outcome, a sign that the PSNS is effectively modulating small changes in excitability. Low HRV signals poor recovery because the SNS is still overactive.
- Recovery modalities aim to restore homeostasis by activating the PSNS to dial back the SNS. The cortisol drop at sleep onset and the cool, dark, uncluttered, quiet room tune down the SNS and tune up the PSNS. Active recovery, yoga, massage, compression and cold therapies plausibly act partly via PSNS activation.
- The brain is the ultimate modulator of long-term SNS/PSNS balance. Effective tools include mental skills training, meditation, breathing exercises, counseling, time with a pet, or floating in a sensory deprivation tank. They are highly individual.
tags: type:recovery type:mechanism level:all subtopic:stress subtopic:hrv subtopic:autonomic-nervous-system metric:hrv
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Stress Relief" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C11-009] Key points for recovery (chapter summary)

- The two best things for recovery: more sleep, and total daily energy intake high enough to support total daily expenditure.
- Active recovery, nutrient timing, massage and compression, and cold therapies can make you feel better sooner, but within 24 hours recovery benefits are roughly equal to passive rest, sleep and adequate calories.
- NSAIDs are dangerous for endurance athletes. Opioids are effective analgesics with high risk of abuse, addiction and overdose.
- CBD: insufficient high-quality research on whether it is an effective, consistent recovery aid. Nevertheless it is here to stay.
- The vast majority of supplements have little to no solid science behind their claims.
- Sleep and energy intake are the priorities, and other modalities are optional comfort or ritual.
tags: type:principle type:recovery level:all subtopic:recovery-priorities
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Key Points for Recovery" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C11-010] Wearables (HRV, resting HR, sleep) in recovery decisions

Devices (Whoop Strap, Oura Ring) can add value, but do not "let the data tail wag the performance dog." Performance remains the best indicator of when to rest. Wearable information should only slightly nudge toward "yes, rest" or "no, keep training," not drive the process. Author has used such tools for decades, back to taking a waking pulse by hand.
tags: type:monitoring type:principle level:all subtopic:hrv subtopic:wearables subtopic:sleep metric:hrv
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 sidebar on HRV, RHR, sleep

### [TEU-C11-011] Taper tantrums (psychology of the taper)

Tapers improve psychological qualities (Hooper, Mackinnon, Howard 1999; Morgan et al. 1987; Raglin et al. 1996), yet many athletes feel they are losing fitness, doubt the process, get antsy, sluggish, irritable. This is normal. Extra time and energy get spent on fretting (repacking luggage, analyzing splits, second-guessing socks).
Management: trust your training (even if imperfect, conscientious work is enough); before the taper review the sheer quantity of training done over months so the last workouts are put in perspective; channel excess energy into deliberate, purposeful rest (as demanding as training, maybe more for ultrarunners), using the time previously set aside for training.
tags: type:recovery type:principle level:all phase:taper subtopic:taper-tantrums subtopic:mental
evidence: clinical-trial (Hooper 1999; Morgan 1987; Raglin 1996); author-experience
src: Ch 12 "The Taper Tantrums"

### [TEU-C11-012] Day-to-day hydration status: the WUT diagram (Figure 13.2)

Source: Cheuvront and Sawka 2005. Evaluate immediately after waking three things: Weight (W), Urine color (U), Thirst (T).
- One indicator suggests dehydration, other two normal: dehydration less likely.
- Two indicate dehydration: likely (W∩T, U∩T, W∩U).
- All three: very likely.
Details:
- Thirst: about 2% body-weight dehydration is associated with thirst (Kenefick et al. 2012), with wide individual variability.
- Urine color: clear to straw-colored is not suggestive of dehydration, but clear is not automatically ideal (also seen with hyperhydration or hyponatremia). Apple-juice color or darker suggests dehydration. Judge from the stream or a collection cup, not diluted toilet-bowl urine.
- Weight: weigh nude after urinating. Day-to-day weight is virtually unchanged; a 1-2% loss from one day to the next indicates fluid change, not mass.
Interpretation: 2 of 3 or 3 of 3 means fluid losses over the previous 24 h were not adequately replaced; usually total daily intake was inadequate (exercise, thermoregulation in daily life, respiration, normal losses), not necessarily the previous workout. Drinking copious fluid in the evening is not the solution (it raises first-morning urine volume and lightens color but largely passes through).
Use over a rolling three-day period. One day of minor dehydration needs no change. If consistent over 2-3 days, adjust habits (common with higher training load, warmer climate/altitude travel, season/weather change): increase water consumption throughout the day rather than guzzling at once, and follow in-training fluid guidelines.
Rationale: starting the day already 2% down means a 2% loss during a run is really ~4% dehydration.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule level:all focus:hydration subtopic:wut-diagram subtopic:urine-color metric:load
src: Ch 13 "Day-to-Day Hydration Status" | Figure 13.2
evidence: Cheuvront and Sawka 2005; Kenefick et al. 2012

### [TEU-C11-013] Witching hour: timing of slowest pace and dropouts

Hallucinations and the "sleep monster" cluster between 2 and 4 a.m. Brager et al. 2020 (Sleep Science), Fort Clinch 100 Mile Endurance Run: slowest pace and highest dropout rate occurred at the same time, about 3:30 a.m. (Zeitgeber time 20, Fig 16.11; average pace slightly below 16 min/mile; slowest near 21 min/mile; runners remaining: 100 percent early, ~42 percent at the end). Consistent with circadian research showing some people have more difficulty controlling core temperature when exercising in early morning hours (Muginshtein-Simkovitch et al. 2015). Performance declines in this window.
tags: type:mechanism type:monitoring level:all sport:running subtopic:circadian subtopic:sleep subtopic:witching-hour
src: Ch 16 "The Witching Hour", Fig 16.11 | no page markers
evidence: study (Brager et al. 2020; Muginshtein-Simkovitch et al. 2015)

### [TEU-C11-014] Sleep extension (sleep banking) before long events

Sleep deprivation cannot be trained like a muscle; overnight runs only build familiarity with the sensation and it is unclear they improve future performance. Author advises only one or two overnight runs at most, sometimes none (high toll, slim benefit).
Sleep extension: about one week of extending sleep by as little as an hour per night improves cognitive function and performance, and has a positive effect on performance (esp. cognitive) during sleep deprivation immediately following. Useful for 24+ hour events (decision-making, navigation, mood).
Protocol: research protocols use 1-2 weeks with ten hours in bed per night (Vitale et al. 2019); linear dose response up to ten hours (a habitual 7-hour sleeper gains from even +1 h for a week). Author's practice: athletes target nine to ten hours per night for two weeks before a big overnight race. Prefer going to bed earlier over waking later (not required). Nine hours per night for one week gives a moderate, tangible effect on staying awake and alert, provided the athlete was not chronically sleep-deprived.
tags: type:protocol type:recovery level:all sport:running subtopic:sleep-extension subtopic:sleep-banking
src: Ch 16 "Sleep Extension", "How to Do It" | no page markers
evidence: study (Vitale et al. 2019); author-experience

## C12 Adaptations by Population / Context

### [TEU-C12-001] Female athletes: participation context and research gap

- Written by Corrine Malcolm, following a framework by Nick Tiller, PhD (Tiller et al. 2021). Jason Koop notes limited experience with female athletes.
- History: 1928 Olympic 800m collapses led to decades of "too fragile" belief; women's 800m reinstated 1960; Olympic 1500m and Boston Marathon allowed 1972; ACSM consensus statement supporting women in competitive running (ACSM 1979); Olympic Marathon 1984. Women now over half of worldwide marathon finishes but only 23 percent of ultramarathon finishes (Knechtle and Nikolaidis 2018).
- Research historically used college-aged males; female studies take about twice as long and require precise control for menstrual cycle and birth control. NIH is pressing for female subjects.
- Terms (NIH): "sex" = biological, genetic, cellular, molecular differences (male vs female); "gender" = social construct of values inherited by society (men, women, nonbinary).
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:female-athletes subtopic:research-gap sport:running
evidence: study (Knechtle and Nikolaidis 2018; ACSM 1979)
src: Ch 2 "Special Considerations for Female Athletes"

### [TEU-C12-002] Female physiology: performance gap, substrate utilization, fatigability

- Up to the marathon, VO2 max, lactate threshold and running economy explain much of the ~10 percent male-female performance difference, but are less impactful in ultramarathons (Davies et al. 1979); other attributes (substrate use, fatigue resistance, muscle structure, hormones, GI function, decision-making) matter more. Figure 2.3 (Joyner 2017): early gap narrowing in elite marathon times is attributed to training and culture (more/harder training, globalization, professionalization); the remaining gap to sex differences (body composition, total body hemoglobin, muscle mass, stroke volume, VO2 max).
- Substrate use: longer/lower-intensity races rely more on free fatty acids, sparing limited carbohydrate and delaying the "bonk". Endurance-trained females show 24 to 56 percent greater fat oxidation than males, even at rest (Melanson et al. 2002); use more fat as % of energy expenditure, higher fat oxidation relative to body weight, and reach maximal fat oxidation at higher intensity. Reasons: greater expression of fatty-acid metabolism genes including CD36 (Kiens 1985; Monaco 2015), and more estrogen, which may enhance lipid oxidation and downregulate glucose use (Isacco 2016). One reason "fat-adapted" strategies are generally ineffective for females: they are already efficient fat burners (plus potential negative health effects).
- Figure 2.4 (Venables, Achten, Jeukendrup 2005): fat oxidation rate (mg/kg fat-free mass/min, axis 0–9) vs intensity (%VO2 max 30–90). Inverted U; peak for men ~45% VO2 max, for women ~52%; women's curve higher overall, higher peak, reaches zero at higher intensity.
- Fatigability: neuromuscular fatigue (diminished ability to voluntarily activate/contract muscle; Millet 2011). At submaximal intensities females typically outperform males (Hicks 2001; Hunter 2014), via fiber type, muscle mass, neuromuscular control. Females have higher % type I fibers (Staron 2000); males more glycolytic type II (Welle 2008) and larger fibers (Miller 1993), giving less blood flow per fiber at submaximal contractions, more fatigable. Theorized female advantage in long events.
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:female-athletes subtopic:fat-oxidation subtopic:fatigability system:fat-oxidation sport:running
evidence: study (Melanson 2002; Venables 2005; Hunter 2014; Staron 2000)
src: Ch 2 "Substrate Utilization", "Fatigability and Muscle Structure", Figures 2.3–2.4

### [TEU-C12-003] Female athletes: GI distress and hormones

- GI distress is one of the top reasons for dropping out of an ultra, next to undertraining (Hoffman 2011); includes nausea, vomiting, cramping, reflux, bloating, diarrhea. Causes generally multifactorial (Chapter 14): more food/fluid than practiced, running harder than trained, failing thermoregulation.
- Females report more GI distress: ~10 percent smaller stomachs (Cox 1945), slower transit time (Probert 1993) and slower gastric emptying (Mori 2017), thought related to estrogen and progesterone (Datz 1987); not fully understood. Practical: training the gut is important.
- Hormones: estrogen, progesterone, testosterone act on nonreproductive tissue (bone, tissue, muscle). Estrogen affects elasticity of muscles/connective tissue, thought to raise risk of connective tissue injury (e.g., ACL) (Joyner 2017); running economy may fluctuate across the cycle (Casey 2014; Fletcher 2013).
- Testosterone: leading factor in male-female performance gap, mostly in speed/power rather than endurance; builds muscle mass/strength, lowers body fat, raises hemoglobin (Bhasin 2001), increases protein synthesis (Griggs 1989). Higher testosterone associated with impulsiveness/risk-taking (Goudriaan 2010); males tend to overestimate ability and slow more over a race (Hubble 2016); females on average pace better (speculative).
tags: type:mechanism level:all subtopic:female-athletes subtopic:gi-distress subtopic:hormones sport:running focus:gut-health
evidence: study (Hoffman 2011; Cox 1945; Probert 1993; Mori 2017)
src: Ch 2 "Gastrointestinal Distress", "Hormone Regulation"

### [TEU-C12-004] Menstrual cycle and training

- Cycle: fluctuating estrogen and progesterone about every 28 days; length varies widely, 21 to 40 days. Three common sequential phases: early follicular (low estrogen, low progesterone), ovulatory (high estrogen, low progesterone), mid-luteal (high estrogen, high progesterone).
- Figure 2.5 idealized 28-day cycle: Days 1–5 early follicular; 6–12 late follicular; 13–15 ovulation; 16–19 early luteal; 20–23 mid-luteal; 24–28 late luteal. Estrogen peaks ~day 12–13, falls at ovulation, second lower peak ~day 21–22, baseline by day 28. Progesterone low until ~day 15–16, peaks ~day 21–22, baseline by day 28.
- Women are most likely to report negative symptoms when estrogen is lowest (early follicular) and often feel best right after as estrogen rises. Estrogen: regulates and spares glycogen, protects against muscle damage and reduces inflammation (faster recovery), increases voluntary neural activation (McNulty 2020). Progesterone is believed to dampen estrogen, so in the luteal phase the boost is less.
- Research has high variance and methodological issues; the current recommendation (McNulty 2020) is to train and compete independent of fixed phases, in as personalized a way as possible: track your cycle and keep a log of how you feel and perform by phase, find trends, adjust training. Example: Malcolm is more prone to GI distress in the early follicular phase, so goes into training/racing ready to troubleshoot.
- Summary: possible female advantages in ultras: greater fatigue resistance, more type I fibers, better lipid oxidation, better pacing. Possible disadvantages: more GI distress, hormonal effects on metabolism and tissues.
tags: type:monitoring type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:menstrual-cycle subtopic:female-athletes metric:rpe
evidence: study (McNulty 2020)
src: Ch 2 "Menstrual Cycle", Figure 2.5

### [TEU-C12-005] Birth control considerations for female athletes

- Oral contraceptives: usually 21 days active + 7 days contraceptive-free. Exogenous estrogens/progestins chronically downregulate the hypothalamic-pituitary-ovarian axis (Elliott-Sale 2020). The daily pill creates daily surges (mini pseudo-phases) and a chronic hormonal profile closest to early follicular (low estrogen and progesterone), the phase with greatest negative performance impact. Consensus: slightly impaired performance versus naturally menstruating females; consistent with oral contraceptives lowering VO2 max by ~4.7 percent (Lebrun 2003).
- Oral contraceptives may be the best treatment for: dysmenorrhea, menorrhagia, symptomatic fibroids, functional ovarian cysts, benign breast disease, polycystic ovarian syndrome, premenstrual syndrome, pelvic inflammatory disease, and to reduce ovarian and endometrial cancer risk. Symptom relief may far outweigh the small performance decrease; balance with individual goals.
- IUDs: hormonal (low-dose progesterone) or nonhormonal (copper); good for three to eight years; hormonal IUD keeps hormone localized in the uterus so does not affect circulating hormones like oral contraceptives.
- Either method can mask amenorrhea: pills give a "fake period"; hormonal IUDs cause ~20 percent of females to lose bleeding. This removes a warning sign of poor health, especially relevant with RED-S risk. Speak with primary care provider or OB-GYN with any doubt.
tags: type:caution type:principle level:all subtopic:birth-control subtopic:female-athletes metric:power
evidence: study (Elliott-Sale 2020; Lebrun 2003)
src: Ch 2 "Birth Control Considerations"

### [TEU-C12-006] Menopause and perimenopause: physiology

- Perimenopause ("menopause transition") often begins mid-forties (between 36 and 45 on average); ovaries make progressively less estrogen, starting on average four to five years before menopause (complete cessation of period). In the last one to two years estrogen drops faster, bringing hot flashes, fatigue, mood swings, disrupted sleep, increased subcutaneous and intramuscular fat, and performance decline (male decline starts later with testosterone).
- Decline is tied to falling estrogen, progesterone, testosterone; accelerated atrophy and loss of skeletal muscle (mainly type 2 fibers), reducing oxygen utilization; VO2 max drops 5 to 10 percent per decade after age 30 (Hawkins and Wiswell 2003).
tags: type:mechanism level:masters condition:general subtopic:menopause subtopic:perimenopause stage:perimenopause stage:postmenopause system:vo2max
evidence: study (Hawkins and Wiswell 2003)
src: Ch 2 "Menopause"

### [TEU-C12-007] Menopause: practical recommendations

- Sleep hygiene: declining estrogen/progesterone impair falling and staying asleep; melatonin and cortisol changes disrupt night temperature regulation. Exercise early in the day; sleep in a cool, dark room away from screens; avoid alcohol before bed.
- Lift heavy things: muscle loss starts sooner in women; falling estrogen and testosterone/growth hormones make building muscle harder. Add ergogenic strength training (Chapter 11) with adequate postexercise protein; also helps bone density and boosts testosterone naturally.
- Watch the heat: hot flashes send blood to the skin rather than muscles/stomach; sweating begins later in exercise with age, so more heat storage early in runs (consider a longer warm-up before hard efforts); thirst dulls with age, so be more deliberate about hydration in training and racing.
- Fructose: declining estrogen increases sensitivity to carbohydrates and insulin, and fructose absorption declines, risking GI problems mid-race. Avoid foods with added fructose; use simpler sugars such as glucose, dextrose, sucrose.
- Rest more: recovery takes longer with age; alter periodization, e.g., a ten-day training block instead of seven-day week, or more recovery weeks, moving from 3:1 or 4:1 ratio to 2:1.
- Eat more protein: lower hormones reduce insulin sensitivity and reliance on carbohydrate and increase muscle catabolism; maintaining lean mass depends on timing of protein in the post-activity window and lifting heavy.
tags: type:decision-rule type:nutrition level:masters condition:menopausia stage:perimenopause stage:postmenopause subtopic:menopause subtopic:sleep subtopic:strength subtopic:fructose focus:macronutrients focus:hydration
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Menopause" subsections

### [TEU-C12-008] Women and fatigue resistance in ultras

Early statistics suggested women might outperform men as distance increases (Bam 1997); later analyses contradicted this (Peter 2014; Zingg 2014). At 2012 UTMB (shortened to 100K), men and women showed similar central fatigue and muscle damage/inflammation (CK, C-reactive protein, myoglobin), but men had more peripheral fatigue: greater loss of voluntary force in knee extensors and evoked mechanical response in plantar flexors (Temesi 2015). Women fatigued less in those muscle groups. Author: does not yet mean women are better ultrarunners, but the door is open.
tags: type:evidence sport:running level:all subtopic:sex-differences
evidence: study (Temesi et al. 2015)
src: Ch 4 | "Are women more fatigue-resistant than men?"

### [TEU-C12-009] The time-crunched ultramarathoner and the minimum maximum

- Most athletes overestimate time needed and linearly expand marathon training (e.g. 8 h/week marathon becomes 16 for a 50-miler). The relationship is not linear: a 50-miler needs no twice the training of a marathon, nor a 100-miler twice a 50-miler; most people devote similar time irrespective of distance, which demands high-quality structure.
- Minimum maximum: minimum time you must devote during your period of highest training volume; a reference for what to expect on race day and whether the distance is reasonable. Needed only in key weeks:
 - 50K and 50 miles: 6 hours per week for three weeks, starting six weeks before the goal event.
 - 100K and 100 miles: 9 hours per week for six weeks, starting nine weeks before the goal event.
- Outside this period lower volume can succeed if training quality is higher. Not a guarantee, but failing to reach the minimum maximums can lead to failure and underperformance. With the time, success depends on how effective training is.
- Use when setting season goals.
tags: type:threshold sport:running metric:volume level:all subtopic:minimum-maximum subtopic:time-crunched
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 "The Time-Crunched Ultramarathoner"

### [TEU-C12-010] Sleep deprivation training for 200-mile races

- 200-milers (Triple Crown of 200s: Bigfoot 200, Tahoe 200, Moab 240; Tor des Géants) are not simply longer ultras in training, preparation or strategy; sleep deprivation eventually affects nearly all runners.
- Anecdotal strategies: overnight sessions, training after short sleep, training after 24 h awake. Little if any scientific evidence that they improve tolerance to lack of sleep; at most familiarization with running overnight on little sleep.
- Costs: quality of sleep-deprived sessions compromised ~2 to 10 percent (Oliver et al. 2009; Roberts et al. 2019; Skein et al. 2011); training before/after must be altered for rest; possible greater injury and illness vulnerability.
- Author's view: normal day-to-day training better maximizes capacity. An overnight session or two may be warranted to test lighting/equipment and get familiar with prolonged headlamp running. Sleep strategies for 200-milers: Chapter 16.
tags: type:decision-rule type:caution sport:running level:advanced subtopic:sleep-deprivation subtopic:200-mile
evidence: clinical-trial (Oliver 2009; Roberts 2019; Skein 2011) / author opinion
src: Ch 10 sidebar

### [TEU-C12-011] Aging athletes and strength training

- Age-related muscle loss starts somewhere north of 40 and accelerates rapidly past 60. Age-related VO2 max decline is directly related to muscle mass loss, a main culprit in declining performance.
- For aging athletes strength training is essential for health and well-being, not just corrective or ergogenic; building/maintaining muscle mass is of utmost importance and should be factored into weekly training availability.
- Younger athletes may let strength take a back seat much of the year; as we age, shift some priority from running time toward moving heavy objects a few times a week.
tags: type:principle level:masters sport:running subtopic:sarcopenia subtopic:aging
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | "Aging Athlete Considerations"

### [TEU-C12-012] Cross-training: when it makes sense

- If you only have time to run, run. Yoga, CrossFit, Zumba, cycling may make a more well-rounded athlete but probably not a better ultramarathoner; running is what improves running. Fitness and specificity matter most on race day.
- Reasonable uses: active injury (cannot run), returning from injury, goal races far off, workload-capped runners (bike, skis or pool to add training), or simply enjoyment (once or twice a week bike is fine) with no expectation of automatic running improvement.
- Translating yoga, swimming, rowing etc. to faster trail running is "a stretch".
tags: type:decision-rule sport:running level:all subtopic:cross-training subtopic:workload-capped
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 11 | "A Place for Cross-Training"

### [TEU-C12-013] Goal-setting for a first ultra or first 100-mile race

A first ultra and first 100-miler are special: the goal-setting should revolve around getting to the finish line at all costs; time, place and performance are secondary. Leadville Trail 100 finish rate often ~50 percent or lower; author attributes this not to altitude or climbing ("crybaby excuses") but to no entry prerequisites, many rookies, and lack of purpose rather than lack of fitness/training (e.g. dropping at the mile-75 Outward Bound aid station). Far more than 50 percent have the fitness to finish.
Author's own 2008 Leadville: single outcome goal (finish, with a ring in his pocket as a constant reminder), no B/C/D goals. Process goals: run conservatively, take time in aid stations, enjoy pacers' company, take additional warm gear; aim to finish in any time under the 30-hour cutoff.
tags: type:principle level:beginner sport:running subtopic:first-ultra subtopic:first-100-miler subtopic:purpose
src: Ch 16 "Goal-Setting in Your First Ultra or First 100-Mile Event" | no page markers
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C12-014] American River 50: course, weather, crux, training and crew

Early April, point-to-point Folsom Lake to Auburn CA; mostly flat first 25 miles ("a road marathon followed by a trail run"); PR course and common first ultra. Aid stations approx. miles 3, 12, 17.5, 20.5, 25, 30, 38, 41, 43.5, 48 (crew-accessible: 3, 20.5, 25, 30, 41). Pacers allowed from mile 24.31 (Beal's Point).
- Weather: warm, not hot (e.g., 75°F) but runners aren't heat acclimated; rain unlikely but spring storms can last all day. Heat peaks in the final section.
- Equipment: trail shoes for the second half; headlamp for first 30 minutes (dump at first aid station). Carry two water bottles (some aid stations over an hour apart).
- Crux: last five miles = 1,500-ft climb, technical; few are psychologically prepared after cruising 45 miles; some fail to finish though they could.
- Mental: failures come from believing it is easy; loping at marathon pace early. Force slowing in first 25 miles. Profile notes: take small descents near miles 7, 9.5, 21 easy; patience miles 28-45; save legs for last 5 miles.
- Training: prepare for technical downhills (speed + fatigue overmatch agility). Tune-up for later summer ultras: VO2 max work in months before is fine. Goal event/first ultra: SteadyStateRun work in the 4-8 weeks before.
- Crew: access easy but parking is the problem; crews underestimate travel/parking time; pick only strategic aid stations.
tags: type:methodology sport:running phase:build subtopic:american-river-50 level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 American River 50

### [TEU-C12-015] Badwater 135: heat, gear, training and crew guidance

Death Valley (280 ft below sea level) to Whitney Portal (8,300 ft); end of July, highs regularly >110°F, all pavement, three mountain ranges, 35 miles longer than 100s. Field capped at 100; qualify with three 100-mile races, most recent within preceding 12 months (Badwater's Salton Sea or Cape Fear finishers get special consideration). Original 1977 race finished atop Mt Whitney (14,505 ft), 8 miles farther and 6,205 ft higher than the current finish.
- Weather: temps can top 130°F, above 100°F much of race (about 70°F on passes at night). Night start now, still hot. Headwind gusts 25-30 mph out of Stovepipe Wells at mile 41.5: use saline solution inside nose, steady lozenges; power-hike if winds are up.
- Run on the cooler white stripe at the road shoulder (black asphalt can melt shoes/blister feet).
- Equipment: clothes that stay wet for evaporative cooling (avoid fastest-drying wicking fabrics); ice sleeves; hat refilled with ice every couple of miles. Indispensable: lots of ice and high-quality coolers (few ice sources).
- Crux: last half-marathon Lone Pine to Whitney Portal, 4,573-ft climb starting mile 122 after >100 miles. Profile notes: easy on descent into Panamint Springs; the climb to Darwin seems endless - be patient.
- Mental: duration and sleep deprivation (possibly three consecutive nights), not temperature alone; exposure time breaks down thermoregulation.
- Pro (Dean Karnazes): more than 60% of training miles should be on pavement (trail runners can do 75% on dirt).
- Training: unlike most hot races ("minimum effective dose" heat acclimation), do many training runs in hottest part of day (gear testing). Heat acclimation strategy in Chapter 7. Build fitness and peak mileage 6-8 weeks out; do not run longest/hardest runs while acclimating.
- Crew: more crew members than runners need medical attention for heat/exhaustion; crew need own hydration/rest schedules; minimum two crew members; crews may help anywhere (except restricted sections) if all four tires are off the main road; pacers may only run behind the runner; read the extensive rule book.
tags: type:methodology sport:running system:aerobic subtopic:badwater-135 subtopic:heat level:advanced
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Badwater 135

### [TEU-C12-016] Comrades Marathon: pacing and training guidance

June, 55-mile point-to-point Durban-Pietermaritzburg, alternating up and down years; ~25,000 runners; road-marathon style aid stations every 2k (43 total), corrals, chip timing. Entry by strict qualifying times (marathon, ultra distances, Ironman marathon leg); qualifying time sets seeding. Oldest ultra (first run 1921); prize purse over $250,000. Up-year highest point near mile 51 (~2,800 ft); down-year highest point near mile 20.
- Weather: mid-June, usually favorable; slightly warm for cold-region runners; no special acclimation needed, but running in afternoon sun in last few weeks can help.
- Mental: easy to be sucked into the crowd and run too hard; in down years save legs. Pace plan from predicted finish time; here rely on watch and splits more than perceived exertion.
- Training, up years: like a flatter 50-mile/100K trail ultra; April-May TempoRuns, maybe SteadyStateRuns for back-of-packers. Trail runners: add road running 3 times per week in last several weeks.
- Training, down years: race pace in portions is faster than normal EnduranceRun or even SteadyStateRun pace, so TempoRuns up to the taper; choose benign trails to keep footspeed; specific downhill work sparingly: three or four sessions total in last six weeks.
- Mid/back-of-pack: some climbs better walked; power-hike and save legs.
tags: type:methodology sport:running phase:build subtopic:comrades subtopic:downhill-running level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Comrades Marathon

### [TEU-C12-017] Hardrock 100: course, gear, training and crew guidance

Mid-July, loop from Silverton CO over remote high passes incl. 14,048-ft Handies Peak; direction clockwise in even years, counterclockwise in odd years; kiss the Hardrock stone to finish. Entry: qualifying ultra; only 47 slots for first-timers; service requirement of 8 hours volunteer work at an ultra. Good time is under 30 hours (cutoff for easier 100s).
- Weather: subfreezing nights up high, mid-70s in Ouray; monsoon season afternoon thunderstorms, extreme temperature drops, even snow; storms usually move quickly. Exposed ridgelines: if lightning is seen/heard nearby, descend to lower elevation as quickly and safely as possible.
- Equipment: packable waterproof breathable rain jacket at all times; plan for two nights out; complete change of clothes in every drop bag; trekking poles on downhills. Indispensable: emergency space blanket (to wait out a storm before a pass).
- Crux: Handies Peak altitude (often at night in clockwise years); gauge effort by exertion rather than speed. Counterclockwise crux: Oscar's Pass and Grant Swamp Pass (~miles 80, 84). Clockwise: if moving well miles ~77-99, you will have a good race.
- Pro (Missy Gosney): counterclockwise, watch pace through Pole Creek to Sherman (~miles 20-29); clockwise, same for the descent off Wasatch saddle into Telluride (jeep road descent easy to overrun) - quads blown by going too fast.
- Course poorly marked by design: know the course; pay attention to location (pacers too).
- Training: bank vertical: long runs with 4,000-5,000 ft climbing/descending on successive days; run at altitude to understand response (not necessarily to acclimate). Critical phase May-June: lots of hiking and descending; most can't match 680 ft change per mile; nearly all uphills are hikes even for winners.
- Crew: only at designated aid stations, some without cell service; lots of mountain/Forest Service road driving. Pacers pack for up to ten hours, enough water/food; few bailouts.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:hardrock-100 subtopic:altitude subtopic:hiking level:elite
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Hardrock 100

### [TEU-C12-018] Javelina Jundred: course, pacing, training and crew guidance

Late Oct (near Halloween), McDowell Mountain Regional Park AZ; 100K or 100-mile; 20-mile loops, direction reversed each lap; 600+ runners; qualifier option for Western States. Crew only at start/finish (camp-style party). Finishing rate roughly 50%. Never more than 6.5 miles from the next aid station; shuttle from off-site parking. Pacers may join after sunset on day one or after the third loop, whichever first.
- Weather: nights high 50s-low 60s, daytime mid-90s, rain rare. Challenge: northern runners acclimated to cool fall run in 90-degree sun, no shade; expect slower than planned. Sudden 40-degree drop at sundown - be prepared.
- Equipment: water bottle or two; gaiters for sand/grit; crew brings pop-up tent and chairs.
- Crux: imperceptible descent to start/finish lulls runners into running too fast, then pay on the slog back up; terrain rarely perfectly flat. Entire course is runnable: pacing discipline a must.
- Mental: party atmosphere (music, beer trucks, disco balls) at start/finish and backside aid: do not sit down.
- Pro (Jamil Coury): after lap 1, at least a third of field runs sub-24-hour pace, unsustainable unless elite; force slow first couple of laps.
- Training: if cool autumns, train in heat of day (not mornings/evenings); get used to sand and gravel (dry riverbeds). Critical phase: EnduranceRun and SteadyStateRun intensity in September and October.
- Crew: easiest race to crew; bring food, fluids, comforts for entire race.
tags: type:methodology sport:running phase:build subtopic:javelina-jundred subtopic:pacing level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Javelina Jundred

### [TEU-C12-019] JFK 50: course, pacing, training and crew guidance

November, Boonsboro to Williamsport MD, oldest US ultra (1962), cap 1,000; ~10 miles on Appalachian Trail then C&O Canal Towpath (flat soft gravel); nearly all climbing in first five miles; fast course but western 100-mile champions may fail to crack top ten. Origin: JFK's 50-miles-in-under-20-hours challenge to Marines.
- Weather: highs mid-50s, start lows high 30s; can rain, sleet, snow; stock drop bags with dry clothes. Carry a space blanket.
- Crux: technical rocky trail early, conga line; if stuck in the pack save energy, settle in, pick spots to pass. Profile notes: first section technical, save legs; be patient in first few towpath miles.
- Mental: on the towpath many figure the hard part's over and go too fast; 35 miles remain; stick to the plan.
- Training: nearly completely runnable; train for trails and flats; versatility. Late-season race: start healthy. Critical phase July-September: TempoRuns and SteadyStateRuns.
- Crew: horseshoe route is easy, but parking battle at first aid stations; plan few meeting points plus drop bags. Pacers allowed, not encouraged.
tags: type:methodology sport:running phase:build subtopic:jfk-50 level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 JFK 50

### [TEU-C12-020] Lake Sonoma 50: course, pacing, training and crew guidance

April, Sonoma CA, out-and-back rolling course, 12 creek crossings, 86% relatively smooth non-technical singletrack, ~90% finishing rate, tune-up for Western States; blind lottery entry. Island View aid station supplied by boat. Pacers not allowed.
- Weather: lows mid-40s, highs mid-70s, much shade; if raining, likely hard all day and cool.
- Equipment: nothing special, but some aid stations nearly 7.5 miles apart: carry more water and food than you think. Remote-station water is for drinking, not dousing; cool off at creeks or lake.
- Crux: from mile 33 to finish feels long and lonely (5 to 7.5 miles between last three aid stations; sparse runners).
- Mental: relentless ups and downs ("death by a thousand cuts") blow quads; pacing, especially on short downhills. Pro (Dakota Jones): train to run uphill and down, not only speed-hike. If patient on front half, move well on second half (miles ~31.5-47).
- Training: foot turnover on short flats; mitigate downhill punishment; include ups, downs, flats in interval work for pace changes.
- Critical phase: as tune-up, some VO2 max work leading up; if goal/first ultra, SteadyStateRun work 4-8 weeks before.
- Crew: easy but Warm Springs Creek station needs long winding drives; self-contained with full tank of gas.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:lake-sonoma-50 subtopic:downhill-running level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Lake Sonoma 50

### [TEU-C12-021] Leadville Trail 100: climbs, altitude, pacing and crew guidance

Late August, Colorado Rockies; lowest point 9,200 ft; no qualifying requirement; 600-800 participants; out-and-back; Hope Pass 12,600 ft. Significant climbs/descents (same segments): Colorado Trail and Hagerman Pass Road 5.65 miles, 1,338 ft; Hope Pass 4.48 miles, 3,204 ft; Sheep's Gulch 2.4 miles, 2,303 ft; Twin Lakes to Colorado Trail 2.7 miles, 1,222 ft; Powerline 3.88 miles, 1,483 ft.
- Weather: mornings low 40s, afternoons mid-70s, late-afternoon thunderstorms (gear above timberline). Some years heavy rain, snow/sleet high; 10 p.m.-4 a.m. cold; fatigue + difficulty generating heat = hypothermia risk; miles 87-95 around Turquoise Lake worst: bring extra clothes.
- Equipment: pacers (join after mile 50) may mule gear: extra clothes, water, food, headlamp batteries, trekking poles. Indispensable: a pacer/mule.
- Crux: Powerline climb over Sugarloaf Pass (11,071 ft) and descent into May Queen (mile 86.5), steep, slow, technical, at night.
- Mental: crowds; groups of shared misery around miles 60-85 slow everyone to slowest pace, causing missed cutoffs: realize it and accelerate out immediately.
- Pro (Dylan Bowman): the Boulevard, 3-mile gradual uphill into town at mile 97, harder than expected; keep pushing until finish line on 6th Street is visible. Profile notes: be patient at Turquoise Lake (crowded), Sheep's Gulch and Hope Pass descents; miles 65-79 relatively runnable.
- Training: altitude exaggerates weaknesses in pacing and nutrition: practice and follow hydration/nutrition plan for weeks and months; slow and steady.
- Crew: tempers flare over parking; position near aid station doesn't matter.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:leadville-100 subtopic:altitude level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Leadville Trail 100

### [TEU-C12-022] Tor des Géants: logistics, sleep, gear and training guidance

221 miles, Aosta Valley Italy; 82,000 ft climbing and descending; 25 passes; Alta Via 1 and 2; start/finish Courmayeur. Lottery weights field proportionally by applicants' country (750-person field); no other prerequisites. Lottery in February: ~7 months to train.
- Weather changes by pass (hail/snow to 80s). Race issues zone forecasts for five regions (app or Life Bases).
- Aid: refugios approx. every 10K; Life Bases every 50K (warm meals, shower, medical, sleep, yellow duffel bag follows runner).
- Mandatory equipment: always carry cold/foul-weather gear, hat, gloves, two headlamps with backup batteries, phone, passport; other mandatory gear (e.g., crampons, knife, extra shoelace string) goes in the yellow bag returned before the start; race may require carrying any item from the list depending on conditions.
- Indispensable: poles (use uphill and downhill; backup pair in yellow bag; pack in cylindrical cardboard tube).
- Critical logistical challenge: sleep. Winners ~70+ hours (maybe trail naps with caffeine); others: aid stations limit sleep to two hours, strict; take full two hours whenever possible. See Chapter 16.
- Training: hike, hike; hardly any uphill running. February-April normal running with intervals; from May focus on low-intensity hiking.
- Crew: big time commitment (over a week); stay in Aosta rather than Courmayeur (saves ~1 hour driving per Life Base); beware speed traps and paid parking.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:tor-des-geants subtopic:sleep subtopic:hiking level:elite
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Tor des Géants

### [TEU-C12-023] UTMB / CCC / TDS: preparation, gear, logistics and crew guidance

Three races on UTMB weekend around Mont Blanc (France, Italy, Switzerland); trails similar so preparation is similar. Entry: elaborate qualifying-points system then lottery; rules change every few years; more value on longer races. Over 100,000 people and 10,000 runners in Chamonix.
- Weather notoriously moody, especially Italian side (sun to hail instantly).
- Equipment: mandatory gear checked at aid stations; don't skimp on protective jacket/pants; prepare for bad weather if any hint in forecast. Indispensable: pack - train with it at least three days per week; test how it loads.
- Pro (Stephanie Howe): arrive a week early (time zone); stay a mile or two outside Chamonix center, use public transport; resist exploring mountain; play to strengths (descending, flats, uphills); poles are a godsend but know how to use them; get required gear ahead and practice; patience - rarely won in first half.
- Logistics: evening start wastes energy; stay slightly outside the city; ignore friends training too hard in taper.
- Training: climbs steep, few switchbacks; look at grade and length of individual climbs, not just totals; in North America almost impossible to do too much climbing/descending; training camp in Rockies or Sierras in July or early August; train with poles.
- Critical phase: July and early August lots of low-intensity hiking on steep terrain with poles; if intensity was built earlier, last six weeks can be EnduranceRuns (and hiking) and RecoveryRuns.
- Crew: strict limits on when crew may enter aid stations; only one crew member per station; gear checked, so make sure runner leaves with mandatory equipment.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:utmb subtopic:poles subtopic:mandatory-gear level:advanced
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 UTMB/CCC/TDS

### [TEU-C12-024] Vermont 100: humidity, road shoes, training and crew guidance

July, loop in east-central Vermont on country roads, gravel farm roads, jeep tracks, short pavement; shares course with 100-mile horse race; also a 100K. Entry: 50-mile ultra under 12 hours or 100K under 14 hours, plus a volunteer day at an ultra of 50K or longer. Aid stations max 5 miles apart, average 3.5; bare necessities: hat and a water bottle or two. Elevation tops under 2,000 ft (altitude nonfactor).
- Weather: 80-85°F day, 55-65°F night; humidity impairs cooling; sweat-drenched gear and chafing.
- Indispensable: cushioned road shoes, not trail shoes (think 100-mile road race).
- Mental: saw-edge profile of short climbs and descents, no rhythm; easy footing makes many start too fast. Profile notes: take initial descents easy (~miles 25-35); alternate hiking and running later climbs (~55-65, ~90-95).
- Pro (Andy Jones-Wilkins): crux is Camp Ten Bear Loop miles 43-70 (27 miles, longer climbs, less tree cover, grittier roads); careless here risks a death slog in final 30 miles.
- Training: heat training vital, ideally humid; prepare quads for punchy downhills on hard-packed surface. Critical phase: constant intensity; EnduranceRuns and SteadyStateRuns in May-June, volume peaking early June.
- Crew: camp at start/finish night before; nearest town 35 miles away - bring everything. Follow race bible exactly (unmarked dirt roads, GPS unreliable, course dialed to 0.01 mile).
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:vermont-100 subtopic:humidity level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Vermont 100

### [TEU-C12-025] Wasatch Front 100: temperature swings, Brighton Lodge, training and crew guidance

Weekend after Labor Day, Salt Lake City to Soldier Hollow UT; altitudes 5,000-10,467 ft (Point Supreme). Entry: 8 hours supervised trail maintenance through a Forest Service office with verified report. Ends 5 p.m. Saturday. Significant climbs: Lamb's Canyon 4 mi, 2,035 ft; Millcreek to Red Lovers Ridge 6.5 mi, 2,000 ft; Catherine Pass 3.6 mi, 1,761 ft. Significant descents: Francis Peak jeep road 4 mi, 1,715 ft; Bald Mountain to Alexander Ridge 5.49 mi, 2,253 ft; Scott's Pass to Brighton Lodge 3.14 mi, 1,135 ft; Catherine Pass to Ant Knolls 1.9 mi, 1,423 ft; "the glide" and "the plunge" from mile 79, "Irv's Torture Chamber".
- Weather: mid-20s early morning to mid-80s afternoon (60-degree range); dry heat miles 20-40 ("HOT and exposed"); some years snow, others >100°F.
- Gear: ice sleeves and ice-filled bandanas by day; double gloves, knit hat, down jacket at night. Carry more water than you think, especially at the front. Indispensable: packable down jacket the size of a water bottle.
- Cruxes: Catherine Pass descent and Irv's Torture Chamber (late, steep technical descending shreds quads).
- Mental: leaving Brighton Lodge (mile 67.3) before climb to Point Supreme; drop bags are inside the warm A-frame; the room beyond is "the morgue" where racers who rest often never leave. Grab drop bag and go back outside, or have crew wait outside. Pro (Jason Koop): the climb out is not as hard as lore; get food/gear and get out.
- Training: can run ~70% of course; if heat is a weakness, train in hottest part of day and perhaps sauna. Altitude hits in last third, early morning hours. Critical phase: July and August big EnduranceRun miles, lots of climbing, SteadyStateRun intensity.
- Crew: pacers can join at mile 45; those starting at Brighton Lodge dress for hiking in subfreezing temperatures.
tags: type:methodology sport:running subtopic:wasatch-front-100 subtopic:heat subtopic:cold level:advanced
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Wasatch Front 100

### [TEU-C12-026] Western States 100: heat, descent, sauna acclimation and crew guidance

Last full weekend in June; Olympic Valley to Auburn CA; first official run 1977 (origin Tevis Cup horse race; Gordy Ainsleigh ran 1974, 23:42). Entry: lottery from those who completed a 100-mile or another qualifying ultra within a year (November to November); sub-100 qualifiers have cutoffs set by officials. Twenty-one aid stations. Pacers from Foresthill, mile 62.
- Weather: nights high 20s, days well into 90s, >100°F not uncommon; heat felt even at 6,000-8,000 ft. Fast runners meet the most exposed section (110+°F possible) between 2 p.m. and 5 p.m.; heat an issue across the pack.
- Equipment: bandanas, headbands, hat, ice sleeves. Indispensable: bandana soaked in ice water, filled with ice, around head or neck.
- Mental/crux: ~17-mile Cal Street descent from Foresthill (mile 62), gradual, drops >3,000 ft, deceptively easy at the hottest time; hold speed to keep legs for punchy climbs in last 20 miles. Profile notes: easy up the Escarpment (mile 2.5-3); take descent miles 31-46 easy; miles 72-78 runnable but easy; last section miles 82-99 can make a difference with good legs.
- Pro (Dylan Bowman): international field; don't react to others' moves.
- Training: do some training runs in the hottest part of day, experiment with fluids/nutrition; use a dry sauna in the last several days (the author insists on sauna acclimation for this race). A colleague's thermal imaging: cooler runners fared better than hotter ones. Critical phase: April and early May TempoRun work, so cardiovascular fitness is maximized before the sauna protocol starts.
- Crew: some stations need shuttles; much driving on narrow mountain roads.
tags: type:methodology sport:running phase:build subtopic:western-states-100 subtopic:heat-acclimation subtopic:sauna level:elite
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 Western States 100

## C13 Contraindications, Cautions & Safety Limits

### [TEU-C13-001] Loss of period / amenorrhea and RED-S warning; contraceptive masking

- WHAT: loss of period (amenorrhea = absence of menstrual cycle for ninety days or more; oligomenorrhea = cycles longer than thirty-five days). LIMIT: not normal even for endurance athletes; treat as a red flag ("canary in the coal mine"). WHO: female athletes, esp. ultra-endurance. WHY: signals low energy availability/RED-S. Reported in up to 70 percent of women in sports vs 2 to 5 percent of general population (Jacobsson 2014; Nazem 2012).
- Low energy availability and menstrual dysfunction can occur without restrictive or disordered eating; easy to fall behind on nutrition in ultra training.
- Contraceptive masking: oral contraceptives ("fake period") and hormonal IUDs (~20 percent lose bleeding) can hide amenorrhea; take into account if at risk for RED-S. Consult primary care provider or OB-GYN with doubts.
- Heat caution (menopause): see menopause entry.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:amenorrhea subtopic:red-s subtopic:female-athletes
evidence: study (Jacobsson 2014; Nazem 2012)
src: Ch 2 "RED-S", "Birth Control Considerations"

### [TEU-C13-002] Menopause and heat/fructose cautions

- Heat: hot flashes divert blood to skin; later sweat onset can raise heat storage early in runs; dulled thirst reduces hydration awareness. Action: longer warm-up before hard efforts, more attention to hydration (no numeric limit given).
- Added fructose: products with added fructose may cause GI distress mid-race for menopausal women because of declining fructose absorption; avoid, prefer glucose, dextrose, sucrose.
- Alcohol before bed: avoid (sleep disruption).
tags: type:caution level:masters condition:menopausia stage:perimenopause stage:postmenopause subtopic:heat subtopic:fructose
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Menopause"

### [TEU-C13-003] Caution: Fat-adapted diets for female endurance athletes

- WHAT: "Fat-adapted" (e.g., ketogenic, extreme low-carbohydrate high-fat) dietary strategies.
- LIMIT: Generally ineffective and not recommended for female athletes.
- WHO: Female endurance and ultra-endurance runners.
- WHY: Female athletes naturally possess higher gene expression for fatty acid metabolism (including the CD36 transport protein) and higher estrogen levels promoting lipid oxidation; thus, they are already highly efficient fat oxidizers compared to males. Adopting fat-adapted strategies provides negligible metabolic upside while carrying significant risks of negative health side effects and exacerbating low carbohydrate availability / RED-S risk.
- EXCEPTION: None noted in text.
tags: type:caution level:all sport:running condition:salud_hormonal focus:macronutrients subtopic:fat-adaptation subtopic:female-athletes
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 2 "Substrate Utilization"

### [TEU-C13-004] Cautions from Chapter 4

- Hydration/blood volume errors: WHAT: low blood volume/hydration mismanagement; LIMIT: avoid; WHO: ultrarunners; WHY: can lead to hospital or death; slow to correct.
- Gut: WHAT: large food portions during high blood-flow competition or jostling; advice: eat smaller portions, bigger ones on slow uphills, finish at least ten minutes before the summit; WHY: gut damage/distress.
- Blisters: WHAT: changing gait to run around a blister; avoid; WHY: injuries up the kinetic chain that cannot be treated in the race. Do not let calluses grow thick (blister under callus). Keep puncture small enough that the blister roof stays attached.
- Lubricants/powders/tape: may increase moisture and friction (Nacht 1981); use sparingly.
- Large broken blisters are more prone to infection.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:safety
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 4

### [TEU-C13-005] Avoid downhill intervals and downhill RunningIntervals

- Activity / Dose: Downhill intervals and high-intensity RunningIntervals (RI) performed on descents.
- Limit: Avoid completely. RunningIntervals must almost always be completed on flat ground or uphill terrain. Downhill intervals are avoided.
- Population: All ultrarunners / endurance athletes.
- Reason: At the same effort level, running downhill produces significantly larger ground reaction forces because running speed is higher. Trying to hit high cardiovascular intensity running downhill carries a high risk of musculoskeletal injury and excessive muscle damage. Additionally, it is difficult to reach the target cardiovascular intensity on descents.
- Exception: Lower-intensity quality sessions such as TempoRuns (TR) and SteadyStateRuns (SSR) may incorporate downhills later in the season during race-specific training phases to mimic course profiles.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all phase:build phase:peak subtopic:downhill-running subtopic:running-intervals subtopic:injury-prevention
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "The Four Disciplines of Ultrarunning", "How to Train for the Four Disciplines", "What's So Great About Uphill Intervals?"

### [TEU-C13-006] Altitude health risks and heat-acclimation safety

- Altitude illness: traveling to altitude above 8,202 ft (2,500 m) carries real health risks of acute high-altitude illness. This includes acute mountain sickness (AMS, which usually resolves without treatment) and potentially fatal conditions: high-altitude pulmonary edema (HAPE, described by author as "swelling of the airway") and high-altitude cerebral edema (HACE, swelling of the brain). Consult a primary care provider before traveling to altitude.
- Pre-travel training caution: avoid high-intensity training in the days immediately prior to traveling to altitude; high-intensity training elevates cortisol levels, which blunts altitude adaptation.
- Acute arrival intensity cap: athletic programs recommend keeping training intensity low (below 70 percent of VO2 max) during the first 24 to 72 hours after arriving at altitude to avoid increased fatigue or maladaptation while heart rate is elevated and stroke volume is depressed.
- Iron toxicity: obtain an iron panel (measuring serum ferritin) through a primary care provider before taking iron supplements; excessive iron intake can be toxic.
- Heat acclimation risks: inducing hyperthermia carries inherent risks. Some active heating methods are time-consuming, uncomfortable, and potentially dangerous. Athletes must monitor core body temperature and hydration status throughout any protocol (e.g., via pre- and post-sauna weighing to quantify sweat loss, or using urinalysis test strips to verify adequate rehydration). Consult a primary care provider with concerns.
- Sauna and tapering contraindication: sauna sessions add systemic stress, which is contraindicated during a standard taper. When utilizing a single-bout heat protocol in the final days leading into an event (Protocol #1), athletes must taper training workload more than normal.
- Race-day heat stroke: heat stroke is a medical emergency. Acclimated athletes must still use active race-day cooling strategies (ice bandanas, arm sleeves, ice on torso, dousing water, cool drinks) and reduce pace or stop if necessary.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:altitude-illness subtopic:hyperthermia subtopic:iron sport:running phase:race
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 7 "Acclimation Protocol for Altitude"; "Is an Altitude Intervention a Good Idea?"; "Acclimation Methods"; "Priority of Methods"; "Protocol #1"; "Race-Day Considerations"

### [TEU-C13-007] Long-haul travel: DVT/VTE risk and Virchow's triad

- WHAT: prolonged sitting (especially more than four hours), dehydration from dry cabin air (1 percent humidity), and hard or long workouts or races can increase risk of deep vein thrombosis (DVT, usually a lower-leg clot) and venous thromboembolism (VTE, a clot traveling to the lungs as pulmonary embolism).
- WHO: highest risk with underlying health problems. Risk rises somewhat for everyone, but for healthy people and athletes it is extremely low (Bartholomew 2011). Otherwise healthy endurance athletes can be at increased risk after hard or long efforts.
- WHY (Virchow's triad, Kushner 2020): venous stasis (sitting still), damage to veins (microtrauma in training and racing), and hypercoagulability (disrupted hemostatic balance between coagulation and fibrinolysis). Markers rise during and after exercise (hemostatic activation; Kupchak 2016; Zaleski 2015) and normally return to baseline. Risk rises when long sitting and dehydration plus damaged veins or clotting predisposition tip the balance.
- ACTIONS: get up and walk the cabin at regular intervals; stay hydrated; compression socks are recommended for people at higher risk due to health issues. For others they might help.
- Evidence on compression socks: little direct research on wearing them on planes after a race. Mixed findings; worn during a race or hard workout they may reduce perceived DOMS and attenuate hemostatic activation in the hours after long, hard runs (Zaleski 2016). Research consistently agrees they are safe before, during and after hard exercise.
tags: type:caution type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:travel subtopic:vte subtopic:compression-socks sport:running
evidence: review (Bartholomew 2011; Kushner 2020; Kupchak 2016; Zaleski 2015, 2016)
src: Ch 8 sidebar "The Long-Haul Flight Home" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C13-008] NSAIDs: danger for endurance athletes
