- WHAT: nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, including naproxen (Aleve), ibuprofen (Motrin/Advil), acetylsalicylic acid (Aspirin), celecoxib (Celebrex) and others.
- LIMIT: the author does not recommend them in endurance and ultra-endurance sports. "The use of NSAIDs in endurance and ultra-endurance sports is dangerous. The research is unequivocal." Even the recommended bottle dosage, compounded by the stress of race or training, is risky.
- WHO: endurance and ultra athletes; risk is greater in hot environments.
- WHY (research summary):
  - They increase risk of hyponatremia (low blood sodium) and acute kidney injury, which can cause serious, even fatal, complications.
  - They are prevalent in triathlon, cycling, running and ultrarunning, and athletes generally do not know the risks.
  - They have not been shown to improve performance, and there is very little evidence they improve injury outcomes.
  - They can perpetuate existing musculoskeletal injuries by masking pain and by affecting the biochemistry of healing.
- EXCEPTION: outside sport, following the label dose or doctor's instructions, NSAIDs can relieve occasional pain, inflammation or fever with low to moderate risks of stomach bleeding or kidney injury.
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:nsaids subtopic:hyponatremia subtopic:kidney-injury sport:running
evidence: author-experience (cites research body; no specific reference)
src: Ch 8 "NSAID Medications" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C13-009] Analgesics: acetaminophen and opioids

- Pain is part of sport. Temporary soreness is normal, and accurate pain assessment is essential to judge how well or quickly you are recovering. Masking pain with strong analgesics can lead to accumulating inappropriate training stress or worsening a minor injury into a major one. The author does not reject all pain relievers.
- **Acetaminophen:** an over-the-counter pain and fever reducer that is neither NSAID nor opioid, and not anti-inflammatory. Occasional use at recommended doses or per doctor carries few risks.
- **Opioids** (morphine, fentanyl, tramadol, oxycodone): powerful analgesics important in acute and chronic severe pain, but with significant risk of addiction and fatal overdose (CDC: in 2018 opioids contributed to the deaths of more than 46,000 Americans).
- Athletes have been suspected of using opioids, particularly tramadol, to mask competition pain. As of 2020 tramadol was not on the WADA Prohibited List but was in its monitoring program, and individual sports, including cycling, banned it in competition.
- RULE: if pain is so intense it can only be managed with opioids, the athlete is not healthy enough to continue training.
- EXCEPTION: opioid use under a doctor's supervision to participate in physical therapy or post-injury rehabilitation.
tags: type:caution type:decision-rule level:all subtopic:analgesics subtopic:opioids subtopic:acetaminophen
evidence: guideline (CDC 2018 mortality figure); author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Analgesics" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C13-010] Weighted pack cautions

- WHAT: pack/vest load in training. LIMIT: <10% body weight; beginners ~5%; avoid >20% (>30 lb at 150 lb). WHO: runners using weighted packs for hiking. WHY: altered biomechanics (forward lean, slowed cadence), injury. 
- WHAT: running downhill with a pack heavier than race pack. LIMIT: be cautious. WHY: downhill is more stressful musculoskeletally; weight adds. EXCEPTION: dump extra water before descents.
- WHAT: RunningIntervals downhill. LIMIT: never. WHY: cannot reach >90% VO2max.
- WHAT: sleds, tire dragging. LIMIT: skip. WHY: biomechanics unlike ultrarunning.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:injury-risk
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9 weight vest and RunningIntervals sections

### [TEU-C13-011] Training and lifestyle contraindications, limits, and cautions from Ch 10

- Sleep Deprivation Training:
  - WHAT: Training overnight, running after restricted sleep, or running after 24 hours awake to condition the body to sleep deprivation.
  - LIMIT: Avoid completely for physiological conditioning. Limit to at most 1–2 overnight runs strictly to test lighting/gear and gain psychological familiarization with prolonged headlamp locomotion.
  - WHO: Ultrarunners (including those preparing for 200-mile events).
  - WHY: No scientific evidence demonstrates physiological adaptation to sleep loss. Sleep-deprived workout quality drops by 2% to 10% (Oliver et al. 2009; Roberts et al. 2019; Skein et al. 2011); training before and after must be disrupted to accommodate recovery; athlete vulnerability to injury and illness increases.
  - EXCEPTION: 1–2 overnight gear/lighting familiarization sessions.

- Restrictive or Food-Elimination Diets:
  - WHAT: Diets that eliminate or demonize particular food groups or macronutrients (e.g., severe carbohydrate restriction).
  - LIMIT: Avoid completely.
  - WHO: Endurance and ultra-endurance runners.
  - WHY: Problematic at best and harmful at worst. Total energy availability is the primary prerequisite; carbohydrates are critical for sustaining high-volume and high-intensity training.

- Inappropriate Taper Workouts (Taper Tantrums):
  - WHAT: High-intensity, non-specific workouts during the race taper (e.g., doing track interval sessions in Chamonix days before UTMB).
  - LIMIT: Strictly avoid non-specific high-intensity work in the final 2 weeks before a major race.
  - WHO: All ultramarathon runners during the taper phase.
  - WHY: Track work is the least event-specific activity possible before a mountain ultra; driven by pre-race anxiety, it risks acute fatigue, injury, and compromising months of accumulated training adaptation.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:sleep-deprivation subtopic:dietary-restrictions subtopic:taper-errors
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Sleep Deprivation Training for 200s?", "Adequate and Periodized Nutrition", "Taper"

### [TEU-C13-012] Restriction: No body-composition changes during hardest training phase

- What: Body composition alteration / deliberate weight loss / caloric deficit.
- Limit: Avoid completely during the athlete's hardest training phase.
- Who: Ultrarunners in peak training phases (90% of the time the TempoRun / lactate threshold phase).
- Why: The balance of volume and intensity in this phase grinds the athlete down more than high-intensity/low-volume or low-intensity/high-volume blocks. All available spare energy and physiological resources are required for hard training and purposeful recovery; layering caloric restriction or weight loss creates excessive stress and elevates maladaptation and injury risk.
- Exception: Schedule body composition changes during other, less demanding periods in the season.
tags: type:caution level:all phase:build subtopic:body-composition subtopic:energy-availability sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 12 "Concepts for the Short-Range Plan"

### [TEU-C13-013] Cautions: fueling and hydration (Ch 13)

- Replacing 100% of sweat loss in an ultra: avoid; can lead to hyponatremia. WHO: ultrarunners. Replace ~95-98%.
- "Consume as much fluid as tolerable": outdated; likely contributed to hyponatremia cases in ultrarunning.
- Salt tablets: avoid; creates an "extremely hypotonic environment in your stomach (too much sodium, not enough fluid)" [Likely typo in source — describes hypertonicity; recorded as printed] and encourages overdrinking.
- Fluids with high carbohydrate: ≥35 g per 20 oz bottle times two bottles/hour reaches 70 g/hr; more carbohydrate beyond the 60-90 g/hr range can cause gastric distress, leading to delayed drinking and dehydration.
- Fat supplements (e.g., MCT oil): limit to ≤30 g (270 cal); more causes GI distress for most.
- Hyperhydration/hyponatremia can also produce clear urine; clear urine is not automatically ideal hydration.
- Dehydration hastens hypothermia; severe dehydration can damage kidneys and alter blood pH; low plasma volume can progress to heatstroke.
- Fluid loss rate during exercise: ranges widely from 500 ml/hour to >2 L/hour.
- Dehydration >2% weight loss in training: no benefit.
- Do not run 57 km without fuel (author: "Hell no. Please don't. That's stupid").
- Pre-workout: avoid high fat/protein/fiber meals right before hard workouts (slows digestion).
- Do not use evening guzzling to fix dehydration; do not guzzle full hourly fluid at once.
- Weighing at aid stations during races is unreliable (scale on dirt, muddy clothes error >2%); thirst perception is unreliable in desert conditions (dust, sand, hot dry air).
- Extremely low-carbohydrate strategies limit pace (fat processing needs ~8% more oxygen).
- Costa et al.: in races >8 h avoid excess protein, fat, fiber, FODMAP-heavy foods.
tags: type:caution level:all focus:hydration focus:gut-health subtopic:hyponatremia subtopic:dehydration
src: Ch 13 multiple sections
evidence: author-experience

### [TEU-C13-014] Avoid these things with fat-optimization strategies

If undertaking fat optimization, avoid at all costs:
1. Low energy availability — too few calories regardless of macronutrient composition; the goal is to manipulate one macronutrient's availability, not total calories. WHY: a host of problems (RED-S; reduced strength, performance, training response, increased injury risk).
2. Hard workouts in a fasted state or while restricting carbohydrate — "a terrible idea"; sets up underperformance and overtraining at once.
3. Hard workouts at the end of long runs (as "training on tired legs" or fat adaptation) — "Just don't do it"; large stress, little benefit.
4. Long runs without fuel (e.g., longest long run, or 50–100 miles without fuel) — no superior adaptation vs. reasonable strategies, little upside, possible maladaptation; "no awards for the lowest number of calories consumed in a race."
Also: LCHF/keto raises bone-stress injury risk (CTX up, P1NP down after 3.5 weeks; Heikura 2020); risk of low energy availability. Safe implementation of dietary fat-adaptation needs a registered dietitian. Fasted training sessions limited to under two hours. Exception: none given. Recommended: avoid extreme strategies such as restricting carbohydrate on hard workouts and longer runs.
tags: type:caution level:all sport:running focus:energy-balance subtopic:fasted-training subtopic:red-s subtopic:bone-stress condition:general
evidence: author-experience; Heikura 2020
src: Ch 14 "Avoid These Things" | no page markers

### [TEU-C13-015] Caution: Reckless or excessive dissociation on technical terrain

- WHAT: Reckless or excessive dissociative focus strategies (e.g., counting, meal planning, zoning out).
- LIMIT: Avoid during technical terrain, rocky singletrack, fast descents, or any situation requiring obstacle avoidance and precise foot placement.
- WHO: Ultrarunners in training and competition.
- WHY: Reckless dissociation causes the athlete to miss critical environmental and perceptual cues, leading to acute musculoskeletal injury and trauma (e.g., tripping over rocks, spraining ankles, falling hard, or striking tree branches).
- EXCEPTION: Deploy intentionally and selectively during non-technical terrain, benign flat sections, or when physical sensations (such as severe foot pain in events like Badwater 135 or Western States 100) must be psychologically down-regulated.
tags: type:caution sport:running level:all subtopic:dissociation subtopic:safety
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 15 "Strategies to Enhance Dissociative Focus"

### [TEU-C13-016] NSAIDs: never in the race-day plan

WHAT: NSAIDs (aspirin, ibuprofen, naproxen). LIMIT: do not include in race-day plan, "Ever." WHO: ultrarunners during races. WHY: increase risk of hyponatremia (Page et al. 2007; Wharam et al. 2006) and greatly increase stress on the renal system, risking renal injury and rhabdomyolysis (damaged muscle tissue emptying into the bloodstream); risk of hospitalization with renal failure. EXCEPTION: none stated.
Related cautions: untested supplements/ergogenics should not be used on race day (unregulated, may contain banned substances); high caffeine (author notes 2,400 mg over 20 miles would be extreme; stay at or below 50 mg/hr for <6h races, up to 100 mg/hr for 3-4 hours overnight).
tags: type:caution level:all sport:running focus:supplements subtopic:nsaids subtopic:hyponatremia subtopic:rhabdomyolysis
src: Ch 16 "NSAIDs" | no page markers
evidence: study (Page et al. 2007; Wharam et al. 2006)

### [TEU-C13-017] Environmental cautions and safety hazards across major ultramarathons

Environmental hazards and safety protocols detailed across the major ultramarathons:
- Lightning hazard (Hardrock 100): Exposed high-alpine ridgelines present severe lightning risk during late-afternoon monsoon thunderstorms. Action: If lightning is seen or heard nearby, descend to lower elevation as quickly and safely as possible.
- Severe hypothermia in late-race fatigue (Leadville Trail 100): Extreme cold between 10 p.m. and 4 a.m. combined with late-race fatigue and inability of the body to generate metabolic heat creates acute hypothermia risk, particularly on miles 87 to 95 around Turquoise Lake. Requirement: Pack and carry extra warm clothing specifically for this section; pacers should carry extra layers.
- Subfreezing night exposure and pacing mismatch (Wasatch Front 100, Hardrock 100): Night temperatures drop into the mid-20s. Pacers joining at Brighton Lodge or in high-alpine sections must dress for subfreezing hiking rather than running, because technical trails and runner fatigue prevent fast movement necessary to generate body heat. Equipment: Double gloves, knit hat, and packable down jacket.
- Extreme heat, surface burns, and dehydration (Badwater 135): Daytime temperatures exceeding 110°F–130°F. Asphalt reaches temperatures capable of melting shoe soles and blistering feet through shoes; runners must run on the white shoulder stripe. 25–30 mph searing headwinds desiccate airways (apply internal nasal saline and throat lozenges). Thermoregulatory breakdown occurs from cumulative exposure duration rather than absolute heat alone.
- Crew health emergency risk (Badwater 135): More crew members than runners require medical treatment for heat- and exhaustion-related emergencies. Crews must enforce strict hydration, cooling, and rest shifts among a minimum of two crew members.
- Extreme diurnal temperature swings (Wasatch Front 100, Javelina Jundred): 40- to 60-degree drops between afternoon sun and post-sunset/night necessitate immediate transition to cold-weather clothing; northern runners arriving unacclimated to desert heat face elevated dehydration and pace collapse.
- Sudden alpine weather transitions (UTMB / CCC / TDS, Hardrock 100): Sunny conditions can transform into violent rain, sleet, hail, or snow within minutes. Skimping on mandatory waterproof/protective gear ("weight weenie" approach) is strictly cautioned against; carry full foul-weather gear and an emergency space blanket at all times.
tags: type:caution sport:running subtopic:environmental-safety subtopic:hypothermia subtopic:heat-illness level:all
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 18 (Badwater 135, Hardrock 100, Leadville Trail 100, Wasatch Front 100, UTMB, Javelina Jundred)

## C14 Formulas & Calculations

### [TEU-C14-001] Velocity equation for endurance running (F x VO2max / Cr)

Velocity = F × VO2max / Cr
- VO2max = size of the aerobic engine.
- F = fraction of VO2max that is utilized.
- Cr = cost of running (oxygen cost to run a given distance).
Use: predicts and explains performance from 5K to marathon (Di Prampero 1992). A bigger engine at equal economy is faster; reducing Cr makes you faster still, especially once the aerobic engine is maximized. Improve VO2max and F, and reduce Cr, to run faster.
Limit: beyond the marathon distance the validity of the equation breaks down; ultramarathon performance cannot be determined solely by oxygen consumption and cost of running. No worked example given.
tags: type:formula sport:running system:aerobic metric:pace level:all subtopic:velocity-equation
evidence: author-experience (cites Di Prampero 1992)
src: Ch 4 "Failure Points and How to Fix Them" | intro

### [TEU-C14-002] Average elevation change per mile (course matching)

Formula: average elevation change per mile = total elevation change in feet / total miles. Total elevation change is gain plus loss.
Worked example, Western States 100: gain 18,090 ft and loss 22,970 ft over 100.2 miles. (18,090 + 22,970)/100.2 = 410 feet of elevation change/mile.
Notes: this is a course-wide average that includes the steepest climbs, flats and varying descents.
tags: type:formula sport:running level:all subtopic:elevation-per-mile
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 5 "How to Train for the Four Disciplines"

### [TEU-C14-003] Trekking pole length sizing calculation

Formula:
$$\text{Pole Length (cm)} = \text{Athlete Height (cm)} \times 0.68$$

- Variables:
  - $\text{Pole Length (cm)}$: Recommended total length of the trekking pole.
  - $\text{Athlete Height (cm)}$: Standing height of the runner in centimeters.
- Alternative physical check: Elbow bent at a 90-degree or slightly greater angle when elbows are at the runner's sides and poles touch the ground.
tags: type:formula sport:running level:all subtopic:trekking-poles subtopic:equipment
evidence: study (Hansen and Smith 2009; Pellegrini et al. 2015 [Likely unit/typo error in source — recorded as printed: Pellegrini et al. 2105])
src: Ch 5 "Should I Use Poles?"

### [TEU-C14-004] Formulas and quantitative calculations in Chapter 6

- Caloric expenditure in level running:
  `Total calories (kcal) = 1 kcal × body weight (kg) × distance (km)`
  (Caloric expenditure on flat ground is a function of weight and distance, not speed).
- Mechanical work:
  `Work = Force × Distance`
- Mechanical power / Work rate:
  `Power = Force × Velocity = Work / Time`
- Training Stress Balance (TSB):
  `TSB = CTL - ATL`
  Where `CTL` is 42-day rolling weighted average TSS and `ATL` is 7-day rolling weighted average TSS.
- Respiratory Exchange Ratio (RER):
  `RER = VCO2 expired / VO2 inspired`
- Perceived exertion correlates (author's rules of thumb):
  - Borg Scale (6–20): `Heart Rate (bpm) ≈ Borg RPE × 10`
  - Field Scale (1–10): `% VO2 max ≈ RPE (1–10) × 10`
tags: type:formula sport:running level:all subtopic:calculations metric:load,pace,hr,rpe
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 6 "Comparing Workload and Work Rate", "RPE and Breathing Rate", "Aggregating Total Training Stress", "Why Ultrarunners Should Embrace Perceived Exertion"

### [TEU-C14-005] Cardiac Output Relationship at Altitude

Formula:
$$\text{Cardiac Output } (Q) = \text{HR} \times \text{Stroke Volume}$$

Variables:
- $Q$: Cardiac output (total blood volume pumped by the heart per minute)
- $\text{HR}$: Heart rate (beats per minute)
- $\text{Stroke Volume}$: Amount of blood pumped out of the heart per beat

Application / Physiological Context:
Upon acute ascent to altitude, plasma volume contracts by 10% to 25% (hemoconcentration), reducing venous return and stroke volume. To maintain stable cardiac output ($Q$), resting and submaximal heart rate must increase proportionally. HR normalizes at rest and at lower aerobic intensities within 24 to 72 hours as acclimatization proceeds.
tags: type:formula level:all subtopic:cardiac-output subtopic:altitude metric:hr sport:running
evidence: research (Sinex and Chapman 2015; Beidleman et al. 2017)
src: Ch 7 "Physiology at a Glance" (altitude)

### [TEU-C14-006] Energy cost reduction and caloric savings from weight loss

- **Relationship:** On flat ground, the energy expenditure of running is independent of speed (overall caloric expenditure per kilometer or mile stays relatively constant). Reducing body weight reduces the energy cost of running proportionally:
  $$\text{Energy Cost Reduction (\%)} = 1\% \times \text{Body Weight Reduction (\%)}$$
  (A 1 percent reduction in body weight yields a 1 percent reduction in the energy cost of running).
- **Worked example:** Over a 160K (100 miles) ultramarathon, a 5 kg weight loss translates to an approximately 800-calorie savings.
- **Biomechanical & physiological effects:** Lowers vertical ground reaction forces (normally 2.5–3× body weight) and raises relative $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ while absolute $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ stays constant.
- **Caveat:** The author cautions that actively trying to lose weight often impairs ultrarunners because losing muscle mass impairs the ability to absorb running-induced muscle damage and metabolic stress.
tags: type:formula level:all subtopic:energy-cost subtopic:weight-loss sport:running metric:volume
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 sidebar "The Body-Weight Conundrum" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C14-007] Sleep efficiency calculation and immune threshold

$$\text{Sleep Efficiency (\%)} = \left(\frac{\text{Total Sleep Time}}{\text{Total Time in Bed}}\right) \times 100$$
- **Clinical threshold:** Participants with less than 92% sleep efficiency were 5.50 times more likely to develop a cold than those with 98% or greater sleep efficiency (Cohen 2009).
tags: type:formula level:all subtopic:sleep-efficiency subtopic:immune-function metric:volume
evidence: clinical-trial (Cohen 2009)
src: Ch 8 "Steps for Better Sleep Habits" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C14-008] Running Velocity and Endurance Performance Equation

Formula:
$$\text{Velocity} = \frac{F \times \text{VO}_2\text{max}}{\text{Cr}}$$

Variables:
- $\text{Velocity}$: Running speed / pace achieved.
- $F$: Fraction of $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ that can be sustained for the duration of the event (sustainable aerobic intensity / lactate threshold percentage).
- $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$: Maximum aerobic capacity (maximal rate of oxygen consumption).
- $\text{Cr}$: Cost of running (energy cost of running / running economy).

Application & Context:
- Running performance is determined by the interaction of how large the aerobic engine is ($\text{VO}_2\text{max}$), the fraction of that engine that can be sustained over time ($F$), and movement efficiency ($\text{Cr}$).
- Elite marathoners can sustain $F = 80\%$ to $90\%$ of their $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ for up to 2 hours.
- In many ultrarunners entering coaching, sustainable intensity (lactate threshold) is compressed against maximum intensity ($\text{VO}_2\text{max}$), with as little as a 3% gap (only seconds per mile difference between a pace held for hours vs. a pace held for minutes). Because sustainable pace cannot exceed maximal pace, further gains cannot be achieved by working threshold alone; the runner must first raise $\text{VO}_2\text{max}$ to create headroom before developing lower sustainable intensities.
tags: type:formula sport:running system:vo2max system:threshold metric:pace level:all subtopic:performance-equation subtopic:running-economy
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10 "Principle 2"

### [TEU-C14-009] Chapter 13 calculations

- Weight in kg = weight in lb / 2.2.
- Hourly sweat rate (oz) = (pre-run nude weight − post-run nude weight) in lb × 16, after a 1-hour EnduranceRun; race fluid replacement = ~95-98% of it.
- Post-exercise fluid = 1.5 × weight lost (e.g., 2 lb = 32 oz lost → 48 oz) with ~500 mg Na/L, within 2 hours.
- Pre-exercise carbohydrate = g/kg × body weight (Table 13.2).
- Carbohydrate calories per hour in an ultra ≈ 50% of total expenditure at ~65% VO2max (700 cal/hr → 350 cal/hr). Time to depletion = glycogen calories / carbohydrate calories per hour (2,000 / 350 ≈ 5.7 h).
- Sodium to replace: 50-80% of sweat sodium loss; intake target 600-800 mg/L.
tags: type:formula level:all focus:hydration focus:energy-balance metric:g-per-kg subtopic:sweat-rate
src: Ch 13 various
evidence: author-experience

## C15 Evidence & Key References

### [TEU-C15-001] Updated scientific understanding cited in the 2nd edition (Ch 1)

Concepts the author says are better understood since the first edition:
- Neuromuscular fatigue likely plays an important role in ultramarathon performance.
- Low-carbohydrate diets impact running economy.
- Hyponatremia (low blood sodium) is more often caused by overdrinking fluids that do not contain sodium than by not consuming enough sodium.
Research volume: 6 papers on ultrarunning in 2004, now more than 100 per year.

- Dong 2016: HRV for monitoring training load/fatigue/adaptation; ECG R-R interval figure adapted from it.
- Borg research: RPE 6-20 number × 10 correlates with HR.

### [TEU-C15-003] Ch 10 references relied on

- García-Pallarés 2010; Mølmen et al. 2019: block periodization slightly favored in cycling, kayaking, cross-country skiing.
- Goutianos 2016; Issurin 2018: biological basis for block training.
- Seiler 2006: time course of VO2 max, lactate threshold, efficiency (Fig 10.4).
- Seiler and Sjursen 2004: hierarchy of endurance training priorities (Fig 10.5).
- Maslow 1943: hierarchy of needs (inspiration for the pyramids).

### [TEU-C15-004] Scientific evidence and key references for strength training (Chapter 11)

- **Balsalobre-Fernández et al. 2016**: Demonstrates that concurrent strength and endurance training improves running economy (by 2 to 4 percent), increases VO2 max, improves real-world performance, and reduces injury rates.
- **Lauersen 2018**: Meta-analysis indicating that athletes who incorporate strength training experience a ~66 percent reduction in injury risk.
- **Ristolainen et al. 2014**: Demonstrates that programmed rest days lower injury risk in endurance athletes.
- **O’Donnell 2018**: Demonstrates that sleeping eight or more hours a night significantly reduces injury risk.
- **Blagrow, Howatson, and Hays 2017**: Long-term studies (24 to 40 weeks) demonstrating concurrent strength and endurance training produces no muscular growth (hypertrophy) despite measurable strength improvements, mediated biochemically by AMPK blunting mTOR activation.
tags: type:evidence sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-training subtopic:running-economy subtopic:injury-prevention subtopic:concurrent-training
src: Ch 11 | "Strength Training as an Ergogenic Aid", "Strength Training and Injury Reduction", "But Won't I Gain Weight That Will Slow Me Down?"

### [TEU-C15-005] Evidence and key references: Chapter 13

- Maunder et al. 2018: Fat oxidation begins to decline at upper EnduranceRun / lower TempoRun intensities (60-70% VO2 max), depending on trainability.
- Gleeson 1998: Human body operates within 97°F to 104°F; 97°F to 100°F is optimal for performance.
- Cheuvront and Sawka 2005: WUT (Weight, Urine, Thirst) diagram for daily hydration monitoring.
- Kenefick et al. 2012: Dehydration of ~2% body weight associated with sensation of thirst.
- Costa et al. 2017; Cox et al. 2010; Urdampilleta et al. 2020: Gut training enabling exogenous carbohydrate intake rates >90 g/hr.
- Knechtle et al. 2011: Equivocal findings on protein ingestion during ultras to mitigate muscle damage.
- Baker 2017: Sweat sodium concentration distribution in athletes (peaks ~700-900 mg Na/L).
- Costa et al. 2019: Systematic review / consensus recommendations for ultrarunning nutrition (Table 13.1).
- Tiller et al. 2019: International Society of Sports Nutrition (ISSN) position stand on ultramarathon nutrition (Table 13.1).
- American College of Sports Medicine (ACSM) 2016: Position statement on nutrition and athletic performance (Table 13.1).

### [TEU-C15-007] Reference list: heat, altitude and environmental adaptation

Studies the author cites on environmental adaptation (the reference list gives citations only, not the claims they support).
- Heat acclimation: Daanen, Racinais, Périard 2017 (heat acclimation decay and re-induction, systematic review and meta-analysis, Sports Medicine); Périard, Racinais, Sawka 2015 (adaptations and mechanisms of heat acclimation); Minett et al. 2016 (Cochrane review, heat acclimation for protection from exertional heat stress); Rivas et al. 2017 (metabolic heat production as mediator of heat acclimation); Sawka et al. 2011 (Comprehensive Physiology, heat stress); Sawka et al. 1993 (exercise in the heat); Casa 1999 (thermal physiology and dehydration); Gleeson 1998 (temperature regulation during exercise); Allan and Macmillan 1963 (heat effects on unacclimatized paratroops).

- Altitude: Beidleman et al. 2017 (normobaric hypoxia for sustaining altitude acclimatization); Carr et al. 2015 (9 h per night at 3000 m normobaric hypoxia); Chapman, Stickford, Levine 2010 (altitude training for winter sport athletes); Chawla and Saxena 2014 (physiology of high-altitude acclimatization); Constantini, Wilhite, Chapman 2017 (clinician guide to altitude training); Garvican-Lewis et al. 2015 (altitude exposure at 1800 m increases haemoglobin mass); Garvican-Lewis 2017 (Altitude: Friend or Foe?); Sinex and Chapman 2015 (hypoxic training methods); Goldfarb-Rumyantzev and Alper 2013 (kidney at high altitude); Anderson 2017 (acclimatization to altitude).

## C16 Notable Statements

### [TEU-C16-001] Notable Statements — Chapter 4

- "Ultrarunning is not a marathon. It’s not even a long marathon. It’s a different sport altogether, and the variables associated with it mean that performance can no longer be determined solely by oxygen consumption and the cost of running." (Ch 4, intro)
- "In my experience, most ultramarathon athletes, even the elites, find success through a lack of failure on race day. They achieve their goals, win races, and get those coveted belt buckles not because they ran one section very well but because they prevented the negative." (Ch 4, "Limiting Factors")
- "All reasonably healthy individuals can locomote at the necessary speed to beat the cutoffs for any ultramarathon. I say this not as an opinion but as a biomechanical fact." (Ch 4, "Limiting Factors")
- "The number one recommendation when you have a queasy stomach is to 'slow down and cool off.'" (Ch 4, "Your stomach and intestines are made of muscle, too!")
- "Any ultramarathon limiting factor can be trained! That’s what this book is for." (Ch 4, "Key Points on Limiting Factors")
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:quotes
src: Ch 4

### [TEU-C16-002] Notable statement on NSAID use in ultra-endurance sport

"I am not going to mince words here. The use of NSAIDs in endurance and ultra-endurance sports is dangerous. The research is unequivocal. Even seemingly benign use (i.e., the recommended dosage on the bottle), when compounded by the stress of the race or training, can exacerbate existing musculoskeletal injuries. Of greater consequence though: NSAID use increases the risk of hyponatremia and acute kidney injury, particularly in hot environments. Both can cause serious, if not fatal, medical complications."
tags: type:caution level:all subtopic:nsaids sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Nonsteroidal Anti-Inflammatory (NSAID) Medications" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C16-003] Notable statement on recovery activity vs rest

"More activities aren’t necessarily better for recovery than simply doing less between training sessions. Don’t mistake being busy with being effective."
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:recovery-philosophy sport:running
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 8 "Key Points for Recovery" | p. n/a

### [TEU-C16-004] Notable Statements: Key Workouts

- "Interval training is effective for improving performance because it enables you to accumulate time at specific intensities, and it is time at intensity that is the most critical aspect to achieving positive adaptations."
- "If there were a workout ultrarunners are allergic to, it would be RunningIntervals. The lung-searing intensity these intervals induce is seemingly incompatible with the long, slow nature of an ultramarathon."
- "Do the work periods hard and the easy periods easy... When the intensity of recovery periods is too high, it results in lower intensities for your work intervals, which reduces the effectiveness of the whole workout. Easier rest yields stronger intervals."
- "The relationship between the training required for a marathon and the training required for an ultra is not linear. A 50-miler does not require twice the amount of training as a marathon, nor does a 100-miler take twice as much training as a 50-miler."
- "After all, if you ain't got much, you better make the most of what you do have."
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:quotes
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 9

### [TEU-C16-005] Notable statements Ch 10

- "You always have far more options for how to improve than you have practical ones."
- "Being the least important should not be confused with being unimportant!"
- "Your best day doesn't happen because you have the highest VO2 max or because you have optimized fat oxidation; it happens because you have prepared yourself for whatever a race can throw at you, and you have prepared more for the things that matter more."
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:quotes
evidence: author-experience
src: Ch 10

### [TEU-C16-006] Notable statements on strength and cross-training for ultrarunners

- "Running is what will make you better at running."
- "If you only have time to run, you should be spending that time running, not cross-training."
- "Remember that strength training should complement your run training, not detract from it."
- "Even if you are crunched for time, you are better off redirecting your efforts into a few heavy squats as opposed to several dozen body-weight squats, lunges, and matrixes."
- "For younger athletes, strength training might take a back seat for much of the year, but as we age we are all encouraged to shift some priority away from running time and toward moving heavy objects a few times a week."
tags: type:philosophy sport:running level:all subtopic:strength-training subtopic:cross-training
src: Ch 11 | "Prescription for Strength Training", "What about leg circuits and body-weight exercises?", "Aging Athlete Considerations", "A Place for Cross-Training"

### [TEU-C16-007] Koop on short-range planning, tapering, and monitoring

- On the standard 3:1 paradigm: "Many coaches and athletes arrange their short-range plans in tidy four-week periods of three weeks 'on' and one week 'off.' ... while this offers a convenient way to organize a typical four-week month, I've always found this approach to be lazy and uneducated."
- On training fatigue: "You must accept the fact that during the training process, you will get worse before you get better. If you do the right things in training, this is intentional."
- On wearables: "While these new devices can add value to the training process, it's important to keep context in mind and not let the data tail wag the performance dog. Performance, or lack thereof, will still be your best indicator of when to apply rest."
- On tapering: "Tapering is not 'maintenance,' which is a word that is carelessly thrown around... No athlete has ever come to me in my coaching career with 'maintenance' as a goal. It should not be yours during the tapering process. Tapering is not maintenance. Tapering improves your physiology, leaving you more fit and ready to perform."
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:quotes sport:running
src: Ch 12

### [TEU-C16-008] Notable statements Ch 13

- "What's in your bottle or hydration pack should serve the purpose of hydration, and the food in your pocket should serve the purpose of fueling."
- "There's no switch."
- "Postworkout nutrition might be the most oversold concept in all of sports nutrition."
- "One of the greatest truths about sports nutrition is that even the best foods are useless if they stay in your pocket."
- "Am I telling you to go out and run fifty-seven kilometers without food? Hell no. Please don't. That's stupid."
tags: type:philosophy level:all subtopic:quotes
src: Ch 13

### [TEU-C16-009] Notable quotes: nutrition, fat adaptation, and fueling in ultrarunning

- "One of my colleagues at CTS boiled the ultramarathoner's nutrition strategy down to this: develop the ability to simultaneously run and eat as much as you can without barfing." (Ch 14, "Ultra-Specific Challenges of Ultradistance Events")
- "We need less obsession over calorie composition and more over calorie quality and adequate quantity." (Ch 14, "I Don't Think It's a Good Health Message")
- "If a simple 'not-so-strategic' banana—with all its vitamins and minerals—is going to destroy your nutrition strategy, there's something wrong with your nutrition strategy, not with the banana." (Ch 14, "I Don't Think It's a Good Health Message")
- "Just eat the sweet potatoes, bananas, and apples, people. They're good for you." (Ch 14, "I Don't Think It's a Good Health Message")
- "Last I checked, there are no awards for the lowest number of calories consumed in a race." (Ch 14, "Avoid These Things")
- "Sports nutrition is dynamic. There is no singular formula that will produce optimal results for all athletes in all conditions." (Ch 14, "Final Word")

## C17 Validity Notes

### [TEU-C17-001] Validity Notes

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Author's statement ("Corrine's section on menopause / VO2 max decline", entry on menopause): VO2 max drops 5–10% per decade after 30. Not changing; consistent with literature. No note.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Author's statement (entry "Fluid consumption: why not drink-all-you-can or drink-to-thirst" and "Sodium: targets, sweat variation, salt tablets"): recommends against salt tablets and replacing sodium by food and drink. Current ultra-endurance position stands (e.g., ISSN 2019 cited by the author) emphasize individualization; no conflicting change known with confidence. No note.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Author's statement (entry "Caffeine on race day"; "Nutritional supplements"): tramadol "not on WADA's Prohibited List as of 2020" and CBD/THC thresholds. WADA removed CBD from the prohibited list in 2018 (THC remains prohibited, threshold raised to 150 ng/mL in 2021); tramadol was added to the WADA Prohibited List in-competition effective 2024 (announced 2023). The author's statements date from 2020–21 and should not be relied on for current anti-doping status; check the current WADA list.

[VALIDITY NOTE — not from source] Author's statement (entry "Hydration states…", "Cautions: fueling and hydration"): hypohydration defined as >3% body-weight loss; hyponatremia <135 mmol/L. These match consensus statements (e.g., 2015 EAH consensus); no change.

Otherwise no validity notes.

tags: type:evidence level:all subtopic:validity
src: Synthesis
